# Warlock invariants

The authoritative record of this repository's hard invariants and the reasoning behind them, moved out of `CLAUDE.md` on 2026-08-10 so that every session loads a short index instead of this file. Nothing was cut in the move. The maintenance rule is unchanged: when an invariant changes, this file is updated the way `CLAUDE.md` used to be — `CLAUDE.md` itself stays a pointer. Read the section covering a subsystem before modifying it.

## What this is

Local AI 3D asset generator: text or image prompt → SDXL 1.0 at full CFG (reference PNG) → `trellis-server.exe` (vendored native binary, not the Python TRELLIS package) → textured GLB, in a single-process desktop app: a pygame window, one ModernGL context, imgui panels drawn through it. No HTTP, no browser.

## Hard invariants

**Three threads, and the split is the design.** The pygame frame loop reads `JobStore` directly (one connection behind an RLock, so that is safe) and must never block; the `warlock-loop` thread hosts the asyncio loop the `Worker` lives on, because `wake` is loop-affine and cancellation is a coroutine; a four-thread `TaskRunner` runs everything that blocks -- exports, Blender bakes, prune, the tokenizer. `studio/runtime.py` is the old FastAPI lifespan, port for port. Progress needs no protocol at all: the frame loop calls `worker.progress.snapshot()` directly, and `TaskRunner` grew the same thing for tasks — a dict under the *same* lock as `_pending` (an answer assembled from two locks can say "not busy, 40%"), returning `{"percent", "label"}` so a pane draws a queue job and a task the same way. It is `set_progress`, not `Done`, because `Done` arrives only at completion: enough for a two-second export, not for a 16 GB download. A report for a key that is not in flight is dropped, or a child's last line landing after collection resurrects a bar nothing will clear.

**What "must never block" costs, concretely.** Four things measured as real stalls, all fixed the same way — do less per frame, not on another thread, because GL is the one context. `attach_files` takes a caller-owned cache stamped `(status, job-dir mtime)`, which is sound because every `LISTED` name is answered by *existence* and a file appearing or going moves the directory's mtime: one stat per row instead of ten, on a page of two hundred, twice a second, unbounded after "load more". **That mtime has a resolution, though, and it is coarse enough to matter**: adding a file left the directory's mtime unchanged 155 times in 200 on this machine (`docs/measurements/2026-08-07-directory-mtime-granularity.md` — Windows writes it from the system clock, whose tick is 15.6 ms unless something has asked for better), so a write landing after a listing but inside the stamped mtime's own tick is invisible to the stamp *permanently*, every later comparison matching a stale answer — which is exactly the case the mtime half exists for, a rig written into the **source** job's directory while that job stays `done`. So `MTIME_RACE_NS` applies git's racily-clean rule: a stamp is stored only once its mtime is safely in the past, and a directory touched moments ago is answered correctly and simply not remembered. The clock is read **after** the listing and that ordering is the proof, not a detail — the hazard needs a write later than the listing yet still in the mtime's tick, which cannot exist if the listing already finished a tick after the mtime. `_sync_viewer` submits `Viewer.parse_model` (pure CPU: accessor decode plus a PNG per texture slot) and adopts the result in `_adopt_model` on the frame thread, where `GpuModel` must stay — the parse used to run inline on the frame a job transitioned to done, which is when the file is largest and coldest. `viewer.pending` is what stops the timer redispatching and what makes a result the selection has moved past droppable; `load_model` survives for the callers where the wait *is* the response (entering the pose editor, loading a Review unit), because a hand-over there flashes an empty viewport instead. **`adopt_model` and `clear` clear `pending` themselves**, or the blocking path leaves it naming a parse dispatched a moment earlier which still matches when it lands and is adopted over the top — in the pose case discarding the editor and its unsaved rotations, bypassing the confirm `pose_panel.guard` puts in front of every other exit. And `viewer/sheet.StripRender` renders **one cell per `step()`**: each cell is a draw plus a synchronous `read_rgba`, and sixteen of those in one frame is a visible freeze. The marching ants are the fourth and the same lesson: `studio/ants.py` prepares a selection's loops once (closed vertex array plus cumulative arc length, cached on mask identity beside the contour trace, because both are pure functions of the mask), and the frame does whole-array numpy plus one `add_line` per straight **run** — not one per unit lattice step per dash, which on a 2048² lasso was 27 844 Python iterations *per frame, forever*. Two things about that are deliberate. The dash pattern is a function of arc length from the loop's start, so it is continuous across vertices rather than restarting at each one. And **a dash is split at every vertex it crosses**, with only exactly-collinear pieces merged back: the tempting version interpolates a dash's two endpoints and draws the chord between them — one line per dash instead of per lattice step, which is the whole speed argument — and it is wrong, because a contour is a staircase and a chord across six steps of one straightens it out and skips a one-pixel spike entirely. The merge is where the speed actually comes from and it is exact: a marquee edge is one line per dash, and a staircase, which genuinely turns every pixel, costs what it always did.

**The service layer is the only business logic.** `service/` holds what `app.py`'s route bodies used to, as plain synchronous functions taking a `WarlockService` first and raising `service.errors` exceptions. The panes call it, the tests call it, and nothing else exists between the UI and the queue. `service/jobs.py` is a **facade** since 2026-08-11: the implementations live in `service/_jobs_{create,list,lifecycle,resubmit,rework}.py` and are re-exported from it unchanged, because every caller imports the module and calls by attribute, and several tests monkeypatch names on it (`create_job`, `import_mesh`, `list_jobs`, `storage_sizes`, `MAX_LIST_LIMIT` — the last read back through the facade at call time by the two functions that consult it).

**Single sqlite3 connection, serialized by an explicit lock.** `JobStore` (`db.py`) wraps one `sqlite3` connection opened with `check_same_thread=False`, and every method that touches it takes `self._lock` (an `RLock`) first. `asyncio.to_thread` does *not* serialize anything — its default executor is a multi-worker pool, so concurrent requests genuinely land on different threads — which makes that lock the only thing keeping two `commit()`s apart. Any new method that reaches `self._conn` must take the lock too. `progress.py` is separately in-memory-only and never touches the DB from its own reader thread; keep it that way. The lock guards statements, not read-modify-write *sequences*: `params` is one JSON blob, so a caller that reads a job, edits its params and calls `set_params` performs a last-write-wins update that discards anything committed in between. Two writers on one row genuinely exist (the worker recording derived values while `service.jobs.optimize_job` rewrites a budget), so a partial params write goes through `merge_params`, which reads and writes under one hold of the lock. **The durability posture is `journal_mode=WAL` + `synchronous=NORMAL`, and that is a decision rather than a default**: a commit stops paying two fsyncs and readers stop blocking on writers, both of which the frame thread depends on, and the cost is that a *power* cut can lose the last commit — never corrupt the file, and never anything a process crash could take. It is stated here because nothing else says it was chosen. Two consequences follow. A copy of the library index is `JobStore.backup_to`, which uses sqlite's own online backup API: in WAL mode the committed database is the file *plus* whatever is still in `-wal`, so `shutil.copyfile` of the `.sqlite` alone is a snapshot missing every transaction since the last checkpoint, and copying the three files separately copies them at three different instants. And a `params` blob that will not parse is answered as `{}` rather than raised (CON-06: one corrupt blob raising out of `next_queued` starved every job behind it forever), which makes the damage invisible to every reader — `JobStore.unreadable_params` exists so that `service.library.verify` is the one place it is visible.

**Fully offline — the pipeline never touches the network, and the app process never becomes able to.** All model weights load from local paths with `local_files_only=True`; `HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1` / `HF_HUB_DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1` are set in `src/warlock/__init__.py` and must remain the first thing the package does. Never add code that downloads at runtime — a missing weight fails the one job that wanted it, with instructions for the one-time manual `hf download` (see README).

**There is exactly one exception, and its shape is the whole of it: an explicit, user-initiated fetch, in a separate process.** Clicking Download in the app-Settings pane spawns `python -m warlock.pipelines.fetch_worker`, which sets `HF_HUB_OFFLINE=0` **in its own environment only**, fetches one repository, and dies. Nothing about the generating app changes: the parent's `setdefault("HF_HUB_OFFLINE", "1")` is untouched, no module the pipeline imports ever writes the variable (`tests/test_fetch.py::test_only_the_fetch_worker_ever_clears_hf_hub_offline` scans for it), and a fetch is reachable from one button and from nothing on the job path. The subprocess is not squeamishness about a library — it is the only way the guarantee stays *checkable*: `huggingface_hub` reads that variable at **import time**, so an in-process download would mean re-setting it and re-importing a package half the app has already imported, after which "is this process offline" would be a question about import order rather than about one line. It is the `blender_worker` argument applied to a different global. Three consequences are load-bearing. The child goes in the kill-on-close job like every other subprocess (it holds a socket and writes gigabytes; an orphan would fill the disk with nothing on screen to say so). It downloads into a **staging directory beside the destination** and moves the files in only on success, because every presence probe in `fetch.py` answers "is this here" from a handful of filenames and a half-populated directory that happened to contain them would read as a finished download forever — and **the move itself is all-or-nothing for the same reason**, since one `os.replace` per file failing partway (ENOSPC, a locked file) lands in exactly the state staging exists to prevent: `_move_into` backs up anything it would overwrite, and on failure moves everything back into staging and restores the backups, every rollback step under `suppress(OSError)` so it cannot mask the error that caused it. And free disk is checked against the plan's declared size **before** the spawn — refusing the whole plan, the way sweep admission does, because half a plan downloaded is a set of directories the user did not ask for individually.

**Planning a fetch is not performing one, and `fetch.py` is the pure half.** `models.Fetch` is the structured record — repo, patterns, local dir, an optional rename, an approximate size — and **`spec.download` is derived from it** (`models.download_text`), so doctor's paste-able text, the README and what the button actually runs cannot drift; the string could never simply be executed anyway (several entries are multi-command, `pixel` carries a rename and `birefnet` carries a `uv sync` that is a `note` rather than a fetch). Two rules in there are the ones that go wrong silently. **Dedupe is on `(repo_id, destination)`, never on model key** — `dir_name` is not unique, `sdxl`/`sdxl_cfg`/`pixel`/`lightning` are four recipes over one copy of `sdxl-base-1.0` — and an *identical* record arriving again changes nothing, while a genuinely different one against the same destination merges (the IP-Adapter's weights and CLIP vision encoder are two doctor lines and one fetch); without that split, ticking all four SDXL recipes claimed 28 GB. And **paths resolve through `fetch.base_model_dir`**, which `doctor` imports rather than copying: only `turbo` honours `WARLOCK_T2I_DIR`, and `WARLOCK_T2I_ROOT` relocates a download the way it already relocates a load. **A rendered command names the directory the fetch will actually land in**, and that is the 2026-08-12 correction: the `--local-dir` literal used to be `models/...`, a *relative* path, so a command pasted from doctor or the docs into any shell whose working directory was not the checkout wrote gigabytes into `<cwd>/models` — rescued only by the one-time home migration, which skips a root that already holds something, so on a machine that had ever fetched in-app the stranding was permanent and doctor went on reporting the model missing. `Fetch.command`/`steps` therefore take the resolved destination, `fetch.download_text(config, kind, spec)` supplies it (PowerShell-quoted, via the same `destination()` the planner uses, so the text and the button cannot disagree), and `spec.download` — which `models` renders with no `Config` in reach, because `config` imports `models` — falls back to the documented default home `$HOME/.warlock/models`, spelled that way rather than `models/` or `~/` because PowerShell expands `$HOME` anywhere in an argument and `~` only at the start. `models.fetch_dests` is the one copy of the resolution rule; `pipelines/text2image.py` renders its own messages through it too, since no pipeline may import `config`. Every presence probe lives there too and doctor calls them, keeping one answer to "is this downloaded" behind doctor's several different sentences about it.

**VRAM modes (`Config.vram_exclusive`, queue.py), and the budget is now measured.** Coexist: an SDXL-class pipe (~7 GB, fully `.to("cuda")`) and `trellis-server.exe` (~16 GB) stay resident together on the 32 GB card; neither is stopped for the other, and `_maybe_evict_idle` evicts both after the idle timeout. Exclusive restores the sequential handoff for a text job: `trellis.stop()` first, then load and run SDXL, then unload SDXL (`finally`) before trellis restarts. Any change to `Worker._generate` dispatch must preserve stop-before-load / unload-before-next-start **when the flag is set**, or you get an OOM that only reproduces under load, not in tests.

**And "coexist" is a claim about VRAM, which is why an `OFFLOAD` checkpoint hands off unconditionally.** The word was written when every image model was `RESIDENT`. An offloaded spec is the opposite shape: `enable_model_cpu_offload()` keeps the whole ~16 GiB checkpoint in *host* RAM for the life of the pipe and streams one submodule to the device, so its declared `vram_gib` is honest about the card and says nothing about commit — and under WDDM trellis-server's ~16 GiB device allocation is charged against that same commit limit. Three consecutive FLUX.2 klein jobs took Python private commit from 24.4 to 45.2 GiB and system commit to 99%: the 2026-08-03 Resource-Exhaustion crash, reached from the host side rather than the VRAM side. So `queue._needs_handoff` — the one owner of the predicate, now asked from exactly one place: `Worker._acquire_t2i`, the single preamble all four t2i stages (`_generate`, `_pixel_sheet`, `_sprite_synthesis`, `_retexture`) go through, the last three passing the `_ALWAYS_CONDITIONED` sentinel because their conditioning is built per band, per candidate and per view — returns true for the flag, for conditioning beside a *running* trellis, **and for `residency == OFFLOAD` whatever the flag says**, at the accepted cost of a trellis restart per offloaded text job. The give-back is `Worker._release_t2i`, shared by those same three stages; `_generate`'s teardown is deliberately *not* it, because that stage hands straight on to the mesh stage inside the same job and so trades the VRAM against something the others do not (the comment at its own `finally` is the argument). The teardown must be `unload()` and never `trim()`: trim returns the CUDA caching allocator's pool, and none of an offloaded pipe's cost is in that pool. `vram.offloaded_base` is the accounting half — `estimate` prices such a job sequentially — because a gate that charged the sum of two stages that are never resident together would refuse the one checkpoint being careful about VRAM.

**A resource is not released until the last reference to it is gone, and the release helper may not hold one.** `Text2Image.load` builds and configures on a *local* and assigns `self._pipe` as its last statement, with `except BaseException` (a cancel mid-load is the case that most needs the weights dropped) calling `_reclaim`; publishing first meant a failed scheduler swap, device placement or LoRA attachment left `loaded == True` over a half-configured pipe that every later `load()` returned early on, resident and unreachable. **`_reclaim` deliberately takes no pipe argument**: the obvious `_release(pipe)` with a `del pipe` inside frees nothing, because a parameter binding is a live reference for exactly as long as that frame runs and the caller's own local is alive beside it — so every caller drops its reference *first* and then calls. That is measured rather than reasoned: with the argument-taking version the GPU tests' pipes stayed on the card and the next thirty queue tests were refused by dispatch-time VRAM admission. **`TrellisServer.stop()` is the same rule about a process**: `kill()` only asks, Windows reaps asynchronously, and clearing `_proc` regardless meant `running` went false while ~16 GiB was still charged to a live process — into which the handoff then loaded an image model. It `wait()`s after the kill and raises `TrellisStopFailed` if it cannot confirm death, **keeping the handle** (it is the only reference to that process, and `_reap_if_dead` still needs it). The three handoff sites and `ensure_config` propagate; eviction, shutdown, cancel and `ensure_started`'s own cleanup suppress and log, the last for `fetch._move_into`'s reason. And the commit ceiling is re-checked at the **stage boundary** (`_require_commit_headroom`, after the cancel check and before `ensure_config`, the first thing on that path that can lead to a spawn), because the image stage is precisely what moves the number and `_log_mem`'s `log.critical` recorded the wall and then walked into it. Its message says the image is finished and saved and only the 3D stage was withheld — a user told "out of memory" after a successful two-minute generation will otherwise assume they lost it.

Which mode is *chosen* is no longer a guess. `Config.vram_exclusive` is tri-state — `None` means `WARLOCK_VRAM_EXCLUSIVE` is unset — and `studio/runtime.py:_resolve_vram` calls `vram.plan()` after `doctor.run_checks` and **before `_make_worker`**, writing a plain bool back onto the mutable Config; queue.py goes on reading a bool once at startup and knows nothing about it. An explicitly set env var is honoured verbatim and auto-detection never overrules it; unset, a card whose budget (device total minus `vram.HEADROOM_GIB`) cannot hold `TRELLIS_GIB + SDXL_GIB` gets exclusive. A 32 GB card resolves to coexist, i.e. today's behaviour is unchanged, and `tests/test_vram.py` pins exactly that. `bench/runner.run` resolves the same way before the manifest is built, because `vram_exclusive` is a recorded field and "auto" is not comparable across runs.

`vram.py` is the measurement everything above was missing, and it is pure in the way `memlog.py` is: no imports from `service`/`queue`/`studio`, `None` rather than an exception when a reading is unavailable, and `WARLOCK_VRAM_TOTAL` standing in for a card so the whole gate tests headless. `device_memory()` reaches torch through `sys.modules` (as `queue.vram_gib` does, and for the same reason) but wraps `cudaMemGetInfo`, so unlike `vram_gib` it *does* see trellis-server's allocation in the other process. The cost table is prose that became numbers — every constant in it was already asserted in a comment in queue.py or text2image.py. **`Plan.explicit` is carried, not re-derived**, and `Config.vram_exclusive_explicit` is what carries it forward: the resolve writes a plain bool over the tri-state, which destroys the evidence, so every later `plan()` call — every health poll, the doctor row — saw a set value and reported an auto-selected mode as "set explicitly by `WARLOCK_VRAM_EXCLUSIVE`". Every caller that resolves a config the startup pass has already touched passes `explicit=`.

**Admission control is at the door, and refusing is the point.** `service.validation.check_vram` runs in `create_job` (after the seed checks, before `_decode` and any file write, so a refused request leaves no `input.png`) and in `promote_to_model`, and raises `Invalid` with a message naming the number *and a remedy the user can act on* — the conditioning to turn off, the resolution to drop, `WARLOCK_VRAM_EXCLUSIVE=1`. `Worker._check_resources` re-checks at dispatch against free VRAM (secondary: on WDDM the driver's free figure is virtualized) and against `memlog`'s commit fraction, refusing past `COMMIT_CEILING`. That last one is not a nicety: overcommitting VRAM on Windows does not raise, it spills into shared system memory and becomes host commit, which is the 2026-08-03 Resource-Exhaustion crash. A job that fails is the good outcome. `check_weights` joins it there and answers the other half of "can this run": a text job whose selected checkpoint, style LoRA, adapter or ControlNet is not on the host is refused with the exact `hf download` line doctor already knows, rather than queueing, waiting its turn, loading and dying with a diffusers traceback naming a directory. Only text jobs, because only they touch SDXL; and deliberately *not* the two fatal rows, because a host missing those has a red banner at startup and re-refusing every submit says the same thing in a worse place. **The style LoRA is the case that justifies the whole check**: `_load_loras` *skips* a missing style adapter where it *raises* on a missing base LoRA, so without this the job finished, looked wrong, and carried a `style_lora` param claiming a style that never ran -- and `style_lora` is in `VECTOR_PARAMS`, so that row then joined the findings corpus as evidence about it. **Every door onto a run takes both halves, and finding the ones that took only VRAM is a recurring bug.** `create_job`, `rerun_job` and `start_pixel_sheet` all admit on weights as well as on the plan (the last two were checking VRAM alone until 2026-08-11); `start_pixel_sheet` spells it out itself — `check_base_model_weights` plus an explicit `fetch.present` on the pixel LoRA — rather than calling `check_weights`. `promote_to_model` is the deliberate exception and calls `check_vram` alone: a promotion reconstructs an `input.png` that already exists, so it loads no image model and there are no per-job weights for it to admit on. Do not read the exception as a gap, and do not "fix" it by adding a text-kind weights check to a job that runs no text stage. A reroll matters as much as a first submit and for a reason a first submit does not have: the source row was admitted against whatever was on disk when it was *submitted*, which can be days and a pruned models directory ago. A pixel sheet's door had the subtler gap — it checked that the base and `models.PIXEL_SHEET_LORA` *fit* each other and never that either was present, and the worker's own tolerance then logged, restyled bare, and wrote a sidecar naming a LoRA that never loaded. The base-model half of the refusal is one function (`validation.check_base_model_weights`) called from all three of `check_weights`, `sprites._check_weights` and the sheet door, because it is one sentence and one `field="base_model"`, and three copies of it is how one of them comes to be relaxed alone. **The lease is what covers the *thread*, and it is the primitive all of this rested on without having.** `leases.MODELS` (`warlock/leases.py`) is a readers-writer lease held by threads, not coroutines: every model operation (`Text2Image.load`/`generate`/`unload`, re-entrantly) takes the shared *use* lease, and every mutation of the store — `service.downloads.download`, `uninstall`, and shutdown before it tears a pipe down — takes the exclusive *maintain* lease. The reason it cannot be a check on the worker loop: the model operation runs inside `asyncio.to_thread`, so while `from_pretrained` reads a checkpoint the event loop is **idle** and will happily run an unload, or a service callable that is about to delete the files being read. Scheduling something on the loop proves nothing about the thread. It is writer-preferring (a store that can never be maintained because jobs keep arriving is the same bug from the other side), it times out rather than hanging (the service turns that into `Conflict`), and it is process-local on purpose — a second Warlock on one home is an OS-lock problem the lease would never have seen. Alongside it: `uninstall` asks `store.active_jobs()` (unbounded) rather than filtering a 5,000-row page, and re-verifies `worker.current_job_id is None` *inside* the loop-side callable that unloads, because the first check is a snapshot and the gap after it is claimable. **Host commit is admitted in bytes, not percent.** The 90% ceiling is the standing "is this machine in trouble" reading; it is not a quantity, and a host at 85% can have well under the ~16 GiB an offloaded FLUX.2 klein needs. So `_require_commit_headroom` also takes a `need_gib`, and `_acquire_t2i` passes `BaseModel.host_peak_gib` (declared per spec, falling back to `vram_gib`) immediately before the load — the last moment the answer is still about the allocation that is about to happen. On a base switch or a store-generation change the stale pipe is unloaded *first* (`Worker._evict_stale_t2i`, refs dropped via `_evict_t2i(forget=True)` per the release rule), because the check would otherwise charge commit the unload is about to return — a live klein load was refused at 13.2 GiB free with ~12 GiB of the shortfall being the resident dreamshaper-xl pipe the switch was guaranteed to unload one call later. The check did not move and it **re-measures** after the unload rather than crediting it arithmetically — arenas can retain part of what was freed (the loadprobe measurement: 1053 of 1475 MB retained) — and the paired `_log_mem` lines around the unload are the record. **The dispatch-time VRAM credit is derived from the estimate, never from a kind list**: `vram.estimate_parts` returns the checkpoint term it charged and `queue._check_resources` credits a resident pipe exactly that, so a kind cannot be priced in one half and forgotten in the other.

**A refusal names the control it came from, and library text never reaches the user unframed.** `service.errors.Invalid` has carried a `field` since it was written and the UI highlights what it names -- but the nine `raise Invalid(str(exc))` passthroughs could not supply one, so the app's most common refusals arrived pointing at nothing. `guidance.GuidanceError` is a **`ValueError` subclass** carrying `field`, which is what let every one of them gain an address without touching a single `except ValueError`; `errors.invalid_from` wraps a library exception in a sentence naming what was being done and carries the field through. Recovering the field by parsing the message was the alternative and is the `judge.STAGES`/`verdicts.STAGES` hazard: two spellings of one fact, drifting the first time a message is reworded. `validation.not_done_message` and `files.unready_reason` are the same rule applied to two sentences that were true of five situations each -- "reference is queued" and "file not ready" -- and `unready_reason` is deliberately a *second* function rather than folded into `ready`, which stays the gate and the authority: it runs only on the refusal path, so a sentence that disagreed with it is a slightly wrong explanation rather than a wrong permission, and folding it in would put string formatting inside the per-row listing loop `attach_files` exists to keep cheap.

**A doctor probe that cannot give its memory back runs in a child.** `pipelines/loadprobe.py` answers "does this checkpoint actually load" for the host matting and pose models -- the question the rows used to decline with "not checked" -- and it is a subprocess for a measured reason, not a stylistic one: loading BiRefNet on the CPU costs **1475 MB** of RSS, and after every reference is dropped and `gc.collect()` has run, **1053 MB is still resident**, because what holds it is the allocator's arenas rather than a live object (`docs/measurements/2026-08-08-load-probe-memory.md`). An in-process check is therefore a gigabyte spent on every launch, for the life of the process, on a user who may never do a 2D export -- against an app whose worst crash is host-commit exhaustion. It also dragged torch into the app process by a different door than the one C29 closed. This is `_probe_blender`'s shape and the third instance of one rule: `bpy` is process-global, `HF_HUB_OFFLINE` is import-time, and RSS is unreturnable, so all three are paid in a process that ends. `winjob.run`, like every other spawn.

**The fourth instance is the matte itself, and it is persistent rather than one-shot.** The same measurement condemns the *inference* path for the same reason: `matting.unload()` cleared a dict and returned about a third of what the load cost, so a session that did one 2D export held a gigabyte until it closed. `pipelines/matting_worker.py` is the child and `unload()` now kills it, which is what finally makes the name true — reached from `queue.Worker._maybe_evict_caches` past the idle timeout, through `asyncio.to_thread` because it is a process kill, alongside `pose2d.unload` (kept in process: small, CPU-only, and moving it out is not justified without its own measurement) and the new `bench.metrics.unload`, which is the one of the three that can hold **VRAM** — its cache key carries the device and the benchmark path resolves `None` to cuda. That eviction is latched on `_caches_evicted` and stamped off the last finished job, because none of the three records a `last_used` and an unload of an empty cache is a no-op worth no thread at all. It is **persistent** where `loadprobe` is one-shot because `derive.get_file` mattes one artifact per call and an asset's icon, sprite and pixel sets are three calls — a one-shot child would pay the load three times. Four rules under it. The boundary is inside **`_model_mask`**, not at `mask()`, so the three-source preference order, the never-fatal fallback and every caller are untouched and a spawn failure, a timeout or a dead child is an exception `mask` already answers with the corner flood fill. Pixels travel as **files** (`blender_worker`'s rule, and here also because a 4096-square matte is 16 MB against a 64 KB pipe buffer); only a marked one-line JSON status crosses the pipe, with the child pointing `sys.stdout` at stderr so a print from `transformers` or BiRefNet's own modelling code lands in the log stream rather than mid-response. The **failure memo stays in the parent** — `_FAILED`/`_last_error` must outlive the child's death, or a broken install costs a spawn and a load per image in an export loop — and only a `stage == "load"` answer memoizes, because one image that failed to matte says nothing about the next. And `matting._infer` is the arithmetic half, kept in `matting.py` beside the constants and the docstring arguing for them and *imported* by the worker, exactly as `blender_worker` imports `rigging.fit_template`.

**`hole_worst` is corpus-dependent, and nothing in the UI may imply it is a quality scale.** It was *inverted* on the 2026-08-07 corpus -- AUC(`hole_worst` -> reject) 0.115, not weakly informative but **backwards**, with 48 of 81 rejected meshes measuring exactly 0.0 and none of the 3 accepted ones -- because the dominant failure mode there was a solid slab and a slab has no visible openings. **On the 2026-08-09 re-baseline it reads the right way round: AUC 0.756, one mesh of 41 at exactly zero** (`docs/measurements/2026-08-09-rebaseline.md`). Nothing about `meshaudit` changed; `bg_removal` did. The slab was an artefact of the `auto` matte, and under birefnet it is gone. **That is the invariant, and it is stronger than the inversion was**: a scalar whose AUC moves 0.64 because an unrelated knob moves is measuring whatever the corpus's dominant artefact happens to be, so a *low* reading is never evidence of quality on its own and a figure quoted without its corpus means nothing. So `widgets.quality_badge` still has **no green branch** below `AUDIT_UNINFORMATIVE` -- the re-baseline's own rule defers that flip to its own measurement and explicitly does not take it, n=41 on one subject and one prompt being no grounds for telling a user a low hole count means a good mesh -- the inspector still says "a solid, featureless mesh scores this too" under a low figure, and the band-sweep table still does not state "lower is better" as a general rule (it is still the right ranking *there*, where every row is one subject at one seed). The escalation is kept, because a high reading is real evidence on any corpus. This is the same doctrine `judge.py` is written under and the reason the probe is trained on pixels rather than on the audit scalars -- an argument the re-measurement strengthens rather than retires, since a probe fitted to those scalars would have learned the slab in August and something else a fortnight later.

**`config.effective` is the one answer to "what is this process running on".** `warlock doctor` prints it and the diagnostics popup draws it, and its value is the `from_env` column rather than the values: an install whose behaviour disagrees with the manual almost always disagrees because something in its environment says so. It reads `os.environ` rather than diffing against a freshly constructed `Config`, because the diff reports "default" for a variable explicitly set to the default value -- which is exactly the case where somebody is asking whether their setting took. `config.SETTINGS` is a written-out table (every default is a `default_factory` lambda closing over its own variable name, so there is nothing to derive it from) asserted against the dataclass in both directions, with `vram_exclusive_explicit` the one stated omission because it is derived by the startup resolve rather than configured.

**Chapters 01-19 are reserved for the tutorial series.** On 2026-08-22 the twenty-five existing chapters moved up by nineteen and the block below them was set aside, because every renumbering this manual has had was forced by an insertion and a tutorial series grows a chapter at a time -- so an exactly-sized insert would have paid the cost again on every wave. The first wave uses 01-13; the next is a new file and an `EXPECTED_KEYS` line. The gap is legal because `chapters()` globs and sorts, and `test_index_sections_match_the_loaders_parts` was tightened to build its expectation from the chapters that *exist* rather than from `[n for n in rng if n <= last]` -- read the other way, the old form asserted that a chapter exists for every number below the highest, which is a claim about the manual's size it was never meant to make. The tutorials go first because they are written for the reader who has just installed the app; appended, they would have sat behind Architecture, which is written for the reader changing its code.

**A manual chapter's number decides both its order and its part, so adding one is a renumbering.** `loader.PARTS` maps number ranges onto part headings and `chapters()` sorts by filename, so a chapter appended at the end wears the wrong part or grows a second heading with the same name. Renumbering is the supported move and it is mechanical rather than risky, because `tests/manual/test_docs.py` asserts the chapter list, every cross-link, every anchor, the index's links and `HELP_TARGETS` -- in both directions. `tests/manual/test_coverage.py` adds the direction those could not see: a pane with neither a (?) nor a stated exemption fails, which is what "the manual documents this and nothing reaches it" looked like before. On 2026-08-19 the 3D viewport became chapter 05 under exactly this rule -- the ~5k-LOC subsystem in the middle of the window had no chapter and no (?), and its toolbar was exempted from the coverage gate for having no titled section to hang one beside, so `render.help_button_inline` is the same button placed where the cursor is (a wrapping toolbar cannot take a right-aligned one, and `test_help_button_call_sites_match_help_targets` scans for both spellings). **Images are in the subset now, as a block and only on a line of their own** (`parser.Image`, added 2026-08-19; inline HTML stays forbidden). Alt text is *required*, because the chapters ship and the screenshots are generated, so "not captured yet" is an ordinary state that has to degrade to a caption rather than to damage -- and `tests/manual/test_docs.py` gates that every referenced image resolves, mirroring the link test. The renderer asks `ThumbnailCache` at `render.IMAGE_MAX_SIDE` rather than its 256 px thumbnail cap, and `max_side` is **in the cache key** for the reason `nearest` is: a cap that was not would hand back whichever size happened to be decoded first. **Chapter 16's bindings and the Ctrl+/ sheet are gated against each other** (`tests/manual/test_shortcuts.py`, and `main.shortcut_sections` is module-level and imgui-free so it can be): one direction only, because the chapter is documented as the full list and the popup as a subset. Nothing compared them before, which is how the chapter came to open by telling the reader to press a button deleted three waves earlier while the popup said "F1 -- Switch to the Manual" and reasoned about thirteen modes against a tree with ten. **The TOC's sub-chapter half is pure and lives in `loader`, not `render`** — `sections()`, `active_anchor()` and `matching_sections()` take parsed blocks and return data, so every rule about what the tree offers is assertable without a frame (`tests/manual/test_sections.py`), and `block_text()` is shared by the chapter search and the section search rather than copied, because two copies is one edit away from a search that finds a chapter and then no section inside it. The scroll-spy compares content-space `get_cursor_pos_y` against `get_scroll_y` — imgui adds the scroll back into the former, so a heading's recorded offset is stable across frames — and it is skipped on any frame that is navigating, or reading the scroll would answer with where the reader *was* and unlight the row they just clicked.

**The image pipeline never runs in the app process.** `pipelines/text2image_worker.py` holds the checkpoint and `pipelines/t2i_client.Text2ImageClient` is the app-side handle, presenting the surface `Text2Image` presented so every call site is unchanged. The reason is measured and is about *host* memory, not VRAM: `unload()` returned the VRAM exactly as it claimed and the host figure did not move, because what holds it is the allocator's arenas rather than a live object. End to end on 2026-08-22, `flux_klein_distilled` charged **24.26 GiB** of private commit for a load plus one 1024x1024 sample and gave **0.26 GiB** back; through the child it charged 24.08 and gave back **all of it**, with system commit returning to its baseline. The session that prompted this ended with the app refusing its own sprite-sheet follow-up at 94% commit, which was the correct behaviour and the wrong outcome. This is the **fourth** instance of the rule above: `bpy` is process-global, `HF_HUB_OFFLINE` is import-time, RSS is unreturnable -- and a checkpoint's arenas are unreturnable too, so all four are paid in a process that ends. The child is *persistent*, because a reroll, a sprite sheet and a tile sheet are three generates against one checkpoint; it dies on `unload()`, which is now a process kill, or with the app via the kill-on-close job. `WARLOCK_T2I_IN_PROCESS=1` restores the old arrangement for debugging and restores the leak with it. **A bare load is not the case that breaks** -- load-then-unload with no sample kept only 2.8 GiB -- so any future measurement of this has to generate. (`docs/measurements/2026-08-22-trampoline-child-pids.md`)

**And its stdin reader may never leave a read pending.** `text2image_worker._lines_from` peeks with `PeekNamedPipe` and reads only what has already arrived, sleeping 50 ms when the pipe is empty. This is not style: with a daemon thread parked in a *blocking* read on the inherited stdin pipe, the main thread's next native-extension import never returns -- `import numpy` takes 0.1 s with no such thread and hangs forever with one, the faulthandler dump showing the main thread inside the loader creating `_multiarray_umath`. Controls place the cause precisely: an idle second thread is fine and one blocking on a *regular file* is fine, while every way of blocking on the pipe fails alike (`for line in stdin`, `readline()`, `buffer.readline()`, a bare `os.read`) -- so it sits below Python's IO layer, in the interaction between an outstanding synchronous pipe read and the Windows image loader. `matting_worker` never met this because it reads on its main loop and imports before ever blocking; this worker cannot, because a **cancel has to be read while a generate is running**. Both directions are pinned, including a test that fails if the underlying interaction is ever fixed and the polling stops being necessary.

**Every subprocess goes in the kill-on-close job, without exception.** `winjob.run()` is `subprocess.run` with the assignment in the gap between spawn and wait, and it is what `doctor._probe_blender` and `pipelines/optimize` use — the two short-lived children that were spawned unassigned and could outlive a hard kill (the bpy probe runs during `Runtime._start`, so killing Warlock while it starts stranded a python.exe mid-`import bpy`). `tests/test_vram.py::test_every_subprocess_spawn_is_in_the_kill_on_close_job` scans the package and fails on any `subprocess.Popen`/`run` call site without a nearby `winjob.assign`. The guarantee is also *visible* now: `winjob.armed()` backs a doctor row, because a host where `_ensure_job` quietly failed used to be indistinguishable from one where it took.

**`source.glb` is the reconstruction; `model.glb` is derived from it.** trellis writes `source.glb` and nothing ever overwrites it — `model.glb` is produced by optimize-then-normalize, which is why `service.jobs.optimize_job` can retarget a triangle budget without another two-minute trellis run. The ordering is load-bearing in one direction only: gltfpack rewrites the node graph, so it must run *before* `normalize_glb` inserts the scale/translation node, and `optimize_job` reapplies the transform for exactly that reason. `_discard_artifacts` deletes **both** files, or a cancelled job could be re-optimized back into existence. Everything else (STL, OBJ zip, FBX, `collision.glb`, `textures.zip`) is a pure function of `model.glb`, derived on first request under the per-artifact `_convert_locks` idiom, and `optimize_job` deletes all of them because they describe the old mesh.

**Nothing is ever written directly onto a name something else may already be reading.** Every write that lands on a *served* file goes through a staging name beside it and an `os.replace`, with the unlink in a `finally` — `service/derive.py::_staged` is the canonical spelling and the one the module's own derivations call rather than re-implementing. The rule is not only about concurrency, and the sharper half is freshness: for several artifacts **existence *is* the freshness test**, so a writer that dies part way through does not merely expose a torn read for a moment, it leaves a truncated file that is served under that name for the life of the job directory, with no way for a retry to get past it. That is what a Blender killed by `pose_timeout` or by kill-on-close leaves behind, and it is why `model.fbx` (`service/derive.py`) and a baked pose GLB (`service/rig.py`) are staged rather than exported in place. The same discipline covers the rig QA sheet (`queue._deform_qa` — where a *re*-rig packs a new atlas while the previous run's `rig_qa.json` already satisfies `files.ready`, so the marker promises a whole sheet over a half-written one), `thumb.png`, and `input.png` on the reference-reroll loop, which rewrites a served name once per rejected attempt. A staging file is a dotfile and **nothing ever sweeps one**, so the `finally` is as load-bearing as the rename: a failed encode that strands its temp leaves it there until the job is pruned. **And the "somebody else already wrote it" escape belongs only to files that are pure functions of something else.** `postprocess._replace_or_accept` accepts whatever is at the destination after three failed renames, on the argument that the artifact is derived and whoever landed first produced our bytes — true for the STL, the OBJ zip and the texture zips, and false for `normalize_glb`, which stages onto **`glb_path` itself**. There the destination is always present and is always the *un-normalised original*, so the escape was unconditionally reachable and taking it returned a scale and a translation for a grounding that had not happened: the row went `done` claiming a grounded asset and every derived export was a pure function of the wrong file, with one `log.debug` line to show for it. `_staged(..., derived=False)` is that path, and it raises. Grounding always runs is an invariant, so its failure has to be able to fail.

**Grounding is not conditional on a size.** `normalize_glb` scales (only when `size_m` was asked for), centres X/Z and puts min-Y at zero on *every* job — a pivot at the reconstruction volume's centre is a manual fixup on every Godot/Unity import regardless. Because it now runs on meshes it never used to touch, a failure is logged and swallowed rather than failing a job whose GLB is already on disk — the same rule `_audit_mesh` and the report follow. `scale_glb` survives only as a thin wrapper.

**And grounding composes `T · S · M_root`, never `M_root · T · S`.** The bounds are measured in *world* space, so the transform has to apply outside each root's own — which is why `_insert_transform_below` **empties the root** and folds its TRS into the inserted child (`_composed`; TRS decomposes exactly because the scale is uniform, and a `matrix` root composes as a matrix). Sandwiching the grounding under an untouched root was wrong twice: a rotated root rotated the grounding offset, and applying the same world-space translation once *per root* while each root's offset stayed unscaled above it meant a Clay export — which emits **one root per object** — grounded every object as though it alone were the scene, so three boxes asked for 2 m came out 8.2 m and reported 2. trellis output hid both, because trimesh writes one identity root. The transform still may not sit *on* a root (trimesh treats one as the base frame and discards it, which would leave the GLB grounded and the derived STL/OBJ not), and a wrapper node above the roots does not work either: trimesh's base frame is named `world` and so is the root its own exporter writes, so the collision resolves into a cycle that drops the transform again. `tests/test_postprocess.py` pins the whole matrix — identity / rotated / translated / non-uniformly scaled / matrix / multi-root, with and without `size_m`.

**The composed SDXL prompt is chunk-encoded, not truncated.** CLIP's text encoders cap out at 77 tokens, and a long user brief plus the LoRA trigger and template scaffolding can exceed that. `pipelines/prompt.chunk()` splits it into multiple <=77-token chunks on comma boundaries (never mid-phrase), each encoded separately and concatenated on the sequence axis in `pipelines/text2image._encode_long_prompt` -- the UNet's cross-attention has no sequence-length limit, only the text encoders do. A single-chunk prompt (the common case) is bit-identical to the old direct-string path. Guidance fragments in `guidance.py` stay 2-4 words each regardless: chunking removes the hard ceiling, not the soft one where a longer sequence dilutes cross-attention.

**A native kernel is an optimisation with a reference, never a replacement.** `native/*.c` builds to `vendor/warlockc/warlockc.dll` (gitignored, like every other vendored binary) and `native.py` is the ctypes loader — pure in the way `vram.py`/`memlog.py` are: stdlib only, no imports from `service`/`queue`/`studio`, `None` rather than an exception when the library is missing or unusable. Every call site reads `if native.available(): ... else: <the numpy code>`, and **the numpy code is never deleted** — it is both the fallback on a machine with no compiler and the reference the parity tests measure the kernel against, which is why `meshaudit._fill` survives as a whole function nothing calls on this machine. The bar for a float kernel is a **bit-identical** result, not a close one, which is what `/fp:precise` (MSVC) and `-ffp-contract=off` (clang) in `build.ps1` are for: an FMA rounds once where numpy rounds twice, and that moves a triangle edge by a pixel. **clang-cl needs both spellings**, and that is the trap: its `/fp:precise` implies `-ffp-contract=on` on clang ≥14, so the flag that makes MSVC exact makes clang-cl permit exactly the FMA the bar forbids — the branch passes `/clang:-ffp-contract=off` alongside it. Nothing else would catch that: the ABI handshake compares a version, not arithmetic, so the only symptom is a parity test failing on one compiler. `WARLOCKC_ABI` in `warlockc.h` must match `native.ABI`, because `vendor/` being gitignored means a checkout routinely holds a DLL built from older sources — an absent DLL is obvious, a stale one silently computing last week's answer is not. Tests that are *about* the numpy path pin it with a fixture rather than trusting this machine not to have a DLL, and the suite is expected to pass both ways (`WARLOCK_NATIVE=0`). Two things the inker compositor's kernels (`native/composite.c`, behind `composite.over`/`paint_colour`/`stack_region`) added to that rule and neither is optional. **A whole-region decision stays in Python**: `over`'s early-out is `every source alpha is 1`, a reduction rather than a per-pixel test, and an opaque pixel *inside* a non-opaque source goes through the full formula — so a per-pixel shortcut in C would diverge in the last ulp, and the fused `stack_region` kernel is instead *told* which layers took it, as `WARLOCKC_BLEND_REPLACE`. And **`composite._MODE_IDS` is written out, not `enumerate(BLEND_MODES)`**: an automatic index would hand the kernel a number no `case` matches and get `normal` composited back for it, silently, whereas a name the map does not carry simply falls back to numpy. The same reasoning is why a kernel that fuses `u8/255.0` into its loads builds a 256-entry table of that exact expression rather than multiplying by a reciprocal. **The map must stay complete, and the reason is a measured cliff rather than a preference**: `_stack_native` is all-or-nothing, so a mode the kernel does not know does not cost *that layer* the fast path, it costs *the document* — one `saturation` layer took a 256² dab invalidate from 5.4 ms to 48.7 and a full-canvas rebuild from 0.26 s to 2.8 (`docs/measurements/2026-08-16-blend-modes-and-dither.md`). That is what the seven appended modes did until 2026-08-16, on the reading that the four non-separable ones "could not be a per-channel C case" — true, and the wrong conclusion, because they are per-*pixel* independent and the kernel was always free to take the whole pixel. `WARLOCKC_BLEND_HUE` is therefore the bottom of the non-separable range and `is_nonseparable` is a `>=` against it: a separable mode added *above* `LUMINOSITY` would be dispatched into `blend_nonseparable` and read a formula meant for something else. Two transcription facts that batch established and that any future non-separable work depends on: numpy's three-element `.sum(axis=-1)` accumulates left to right **from a zero seed** (the pairwise sum's `n < 8` branch), and `np.argsort` over three elements is **stable** (introsort falls to insertion sort), which is the only thing that makes `_set_sat`'s two-equal-channel case well-defined — both were checked against this build's numpy over two million triples before the C was written, not assumed. **`native/dither.c` is the one kernel whose reference is a Python loop rather than a numpy expression**, and that is a licence rather than an oversight: `dither._floyd_steinberg` is sequential by definition, so there is no vectorisation of it that is still Floyd–Steinberg — which is exactly why it cost ~10 µs/px (43 s for one 2048² conversion) and why C bought 76×. Being scalar and sequential, it also has none of the bit-parity tension a float *reduction* has, so its bar is byte identity like everything else here; the one thing that needed care is that the four error-diffusion writes land in the reference's order, because float addition is not associative and several of them are written again by the next pixel along. **`native/contours.c` is the one kernel whose bar is not identity to the reference's output, and the licence for that is in the reference**: `SelectionMask.contours()` leaves loop order, starting vertex and winding unspecified (`_chain` starts from `next(iter(set))`), and at a checkerboard corner — where two regions touch diagonally and four boundary edges meet — the tracer's left-turn rule and the reference's dict order partition the *same edges* into different loops. So `tests/inker/test_contours_native.py` asserts the **set of unit edges**, plus closure and unit steps and the reference's `len(loop) > 2` floor, and asserts the loop *count* only on masks with no diagonal corner in them. A loosened bar is otherwise how a kernel quietly stops being checked, which is why this one is written down beside what makes it sound. Two consequences of the walk that look like bugs and are not: a loop legitimately passes through the same vertex **twice** at a pinch (one region touching itself diagonally), so the walk ends when it arrives back at its start rather than after a vertex count; and the kernel allocates nothing, so the caller sizes the point buffer from `_boundary_edges` — an *exact* count of the boundary edges, not an estimate, which is why a `-1` means fall back to numpy rather than retry bigger. **`native/bvh.c` is the second such kernel and, on the evidence of how long it took to get a third, should be the last**: `viewer/picking.build_bvh` splits on `np.argpartition`, which is introselect — its permutation among *equal* keys is unspecified, so a C median split separates coincident centroids differently and builds a different, equally valid tree. Asserting node-for-node equality would be asserting a property the numpy path does not have either. The bar is therefore the **pick result** — the triangle a ray returns, whose tie-break (lowest triangle index) Part J already pinned precisely so that the tree and the full linear sweep agree — plus the structural invariants any correct tree has: every triangle in exactly one leaf, an interior node holding none of its own, and a child's box contained in its parent's (`tests/test_bvh_native.py`). The boxes themselves *are* bit-identical, because min and max over doubles are exact whatever order the triangles inside a node ended up in, and the one legitimately oversized leaf — a span whose centroids all coincide, where no split separates anything — is asserted rather than tolerated. The node budget is `2 * (T / 4 + 2) + 8`, from the fact that a median split of nine or more leaves at least four a side; the kernel returns `-1` rather than overrunning it, and that is a fall-back, not a retry, exactly as `contours` is. **ABI 10's other two kernels answer only part of their reference and say so at the seam.** `warlockc_blit_cells_u8` implements `render._over`'s masked-copy branch alone — binary source alpha, full opacity, normal mode — and `_blit_cells_native` declines for everything else, including the mixed tile sizes a collection produces; the "is this alpha binary" test is one vectorised pass per *distinct oriented tile* rather than per cell, which is the only thing that makes asking affordable, and the both-clear quirk (`_over` writes rgb 0 where source and destination are both transparent, discarding colour stored under a zero alpha) is reproduced rather than tidied up. It is fed the cells in **draw order**, not grouped by tile: grouping would be faster still and would paint an oversized tile's overlap the wrong way round. `warlockc_palette_nearest_f64` **keeps the sqrt**, which looks like an oversight and is the contract — sqrt is monotonic but it also rounds, so two distinct squared distances can land on the same double, and an argmin over the rounded norms then takes the earlier index where an argmin over the exact squares takes the strictly smaller one, which may sit later. Same ordering, different pick, and `tests/test_palette_nearest_f64.py` constructs the pair (`1` against `1 + 2⁻⁵²`) rather than hoping to sample one. **Before writing a kernel, measure — and the harness is `scripts/bench_native.py` rather than a script that is then thrown away**, which is what let batch 3's ranked follow-up queue rot until three of its five entries had been silently fixed or invalidated by unrelated work. Its two triage rules are earned: sweep the work parameter, because a cost curve flat in it is dispatch-bound and a kernel wins enormously where a rising one buys only the constant factor; and a kernel that leaves the fallback unchanged need only be faster, while one that requires rewriting the reference must beat the *original* reference — which is what killed `warlockc_blur_f32` in batch 2. Numpy fixes come first regardless: they help a checkout with no DLL, which is every checkout until the installer ships, and batch 5's largest single win was one of them (`indexed.histogram`, 11.4 s to 117 ms). Where such a fix replaces its own reference there is no seam to toggle, so its parity assertion is against the reference written out in the test and its budget likewise (`tests/test_perf_native_batch5.py`). Two of batch 5's candidates were **rejected on the numbers and the rejections are the point**: `np.add.at` stays on the 1-D counters in `clay/ops_topo`, where bincount measured *slower* (2.8 ms against 4.2 at 200k, because adding a scalar has a buffered fast path that adding a row does not), and `viewer/scene._flat_normals` stays on it too, because its accumulator is f4 and bincount would sum in f8 and round differently. (`docs/measurements/2026-08-22-native-batch-5.md`)

**Two different mesh measurements, deliberately not merged.** `meshaudit` answers "can you see through it" (a silhouette check, what a player notices); `meshreport` answers "will an importer accept it and will it sit on the floor" (topology, materials, budget, pivot). Only the second may use the word *watertight* — the badge used to apply that word to the first, which it never proved. Both are stored on the job and `params["mesh_audit"]` keeps its old shape so existing readers are unchanged.

**gltfpack is vendored and present; the named tiers are unqualified, and that is now the only reason `raw` is the default.** `vendor/gltfpack/gltfpack.exe` landed on 2026-08-07, so `pipelines/optimize.py`, the config field, the non-fatal doctor check and the API are all live rather than dormant — which means the constraint changed shape rather than going away. A named tier is no longer a button that *fails*; it is a button that silently ships a worse mesh, which is harder to notice. So the bar stands unchanged: a tier stays out of the generate form until it has been run against a chest, a sword and a rock and shown to keep UVs, both PBR maps and material assignment. `Config.mesh_profile` defaults to `raw` and `panes/settings_3d.PROFILES` offers `raw` alone for that reason and no longer for the missing binary. The retarget control (`studio/panes/retarget_panel.py`, in the inspector under "Triangle budget") is the qualification path: it offers only `raw` while `vendor/gltfpack/gltfpack.exe` is absent and the whole tier list once it is there — which is today — so a tier can be exercised without being exposed to a generate form. A qualification was attempted on 2026-08-13 (`docs/measurements/2026-08-13-tier-qualification.md`) and stopped at its pre-registered corpus gate — zero of the twenty corpus meshes were accepted, so no tier was measured and the bar stands untouched; the 0/20-at-settings-that-accepted-19/41 anomaly it surfaced was adjudicated the same evening (`docs/measurements/2026-08-13-mesh-regression-check.md`: no code regression — the collapse is subject-intrinsic reconstruction failures plus the binary-to-graded instrument change), so a retry needs trellis-friendly subjects and a graded-scale bar, not a fix. `service.jobs.optimize_job` refuses a job that is still `queued` or `running` with a `Conflict`, because its half of the write takes a lock the worker's `_optimize`/`_apply_scale`/`_audit_mesh` do not. It also resolves its tier through **`_resolve_profile`, the same one `create_job` uses**, rather than calling `optimize.resolve` itself: the second copy raised a fieldless `Invalid` and skipped the gltfpack-presence refusal, so a retarget on a host whose binary was broken silently shipped the raw copy while `params["profile"]` named a tier that never ran — and `profile` is in `VECTOR_PARAMS`, so that row then joined the findings corpus as evidence about a tier nothing had run. Writes onto a served `model.glb` are always staged (`optimize.staged_copy`, `postprocess._staged`) — `optimize_job` runs on *done* jobs, so a bare `copyfile` would let a concurrent read see a truncated file — and it reports (never deletes) the rig artifacts a retarget makes stale, which the retarget panel warns about before the button rather than after.

**Blender never runs in the app process.** `bpy` is process-global, not thread-safe, and hard-crashes (rather than raises) on some non-manifold geometry — which trellis output frequently is. So it runs out-of-process as `python -m warlock.pipelines.blender_worker`, mirroring the `trellis-server.exe` subprocess pattern. `rigging.py` is the host side and must stay importable with no bpy anywhere; `pipelines/blender_worker.py` is the only module allowed to `import bpy`, and it imports `rigging.fit_template` so host and worker can never disagree about where a joint goes. The single-sqlite-connection / single-event-loop invariant is untouched by this: `rigging.run_worker` is blocking and every caller dispatches it through `asyncio.to_thread`.

**A rig writes into the *source* job's directory, via temp names.** `rig.glb`/`rig.json` land next to the `model.glb` they were fitted to, not in the rig job's own dir — the rig belongs to the mesh. The worker never writes the served names: `rig_spec` points it at `.rig.tmp.glb`/`.rig.tmp.json` (the `.glb` suffix is load-bearing — Blender's exporter appends one to any filepath without it) and `rigging.finalize_rig` renames them into place on success, GLB first so `rig.json` — the completion gate `_attach_files` and the file route key on — never advertises a mismatched GLB. That gate alone is not enough on a *re-rig*: the old `rig.json` already satisfies it while Blender is writing, which is exactly why the temps exist. Consequences that are easy to get wrong: `Worker._discard_artifacts` must resolve `params["source_job"]` when cleaning up a cancelled rig (deleting `model.glb` there would destroy a different, successful job's mesh) and deletes **only the temps** — a cancelled re-rig must never destroy the previous successful rig's artifacts — and `queue._rig` skips `finalize_rig` when the cancel event is set, so a cancel after the solve finishes doesn't publish anyway.

**A stage that has published onto a served name commits the cancel token, and a stage whose cleanup deletes served names may not mint its own id.** `_Cancel.committed` is set the moment an artifact reaches a name the app serves: after that a cancel cannot un-publish it, so recording the row `cancelled` is a lie about the file on disk and every follow-up gated on `status == "done"` silently never fires. Four stages publish — `_rig` (`finalize_rig`), `_pixel_sheet` and `_charsheet` (the sidecar, which is what `list_sheets` reads) and `_retexture` (the `os.replace` onto another job's `model.glb`) — and two of them had no commit. `_retexture` is the worst of the four because its publish is *irreversible*: the skin the user had is gone. `_charsheet` is the worst in effect, because `_discard_artifacts`'s sheet branch deletes the **served** pair rather than temps, so a cancel one line late unlinked a character sheet Troupe was already drawing. That branch is safe only while every door mints a fresh `sheet_id`, and both workers used to paper over a missing one with `or new_id()`; they refuse now, because a fallback there is precisely how the assumption would be broken with nothing going red. Cancellation before the publish is still real and still wanted: a charsheet's packed atlas with no sidecar is listed by nothing, so discarding it is the right answer rather than a loss. `tests/test_job_durability.py` scans all four publishers and drives the charsheet case end to end.

**Every write onto a name the app serves is staged, with no exception for "nothing is reading it yet".** `_deform_qa` packs to `.rig_qa.png.tmp`, `_pixel_sheet` stages its atlas, `_charsheet` stages both its render and its quantised result. `_sheet` packed straight onto `sheets/<id>.png` and `_sprite_synthesis` saved its draft PNGs in place, both on the grounds that a fresh id means nothing holds those names yet — which is true of the first run and of no other, and is the same reasoning the sidecar-as-completion-marker rule already rejects: on a re-run the *previous* sidecar goes on calling the file ready for the whole minute the pack spends writing over it.

**A rig welds before it heats, and every rung of that chain names itself.** trellis meshes are UV-atlased, and an xatlas seam split is a non-manifold edge that is not a hole — the same root cause that had `meshreport` measuring seams and calling them holes, addressed there in the *measurement* and here in the *solve*. So `blender_worker._skin` merges coincident vertices (`weld_distance`, which is `meshreport.WELD_TOLERANCE` of the bbox diagonal — imported, not restated, because it is one judgement about one mesh), recalculates normals, and only then attempts `ARMATURE_AUTO`; the chain is weld → verify → unwelded heat → envelope, and envelope is still the floor. The weld must be invisible to the user, and the whole argument that it is rests on merge-by-distance keeping face-corner data — UVs are per-loop, so the two halves of a seam keep their own coordinates while sharing one position — which `tests/test_rig_weld.py` proves against a real Blender by comparing exported texture bytes and the per-loop UV set across the weld rather than accepting that it looked fine. Two decisions are pure so a test can reach them without bpy, exactly as `_rig_bones` is: `weld_distance`, and `_skin_steps`, which is where **a weld that merged nothing is reported as plain `automatic`** — the mesh the solve saw is the mesh it would have seen anyway, and there is no second, identical attempt to fall back to. The pre-weld datablock is kept and put back for the unwelded rung, because a welded solve failing is not evidence the unwelded one would. `automatic-welded` joins `weighting`'s vocabulary additively; the reason string names every rung that failed, since "it failed" sends the reader looking at the weld when it was the geometry.

**The deformation battery is data, rendered through the one sheet pipeline, and it scores nothing.** `src/warlock/templates/deform_qa/<template>.json` holds the poses a rig is *reviewed* in — squat, arms overhead, elbow and knee at 90°, torso twist — in the same normalized-JSON spirit as the skeleton templates and the same local-quaternion format as the shipped pose presets, because `queue._deform_qa` renders them through `sheetlib.plan`/`pack`/`sidecar` around `op_sheet` and nothing else: a second renderer would be a second set of camera conventions to keep in agreement with the first. It is a separate directory from `src/warlock/templates/poses/` because these are not poses anybody would ship an asset in, and the rows carry synthetic ids (a sheet row is keyed by `(pose id, frame)`, so a battery of id-less rows renders the first pose into every row). The output is `rig_qa.png` + `rig_qa.json` in the **source** job's directory beside `rig.glb` — deliberately not a sheet in `sheets/`, so it never joins `list_sheets`, counts against `MAX_SHEETS` or dies with a sheet delete — served only once the sidecar exists, which is the `rig.glb`/`rig.json` ordering again. A failure is logged and swallowed (`_audit_mesh`'s rule: the rig is already published), a template with no battery renders nothing, and `Config.deform_qa` is a kill-switch rather than an opt-in for the reason `pose_fit` is one. **Nothing scores it.** The point is a picture a person looks at; a metric would be a claim about deformation quality that nothing here has earned yet.

**Where a joint goes has three sources, in a fixed order, and the third is wholesale or not at all.** `rig_spec` takes `bones` (world-space joints the *user* moved, already through `validate_joints`), `template_bones` (the template's own normalized landmarks, replaced with ones measured off the reference image) and neither, and `blender_worker._rig_bones` is the single place that decides between them — its own function precisely because everything around it in `op_rig` needs bpy, so that decision is the part a test can reach. **The measured landmarks are a better *template*, never a second fitter**: they are still normalized, still the template's names and parentage, so the worker scales them with the same `fit_template` and the bbox scaling stays owned by one side. `pipelines/pose2d.refit` is that mapping (COCO-17 → the humanoid template), split from the model half the way `reference.py` is split — arithmetic on landmarks, stdlib only, so every rule about where a joint lands is assertable from synthetic numbers, while torch stays inside `_load`/`_detect` because `available()` is the question every caller asks first. **Depth is the template's for every joint**, since one view fixes x and z and says nothing about y. The premise is that the mesh *is* `input.png` reconstructed, which is also why the person box comes from `reference.measure` rather than a detector: the composition gate has already established one subject and already measured its silhouette. **Any doubt refuses the whole fit** — a landmark under `MIN_KEYPOINT_CONFIDENCE`, one the detector never produced, a figure whose knees are above its hips, a landmark outside the subject bbox past `BBOX_TOLERANCE`, a template outside `POSE_FIT_TEMPLATES` — and never per-joint: an arm measured against the subject beside a shoulder assumed from a T-pose is not partly better, it is a skeleton internally inconsistent in a way nothing downstream can detect. `Worker._wants_landmarks` gates on cases where the answer *could not be used* (wrong template, kill-switch, corrected joints already present, no `input.png`, no weights) and never on how good it might be, which is `refit`'s business; `_landmark_bones` runs in `to_thread` and log-and-swallows like `_audit_mesh`, because the artifact this could fail is one the user asked for and the improvement is one they did not. `rig.json` gains `fit` — additive with no version bump, since every reader is `.get`-based — and `adjusted` still means only "the user moved these".

**A pose is a map of glTF node-local quaternions, and that is the whole contract.** The viewer never sees a Blender bone and the worker never sees a viewer joint node; they agree because both talk about the joint node's *local* rotation. Blender composes a posed bone as `pose(b) = pose(parent) @ (rest(parent)⁻¹ @ rest(b)) @ basis(b)`, and a glTF joint's local transform is `pose(parent)⁻¹ @ pose(b)`, so `node_local == rest_node_local @ basis` exactly — `blender_worker._rest_local_rotation` is that one line, and no per-bone axis correction is involved (the Z-up→Y-up flip cancels because the exporter applies it once at the root). Two things keep the identity true and both are load-bearing: the worker imports with `bone_heuristic="BLENDER"` (the default re-aims bones at their children and silently changes `matrix_local`), and exports with `export_rest_position_armature=False` (the default writes every joint at rest and demotes the pose to an animation track). Quaternions are stored XYZW (the order `THREE.Quaternion.toArray()` used, kept because every pose on disk is in it); the worker converts to WXYZ. `studio/viewer/pose.py` imports `rigging.mirror_quaternion` rather than reimplementing it -- the browser's copy carried a comment insisting the two stay identical, which is exactly the sign convention that is wrong in a way a still image cannot show. `tests/test_rigging.py::test_a_posed_glb_carries_back_exactly_the_rotations_it_was_given` pins it by reading the exported GLB's JSON chunk directly, not through bpy.

**Poses are files, not rows.** `<job_dir>/poses/<pose_id>.json` (one per pose, so a save needs no read-modify-write and therefore no lock) with the baked `<pose_id>.glb` beside it. `rigging.pose_path` is the *only* place a caller-supplied pose id becomes a path and it raises on anything that isn't `[0-9a-f]{12}`. Overwriting a pose deletes its cached GLB — the bake depicts the old rotations. The bake itself is derived on demand in the request under the same `_convert_locks` idiom as the STL/OBJ exports, not on the job queue: it is a one-second subprocess, and putting it behind the serial GPU queue would make it wait on a trellis run.

**A pose or rig JSON is validated at the read door, not only at the write door.** The library is a directory of files any other program can edit, and until 2026-08-11 every guard sat on the way in: `read_record`/`read_pose`/`read_rig`/`read_preview_sidecar` returned whatever `json.loads` produced, so a hand-edited record missing `bones` reached the panes as a `KeyError` and a non-UTF-8 one raised out of a directory listing. All four now share one shape — stat against `rigging.MAX_RECORD_BYTES` (1 MB) *before* the read so a blob costs a log line rather than the RAM, `except (OSError, ValueError)` because `UnicodeDecodeError` and `JSONDecodeError` are both `ValueError`, and an `isinstance(record, dict)` gate because a JSON array parses fine and then fails one frame later. A file that fails any of the three costs itself and nothing else. The *semantic* half lives one layer up: `service.poses._record_or_not_found` runs `poselib.validate_record` on the way out of storage, so Rename/Duplicate/Apply/Update refuse with a sentence instead of a traceback. Two callers deliberately skip it — `delete_library_pose` (a record too corrupt to validate must still be removable) and `list_library` (a record the user cannot see is a record they cannot delete). Filenames win over contents: `list_poses`/`list_records` list a record whose internal `id` disagrees with its stem under the *stem*, because the stem is the address every read, save and delete goes through — listing the record's own id made a rename write a second file and leave a phantom duplicate behind.

**The glTF loader has ceilings, because import is deliberately structural-only.** `check_glb` at the door checks the container and a size limit, so any file under it reaches `viewer/gltf.py` with whatever its JSON chunk claims — on the frame thread. Three refusals or degrades hold the line: `update_world` carries a `seen` set and a range check, so a children cycle or an index naming no node costs the malformed part rather than spinning the frame loop; `load` refuses a document declaring more than `MAX_NODES` (100 000) nodes, a hang rather than a scene; and a texture over `MAX_TEXTURE_PIXELS` (16 000 000, mirroring `service.validation.MAX_IMAGE_PIXELS` without importing service into the viewer) is skipped and counted, the same policy a corrupt map already had — measured from `Image.open`'s lazy size, before any pixel is decoded. Both ceilings are module-level constants so a test can lower one rather than craft a hostile asset. Below them, `glbio.split_glb` turns `struct.error` into `ValueError`: every other refusal in that module is a `ValueError` and callers key on it, so a file too short to hold its own header used to sail straight past them.

**A sprite sheet's grid is decided on the host, never in Blender.** `pipelines/sheet.py` is pure — `plan()` works out the cells, `pack()` composites them with Pillow, `sidecar()` writes the JSON — and `blender_worker.op_sheet` only renders one square transparent PNG per cell into a scratch directory. That split is what makes the grid testable without a GPU and keeps the in-app preview, the renderer and the sidecar from disagreeing. The preview's own camera math lives in `studio/viewer/sheet.py` and has to agree with it: yaw 0 on +Z, extent `max(hypot(sx, sz), sy) * 1.12`. That margin has **one owner**, `pipelines.sheet.FRAME_MARGIN`, which the worker and the preview both import — it used to be two hand-written literals holding up an agreement the module docstring merely asserted, beside a `spec.get("margin", 1.12)` nothing ever wrote a `margin` into. The dead indirection is gone rather than made real: a per-spec margin is a second way to frame a sheet that the preview could not see. Three details that are easy to undo: the camera is framed **once** from the rest bbox (reframe per pose and the subject jumps between rows); the sidecar carries a **flat `cells` list** so an animated clip becomes more cells with `frame > 0` rather than a format v2; and cells arrive grouped by row so the worker re-poses once per pose, not once per frame. Column 0 is the front view — yaw 0 looks along +Y in Blender, and the templates put the subject's forward at −Y.

**Blender's glTF importer invents objects you must ignore.** It parks bone-shape widgets for an imported armature in a collection named `glTF_not_exported`. The collection is excluded from the *view layer*, so nothing renders and nothing re-exports — but the objects are still in `scene.objects`, and a unit icosphere among them trebled `_scene_bounds` and framed every rigged sheet's subject at a third of its size. Every `import_scene.gltf` in the worker is followed by `_purge_import_helpers`.

**Derived values never survive into a new job, and inputs are bounded at the door.** `service.validation.DERIVED_PARAMS` is the single strip list for rerun/promotion — anything the worker records about a finished job's *artifacts* (`composed_prompt`, `mesh_report`, `transform`, …) must be added there when introduced, or a reroll wears a quality verdict about a mesh that doesn't exist yet.
