Anything that *records* one of those values rebinds its top-level key rather than mutating a nested value in place: `_q_generate`'s remesh loop snapshots the winning attempt with a shallow `dict(params)`, and that is exact only while every callee obeys — `note_degraded` grew a `setdefault` plus an item assignment, and with `WARLOCK_MESH_RETRIES` set, attempt 2's failures then wrote straight through the shared dict into `best["params"]["degraded"]`, so the job that shipped attempt 1's mesh carried attempt 2's health record. On the way in, `create_job` enforces the caps next to it (`MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` 20 MB before decode, `MAX_IMAGE_PIXELS` 16 MP from the header before pixels decode, `MAX_PROMPT`, seeds 0..2³¹−1 via `_check_seed` on every route that takes one) and removes the job dir it pre-wrote if the DB insert fails — `input.png` is deliberately written before the row exists, so the cleanup is the other half of that ordering.

**The guidance taxonomy was retired on 2026-08-17, and stale keys are tolerated rather than aliased.** Twelve option tables (`category`, `silhouette`, `material`, `condition`, `rarity`, `emissive`, `setting`, `genre`, `mood`, `art_style`, `palette`, `framing`) were removed wholesale — from `guidance.py`, from `vectors.VECTOR_PARAMS`, from the profile schema, the sweeps axes and the Create form — per `docs/measurements/2026-08-17-taxonomy-retirement.md`, which records the corpus re-keying cost and why no axis ever earned its keep. `platform` survives as geometry resolution only; its prompt-fragment role is gone and `PROMPT_VERSION` moved to 5 (the empty-params composition stays byte-identical because the view clause is re-inlined as the literal it came from). What replaced the old alias table is a **tolerance**: `normalize` iterates its own `_TABLES`, so a retired key in stored params is simply not read; `config_vector` skips params keys outside `VECTOR_PARAMS`; and `compose_prompt` composes only what its (now model-scaffolding-free) field list names. Reroll and promotion of a pre-retirement job must keep succeeding with the fragments simply gone — that tolerance is the invariant, and deleting it would make every stored asset un-rerollable. The rump `_LEGACY_ALIASES` keeps only `platform`'s old hardware-tier spellings, for the same rerun/promote reason it always had.

**Prompt expansion: the mode is an input, the text is derived, and "off" is spelled by absence.** The `expand` field ("asset"/"scene", the local Fooocus GPT-2 in `pipelines/expand.py`) is in `vectors.VECTOR_PARAMS` and `normalize` stores it **only when a mode is on** — writing `"off"` on every job would silently re-key every same-settings vector, which is the taxonomy-retirement cost paid again for nothing. What the expander appended is `expanded_prompt`, in `DERIVED_PARAMS`, so a reroll re-expands (same text: expansion is seeded by `reference_seed` and runs **once, outside the reroll loop** — the reroll redraws the image, never the prompt). Missing weights are **refused at the door** (`check_weights`, field `expand`), for the style-LoRA reason: a stored mode whose expansion never ran would join the findings corpus as evidence about it; past the door, a broken expander costs the enrichment and never the job. Generation is constrained to the `positive.txt` whitelist — the expander may only append aesthetic vocabulary, never rewrite the subject — and prompts already ≥ `expand.DETAILED_PROMPT_TOKENS` are left as written (arXiv 2407.14333). A **tile is never expanded** and `SCENE_TEMPLATE` loses to the `sheet` and `tile` flags: what the pixels are for outranks a prompt mode. `PROMPT_VERSION` moved to 6 for `SCENE_TEMPLATE`; the object/tile/sheet paths are byte-identical to 5 with expansion off, and `tests/test_prompt.py`'s literal pins it.

**Every workspace mode's arm in `_shortcut` returns unconditionally, whether or not its `handle_key` consumed the key.** Each of the seven replaces the asset viewport and the form column with its own, so the shared 2D/3D block at the foot of the function — Delete, the arrows, Ctrl+Enter, F/W/S — is addressed at panes that mode is not showing; `handle_key` answers False for every key it does not bind, so a missing `return` is a fall-through rather than a no-op. Packwright's was lost when Troupe's branch was spliced in ahead of it (the giveaway is an unreachable second `return`), and **Delete in the atlas packer then sent the selected library asset to the trash** — confirm-free, because the binding it fell through to is deliberately confirm-free — while Ctrl+Enter queued a generation. The per-mode key tests cannot see this class of bug: they call `handle_key` directly and never route through the dispatcher, so `tests/test_mode_keys.py` asserts it from the dispatcher's side, over every member of `WORK_MODES` bar `create`. **A global binding is checked above the modes, and that ordering is why there are so few of them.** `_shortcut` answers Ctrl+K's command palette, Ctrl+/, F1 and F10 *before* the `WORK_MODES` gate, because Inker, Clay and Review consume every key they are given. Whatever a global binding names, it takes from those three permanently -- Inker already binds Ctrl+0/Ctrl+1 to its zoom and Clay's axis views want Ctrl+1/3/7 -- which is the argument that eventually took the positional Alt+digit switch out altogether (below). **Esc leaves the pass-through modes** (Home and app Settings; the Manual stopped being one in wave 3 and Esc closes its overlay instead) for the mode it came from, and `App._note_mode` samples that pair **once per key event rather than once per frame** -- a mode switch happens from inside `_shortcut`, so a frame-start sample would still be holding the mode from before it and Esc would go two steps back. Home is the floor rather than a place you escape from: the app opens on it, so there is routinely nothing behind it. `studio/palette.py` is the palette's pure half -- commands, subsequence match, ranking, stdlib only -- and its mode commands are **derived from `modes.MODES`**, because a palette is a second index of everything the app can do and a hand-written one drifts; switching is `state.set_mode`, the one function `App._set_mode` also calls, after the palette's own copy drifted in the way that matters -- it lacked the early return on `key == state.mode`, so re-selecting the current mode set `previous_mode == mode` and the next Esc fell back to Home. Home's **New...** menu is the third index of the same list (the tile grid it replaced in wave 4 was the navigation drawn twice) and is checked against `modes.WORK_MODES` for coverage, names and order left editorial; a command that cannot run right now is *listed and greyed*, since an empty result teaches neither that it exists nor which mode owns it.

**`ConfirmQueue` and `PromptQueue` really queue, and `pending` is the head rather than a field.** They used to keep one slot and drop anything that arrived while a question was up, on the reasoning that the user cannot act twice between frames -- true of *clicks* and false of a finished task landing beside one, and false of any path that asks more than once. Assigning `None` to `pending` was how a caller cancelled, which on a queue would silently discard everything behind it too, so it is read-only and `dismiss()` is the operation. `_request_quit`'s three hand-nested lambdas became a list walked by index; the *chain* stays, because it is the semantics rather than the workaround -- asking all three side by side would mean clicking "Keep editing" on the first still left two questions to dismiss. A modal owns the keyboard while it is up (`App._modal_open`), or one Esc both cancels the dialog and leaves the mode behind it; releases still pass, because Inker's space-to-pan is a hold and would otherwise latch.

**Four toast levels, and the ladder is reading time rather than severity.** `state.TOAST_LEVELS` is the table -- info and success share a dwell because a confirmation is read at a glance, a warning is a sentence, an error usually says what to do next. An unrecognised level renders as an ordinary notice, the rule `Toast.action` already followed and what lets a level be raised before `widgets` has heard of it. Hovering *pauses* a toast by pushing `born` forward by the frame's delta rather than by recording a pause timestamp: age is read from `born` in four places and a second field saying "but not that age" is how two of them come to disagree. `state.toast_log` is the session's history, in the diagnostics popup rather than behind a bell of its own -- it is the same question as "what is wrong with my install" asked about the last ten seconds -- and it holds the `Toast` objects, because a list of strings could not say what level a message was. A sweep raises exactly **one** toast (`jobs_cache.sweep_summary`), judged against the *loaded* window, which is a stated limitation that fails in the safe direction: an early summary is a slightly wrong number where a missed one is silence at the end of an overnight run.

**A filter box that is also a query, and a sort that admits what it cannot answer.** `state.parse_query` splits `tag:`/`status:`/`kind:`/`stage:`/`id:`/`name:` out of the library's text box, and **an unrecognised prefix stays free text** -- silently reinterpreting a colon somebody typed on purpose is how a search starts returning nothing with no explanation. Field terms *add* to the combos rather than overriding them, so `status:error` with the status combo on *done* correctly shows nothing. `Filters.order` offers six keys, every one stable within a tie, and every one buckets its **unanswerable** rows at the end in both directions -- the rule `best` already followed for an unranked job, generalised, because "unknown" is not a value at one end of a scale and filing the unmeasured backlog under "smallest" would be a wrong answer rather than a partial one. Size reads the deferred storage walk, which is why `JobsCache` carries a `_sizes_generation` the memo keys on.

**The trash is a column, and Delete stopped asking.** Migration 9's `deleted_at` makes `Filters.trash` a *view* rather than a filter -- everything else narrows what you are looking at, this changes which set you are looking at -- and the files stay on disk, which is the whole feature. `library.delete_asset` trashes and keeps its name at every call site: what the user asked for is "get this out of my library", and the reversibility of that is a property of the app rather than of each call site; `purge_asset` is the permanent one and only the trash reaches it. The card's Delete raises no confirm, because the trash *is* the confirmation and an undo available an hour later beats a question answered in half a second while looking at something else -- the irreversible paths keep theirs. **Trashing a queued job cancels it**: deleting used to remove the row so the worker's poll never saw it again, and a trashed row is still a row. `empty_trash` reads `store.trashed()` rather than a page, since "empty" that left the older half behind while reporting success is the worst reading of the word. Prune deliberately still deletes from disk -- one that trashed two hundred jobs would free nothing while reporting that it had. **And because it is a view, it is not persisted**: `state.VOLATILE_FILTERS` drops `trash` from the saved filter bar in *both* directions, so a settings file an older build wrote opens on the library too. Everything else in `Filters` narrows what you are looking at and is worth restoring; restoring this one reopens the app into a set that hides every asset, which is a setting nobody chose. The size/count figure the bulk bar draws beside **Empty trash...** is `service.jobs.trash_size`, measured through `ctx.submit` and drawn from the last answer -- the walk is the deferred-storage rule again, never the frame thread.

**The palette is three tables of one set of names, resolved live.** `tokens.PALETTES` holds dark, light and pixel, and `theme.__getattr__` (PEP 562) resolves `theme.ACCENT` through whichever is in force -- which is why the switch needed no edit at any of the dozens of call sites, and why a module constant would have been wrong: it binds at import, so a switch would have repainted imgui's own style and left every hand-drawn rect, badge and ring on the old colours. `theme.apply` still has to be re-run, because imgui's style holds *copies*. The light palette keeps the elevation **roles** rather than inverting the numbers: PANEL is still the surface a form sits on and ELEV_1/ELEV_2 are still steps away from the floor, which on a light ground means darker. The third palette is the demonstration that the claim above was real: `pixel` -- the warm neutral ramp, near-black canvas surround and amber accent that the pixel workspaces are drawn in, because Inker is measured against the program its users already know in format and behaviour and, until it shipped, in look not at all -- needed **no edit** to `theme.apply`, to the Settings combo (which builds its options from the table), to persistence, or to a single pane. It keeps dark's elevation *direction* and differs from it in temperature, which is what `test_the_pixel_palette_is_warm_and_keeps_darks_elevation_direction` pins: every contrast bar and every name check would still pass on a palette that had drifted neutral and thereby lost the only reason it exists. It is not an eyedropper copy of anyone's chrome -- a real pixel editor's greys fail several of this repo's bars outright, so the hues and roles are taken and the values lifted until each clears. Adding a fourth is the same shape of change: fourteen names, the contrast gates in `tests/test_accessibility.py` (which parametrize over `sorted(tokens.PALETTES)` and so enrol it unasked), a label in `app_settings._THEME_LABELS`, and the `--themes` default in `scripts/screenshot_modes.py`. A stored theme name that this build does not carry falls back to dark rather than raising, because it comes out of a settings file. The font atlas is re-baked on a scale change **between frames** (`state.fonts_dirty` -> `fonts.reload`), never inside one: `clear_fonts` invalidates every `ImFont` handle and those are pushed and popped all through `_build_ui`. What actually needs the rebuild is `ICON_OFFSET`, baked as an absolute pixel figure at load time.

**Every animation in the app is a number `motion.py` owns, and reduce-motion is why that matters.** Nothing hand-rolls an easing any more: `motion.value` is the exponential approach for a value chasing a target that can change under it (hover, the sliding pill, the sidebar width), `motion.ease` is the one-shot ramp for a move with a known length (the mode veil, a popover's rise), and `ease_out_cubic`/`ease_in_out_cubic` are the curves -- the toast rise had its own written-out copy of the first of these, and a second spelling is precisely what the accessibility switch could not reach. `motion.REDUCED` collapses every duration in one place, so a consumer inherits it by reading from this module and by nothing else; under it a *fade* still runs where it is not movement (a toast's opacity, `drop_flash`'s acknowledgement keeps its dwell and loses its ramp) and `ease` reads 1.0 on its first frame -- **arrived, not never-appears**, which is the failure a naive `duration = 0` has. Three rules under that are load-bearing. `peek` reads without advancing, because a button's colour is needed to *draw* it and its hover is known only afterwards; calling `value` twice instead steps the easing twice a frame and towards the wrong target first, which is what made `card`'s lift take visibly longer to arrive than to leave. `animating()` is the idle clamp's fifth wake condition and counts only keys **the last frame touched** -- a card hovered and then navigated away from keeps a target nothing will ask for again, and without the frame stamp the app would never idle again either. And the one-frame lag in every hover is not a compromise but the only order available, which is why `note_hover` is a separate call after the item rather than a flag passed into it. `scripts/screenshot_modes.py` waits (bounded) on `animating()` before it reads a frame: three warm-up frames are 50 ms of a 200 ms transition, so without that every capture in the repo's own "somebody looked at it" pass is a picture of a half-cleared veil.

**Eleven modes and one rail; Create is one destination with five stages.** `studio/modes.py` is the whole list — `home`, `library`, `create` | `inker`, `clay`, `poser`, `troupe`, `plotter`, `packwright` | `review`, `settings` — and it is *data only*: the dispatch stays hand-coded in `main._build_ui`, because "`state.mode` is the only thing that decides what a pane shows" is true only while there is exactly one place doing the deciding, and a table of callbacks would be a second. The rail is drawn in every mode (a mode that hides it is a dead end), and `AppState.mode` defaults to `"home"` — the app opens on Home every launch, which is what the deleted `state.landing` flag used to mean. **The Library is a mode, and that is part of what let Home stop being a tile grid**: it was a tile behind a `state.landing_view` sub-view enum, which is what a real destination looks like when there is nowhere to put it, while the other seven tiles were the navigation again under the same names and the same glyphs. It is a single pane drawing the call Home's tile used to make, its glyph *moved* from `landing._SUBVIEW_ICONS` rather than re-picked, and `landing_view` is gone in both directions. **The Manual and the profile manager went the other way** (the UI redesign, wave 3): both are *about* the screen you are on rather than places to go, so both became overlays -- `manual.render.draw_overlay` (F1, every `help_button`, Esc) and `panes/profiles_panel.draw_sheet` (the Reference stage's **Manage...**, Esc through the dirty-draft guard). A mode switch to show help took away the control the reader was asking about, which is the failure the (?) button exists to prevent. No mode is persisted at all — a stored one would have no reader, and a write with no reader is how the halves drift, so `test_no_mode_is_persisted_anywhere` scans for `settings.set("mode", …)` and fails on any. `modes.WORK_MODES` (`create`/`inker`/`clay`/`poser`/`review`/`plotter`/`packwright`) is the subset that owns a viewport or a form and is therefore the only subset that takes keyboard shortcuts -- with one deliberate widening: Home's Resume list and the Library both take Up/Down/Enter for list navigation (a cursor over what is on screen, not a command about a pane that is not); `modes.VIEWPORT_MODES` (`create` alone, since wave 5) is the narrower one that draws the *asset* viewport and so the only one `_sync_viewer` acts for. Inker, Clay, Poser, Review, Plotter, Packwright and Troupe are work modes that own their own centre pane — `modes.WORKSPACE_MODES` — which is why `_shortcut` returns after each one's `handle_key` **whether or not it consumed the key**: all of them return False with nothing open, and the fall-through let F/W/S and Ctrl+Enter act on panes those modes have replaced. **There is no positional Alt+digit binding any more, and the removal is the decision rather than a casualty of one.** It was defensible while there were ten modes and ten digits, because the binding was then the picture on screen rather than a second table to keep in agreement with it. Thirteen segments against ten digits ended that: either two modes have no key, or something states which two — and that something is precisely the second table the positional scheme existed to avoid. So `mode_for_digit`/`digit_key_label`/`digit_for_mode` and `main._digit_keys` are **deleted rather than left unused** (a helper with no caller reads as a supported entry point — the `setup()` lesson), the digits go back to Clay's element modes and Review's grade scale, and `Ctrl+K` becomes the *only* keyboard route to a mode. (Wave 5 took the count back down to ten by folding 2D and 3D into one; the decision does not follow it back down, because the objection was the second table and not the arithmetic.) which is what turns `palette._mode_commands`' derivation from `modes.MODES` from tidiness into a requirement. **The order is the grouping, and the grouping is now written out.** `modes.RAIL_GROUPS` is three hand-written tuples -- where an asset begins (Home, the Library, Create), the six creative workspaces, and the rail's footer (Review beside Settings) -- and `MODES` is asserted to be their flattening. That is the *reversal* of the derived `GROUP_BREAKS` it replaced, and the reason is that no predicate over `WORK_MODES` can make the grouping: the first group is one work mode and two that are not, and the footer is one of each. The footer is the *end matter* -- the two destinations where you are not making something -- which is why Review moved there out of the middle of the six workspaces: judging what came out is not one of the six things you make. Deriving the gaps was right while the only claim being made was "workspaces are not places"; a rule that cannot state the grouping is not a better version of stating it. **The rail gives way in a stated order, and the last thing to give is an item's reachability.** A clipped control is an unreachable mode whichever axis it runs off, which is what `widgets.segmented_control`'s `compact` labelling was for (the glyph alone, full label in a tooltip, **all-or-nothing** -- abbreviating only the segments that did not fit would be one control saying two kinds of thing, and which two would change as the window was dragged; that widget and its rule survive, in the Settings pane's categories). The rail restates it on both axes. Horizontally: an expanded rail that would leave the three columns unusable draws collapsed instead, *without touching the preference*, because what the user chose and what fits are two different facts. Vertically: a window too short for every row closes the row-to-row spacing first and only then compresses the rows themselves, down to `rail.MIN_ITEM_H` -- the same 24 px "comfortably clickable" figure the old health dot grew a hit area to meet -- and scrolls below that. `layout.RAIL_RESERVED` is how the sidebars learn what is left: `rail.tick` sets it immediately before `layout.tick`/`measure`, because a rail measured afterwards leaves the columns disagreeing with the window by exactly its own width for a frame. Review is the one that *borrows* the shared asset viewer rather than drawing its own — for a bench run's `items/<unit>/model.glb` — which is exactly why it must stay out of `VIEWPORT_MODES`: `_sync_viewer` would otherwise reload the selected asset over the sweep unit on screen, and leaving the mode needs no cleanup because that same comparison against `viewer.path` reloads on the way back into Create. **What decides whether Review loads is `viewer.path` too, never a remembered unit key**: unit keys repeat across runs of one sweep spec, so a key-keyed marker said "already showing that" about a *different run's* mesh and let a verdict be filed against something nobody had looked at — and it survived a trip through Create, so coming back drew a library asset under Review's verdict buttons. `viewer.path` is set even when there is nothing to show, so an errored unit is attempted (and reported) once rather than every frame. `_review_viewport` also honours `viewer.pose_mode` exactly as `_sync_viewer` does, because loading over the pose editor discards unsaved rotations without the confirm `pose_panel.guard` puts in front of every other exit. Its state (`studio/review_mode.py`, the `clay_mode.py` shape: no imgui, `ctx.submit` for the bench-directory walk, `verdicts.record_verdict` inline on the frame thread) persists nothing at all, because a stored run directory outlives the sweep it names. Clay's viewport also takes the mouse in `_events` before the asset viewer sees it, or one drag orbits both. `ManualState.open` is the deliberate inversion of that class's old docstring -- while the Manual was a mode a second visibility flag would have been a way for the two to disagree, and there is no mode left to disagree with. The app-Settings pane (`panes/app_settings.py`) is the app's own settings rather than a job's -- theme, layout, and what the app loaded. Its model list was read-only **by design** for as long as the app had no way to fetch anything, and that sentence is now retired: the rows carry a checkbox and a Download button (see the offline invariant's stated exception — the app still has no way to fetch anything, which is why the button spawns a child that does). What survives of the old rationale is the rest of the pane, which stays read-only where the answer is not the user's to change. Two things the button changed elsewhere. **Missing-ness is a flag, not a substring**: the rows used to be marked by testing a label for the literal word `"missing"`, which `main.py` only ever put into base-model labels — so every style LoRA was silently unmarked — and `service.downloads.rows` now carries a real `present` per row, from the same probes doctor reports. **And the pane's group headings are `fetch.KINDS`' own column, not a table beside it**: `_GROUPS` was a third hand-copy of the download-kind vocabulary (with `CHECK_PREFIXES` and `entries()`), under a comment claiming a new kind "cannot silently append unlabelled" — the exact opposite of what it did, since `_models` iterates the headings and so *dropped* an unlisted kind entirely. One `KINDS` table now owns the key, the registry it reads, doctor's row prefix and the pane's heading, and an unrecognised kind falls into an "Other" group rather than vanishing. And `Ctx.base_models`/`style_loras`/`model_rows` are **no longer immutable**: they were documented as read-once-at-startup, which was true only while nothing could make weights appear, so a finished fetch re-runs `doctor.run_checks` wholesale (`current_checks(force=True)`, past the health TTL, because the disk demonstrably just changed) and recomputes all three. Create draws the settings / viewport / selected-asset-inspector three-column layout, with `widgets.stage_rail` above its settings column; everything in `main._SINGLE_PANE_MODES` (`home`, `settings`, `library`) fills the host window with one pane; `WORKSPACE_MODES` fills it with a mode's own three-column workspace; and those three sets must partition `modes.KEYS` exactly — the dispatch ends in a bare `else` that would otherwise draw Inker for an unlisted mode. **Create's five stages are the pipeline that already existed, made walkable rather than made up** (the UI redesign, wave 5). `studio/create_stages.py` is `STAGES` (`reference`, `mesh`, `rig`, `pose`, `export`), `shows`/`available`/`reached`/`go`, and it is imgui-free and draws nothing — the rail is `widgets.stage_rail` and the panes are unchanged. **A stage is a derived position, not a stored one**: `state.create_stage` says where you are standing, `reached(job, rig_meta, poses)` says what the asset has actually got, and those are different facts — the rail's ticks come off the second, `reached` returns None with nothing selected, and Export is never reached at all because `save_artifact` copies a file somewhere the job row never hears about. It takes **three** pieces of evidence and not two, because a pose is a file under `<job>/poses/` and none of its artifacts is in `files.LISTED`. `at()` asks the *mode* and the stage together, deliberately: `create_stage` is not cleared on a mode change (coming back from Inker lands where you left), so a pane asking only the stage would grow Create's sections in Poser. `go()` is the only thing that moves the selection, and it takes `follow=False` for the two arrivals that are about to *make* something — a dropped image and a promotion both carry their own source, and walking onto a mesh the reference already has would describe the wrong asset while the form builds another. **Job stages and UI stages are two vocabularies on purpose**: the corpus is keyed on `verdicts.STAGES` (`model`, `reference`, `blank`) and the rail says `mesh`, so `test_create_stages.py` pins that no `service` module imports `create_stages` — a UI stage name in a verdict record would be a rename of the corpus. The panes keep their one-owner rule across the fold: the Reference stage always submits `output=reference` and owns every field that composes the SDXL prompt; the Mesh stage starts from a finished reference (`service.jobs.promote_to_model`, whose mesh-side params are all optional overrides) or an upload, and owns only mesh, rig, pose and sheet controls. `platform` belongs to the Mesh stage alone since the 2026-08-17 taxonomy retirement — it is a geometry-resolution selection and `settings_3d` owns the select; `settings_2d`'s prompt-fragment copy is gone with the taxonomy — and those two modules keep their names, their `FOCUS_PANE` keys (`"2d"`/`"3d"`) and their persist keys, which are not mode names and were left alone rather than routed through the repo's densest test surface for a rename. Plotter and Packwright follow the same rule inside their own workspaces: the tileset pane is the sole owner of what a stamp puts down, the layers pane of object metadata, and `packwright_settings` of `PackSettings` — every write to which goes through `packwright_mode.set_settings`, the one place that both pushes the undo step and marks the pack dirty. Anything the worker records about a finished job's artifacts still has to join `service.validation.DERIVED_PARAMS`, or an override-carrying promotion inherits a verdict about a mesh that doesn't exist.

**The shell is a menu bar and a status bar, and neither owns an action or a fact.** `studio/menus.py` draws one `menu_bar` on the host window in every mode and `studio/status_bar.py` draws one 24 dp line at its foot, and the rule both obey is that they are **presentation over a registry that already existed**. `menus.specs` builds its rows from `palette.commands(ctx)` and `inker_ops.OPS` — label, enabled, `checked`, shortcut and `disabled_reason` all come off the command, so the menu cannot offer what the palette does not, cannot enable what a key would refuse, and cannot explain a refusal differently. `_COMMAND_PATHS` is the *only* thing the menu adds: a placement table from command key to root, which is a fact about the menu and about nothing else. A command with no entry in it either becomes a **contextual menu named for the current workspace** (Actions/Viewport groups, outside `home`/`settings`/`library`/`inker`) or is simply not in the menu — never filed into File or Edit, because a root everybody understands stops being one the moment it becomes the place miscellaneous things go. `ROOTS` fixes the order and `roots()` puts the contextual menu between Edit and View, so a workspace's own menu appears in the same place every time rather than wherever its name sorts. Likewise `status_bar.items` is a **pure data function** returning `StatusItem`s, which is what lets a test assert the shell's account without a window; `draw` measures and elides from the tail, so the leading item — which workspace you are in — is the last thing a narrow window gives up. **The resource meter is deliberately not in `items`.** It is right-anchored in `draw` and its width is *reserved before* the leading items are measured, following `overlay.doctor_banner`'s rule: in the list it would have been the first thing dropped, which is backwards for the one number that is read while a generation is being decided on — and the reservation is conditional, so below the width where the first left-hand item would not fit beside it the meter goes whole rather than truncating. **What the shell took, it took from somewhere that then stopped drawing it**: the health indicator was a dot with a hit area in the rail's footer and is now `items`' one `warning=True` row (`N issue(s)`, `rail.request("diagnostics")` on click, and *nothing at all* when every check passes — there is no green state to read), and the navigation-labels toggle was a chevron at the rail's foot and is now `Window ▸ Navigation labels`. `rail.draw` states the resulting rule in one line — *the rail is destinations only* — and that is what keeps the two from growing a second copy of each other's chrome.

**Home answers three questions, and none of them is "where do I go".** The first-run orientation card is gone with the grid it explained: a card telling you which pipeline to start in sat above a screen whose whole left column is now a status report and whose right column is a list of what you were doing, and an explanation of a screen that explains itself is one more thing to dismiss. It was a grid of nine 380x64 tiles, seven of which were `modes.MODES` again — the same names under the same glyphs, one click either way, against a switch that is drawn unconditionally at the top of *every* frame. So the screen the app opens on spent all of itself re-offering navigation that is permanently on screen. It is two columns now: what changed and what the machine is doing on one side, what you were working on down the other. Four rules under that. **The changelog is hand-written and only read** (`CHANGELOG.md` at the repo root, force-included into the wheel exactly as `docs/manual` is, parsed by the stdlib-only `warlock/changelog.py` which answers `[]` for every way of having nothing to say): every commit subject in this repository is `Warlock vN.N.N` and carries no detail, so a generated changelog would be a column of version numbers — and `tests/test_changelog.py` pins the top heading against `pyproject.toml`, because a hand-written file is exactly what a release bump leaves behind. **The Resume list is one list, which is why `studio/recents.py` had to exist**: Inker, Clay, Plotter and Packwright each kept their own `recent: list[str]`, and merging four bare path lists at draw time is not untidy but *impossible* — they carry an order within themselves and none between them — so the four fields and their eight near-identical `remember`/`forget` methods are gone, replaced by one timestamped list with a one-shot migration off the four legacy settings keys (which are left in place, so an older build still reads them; a `None` stamp sorts last, `Filters.order`'s rule for an unanswerable row). **Assets get no write path at all**: a job row already has a `created_at`, so Home derives those rows from the cache at draw time, and a row that is trashed or pruned simply stops being an answer with nothing to clean up. And **the status block's unreviewed count is asked for on a cadence, never on the frame thread** — it is a table scan behind the one serialized connection — with the stamp moved only when `ctx.submit` *accepted* the request, the `findings_dirty` rule, or a refusal strands the row on a figure nothing will recompute; `None` (not asked) and `0` (nothing waiting) both draw as no row, because "0 unreviewed" is a claim about an install nobody has measured.

**Plotter is a mode, and its engine is pure.** `studio/plotter/` is the fourth headless package beside `inker/`, `clay/` and `packwright/` — stdlib, numpy, a lazy Pillow, `studio.undo`, and `studio.tilegrid` (the shared tile vocabulary the gid word, the sliced atlas and the blob collapse were promoted into on 2026-08-18, so `.gid`, `.tileset` and `.blob` are no longer siblings here but a leaf reached for like `studio.undo` is), pinned exactly by `tests/plotter/test_plotter_imports.py`. **A cell is one `uint32` and the three transform flags travel through every hop bit-exactly**: `FLIP_H` is `0x80000000`, so an `int32` layer would store it as a negative number and every comparison after that is an argument about two's complement. Nothing between the reader and the renderer re-derives them, which is what makes `tools.flood_fill` match on the *full encoded value* — a mirrored wall tile bounds a fill of its unmirrored twin, which is the right answer because two cells that draw differently are two different cells. **A retile costs the brush, not the map.** `terrain._retile_into` runs each terrain's membership pass over the box grown by one ring — every cell the blob case can depend on — and not over the whole layer, which is what a paint costs when the pass is whole-layer and a terrain drag calls it once per cell along the interpolated line (7.9 ms a cell at 512², the size `plotter_setup.MAX_TILES` allows). It is exact rather than approximate: the ring's own cases are discarded, and where the grown window meets the map edge the padding `blob.masks_from` applies there *is* the map edge, so `outside` lands identically — asserted against a literal copy of the whole-layer pass at every corner and edge in `tests/plotter/test_terrain.py`. **A Tiled feature this editor does not model is refused by name, never dropped** -- and a refusal only ever moves because the editor learned to model the thing: isometric left the list when `plotter/project.py` arrived and `tests/plotter/test_tmx_refusals.py` gained the acceptance case in the same change, while staggered and hexagonal stayed, which is also what keeps `gid`'s missing hexagonal-rotation bit honest (a file that could set one never gets past the door, and `tmx._finish` now probes for it *by name* rather than letting it fail as an out-of-range id, which is how it was caught before -- the right outcome under the wrong sentence). Tiled's Wang sets were **recognise-or-refuse**, and that asymmetry ended when the editor learned to model the general case -- which is the same rule this paragraph states, applied to itself. The blob preset is still *recognised* first, and what is recognised is still exactly what `tsx.write_wangsets` emits, so its positional terrain rows and its byte-identical export are untouched; a **foreign** set -- corner-only, edge-only, or mixed with a table of its own -- is now read as data into `Tileset.wangsets` and painted by wangid constraint matching (`tilegrid/wang.py`, `terrain.paint_wang`) instead of being named and refused. Two rules make that safe rather than a half-read. The general path is *beside* the preset and never touches it, so `is_terrain_set`, `terrain_of` and `_retile_into` still mean the 47-case collapse and the whole existing corpus stays byte-identical. And **a cell with no matching tile is left untouched** rather than given the nearest one: a wrong tile is the silent half-read in picture form, where a hole in a field is something a user can see. The eight-slot sharing geometry is the part with a trap in it -- an edge is shared with one neighbour, a **corner with three**, because a corner is a lattice point and four cells meet at every lattice point, so a matcher consulting only the diagonal reconciles against nothing on a stroke painted left to right. The refusals are: the drop is invisible right up to the moment the user saves, at which point the other half is gone, and `tests/plotter/test_tmx_refusals.py` has one case per entry. **Rotated objects round-trip, and that is the shape a refusal is supposed to take on its way out.** They were refused for exactly as long as the canvas drew an unrotated outline for them -- a wrong picture is worse than a refusal -- and the refusal lifted in the change that taught the canvas to draw the rotated outline, hit-test it and resize against it, which is the rule this whole paragraph states: a refusal only ever moves because the editor learned to model the thing. The *document* always carried `rotation`, because what a refusal is one day flipped onto has to exist before it can be flipped. The resize is the part with a trap in it and it is stated here because it was got wrong once: `x`/`y`/`w`/`h` are the object's **unrotated** origin and extent, so a resize computed as the min and max of two *map*-space points writes a rectangle in the wrong place at any nonzero angle -- `plotter_canvas._resized` works in the object's own frame and puts the answer back. **An object's geometry is one tagged `Shape`, and `kind`/`w`/`h` are derived from it.** All eight geometries (rect, point, ellipse, capsule, polygon, polyline, tile, text) are frozen dataclasses in `_map_model.py`, and `OBJECT_KINDS` is all eight -- it used to narrow to the two the canvas had a gesture for, and the canvas now has one for each. An exotic shape is still *constructed* (`MapObject(shape=Ellipse(...))`) rather than spelled, and a kind string outside the eight stays a refusal at both the constructor and `set_object`. `capsule` is **Warlock dialect** and not a Tiled shape; see the dialect rule below. Frozen is what lets `snapshot()` share one shape with the document and with every step on the stack instead of deep-copying geometry once per drag frame, and the compat properties are **read-only** because two spellings of one fact must not be separately settable -- `obj.w = 5` beside a stored shape is the entire bug class. `merged_object_values` is the one place a `w=` from the canvas and a `shape=` from anywhere else are reconciled, which is why `set_object` and `place_object` both route through it and why `_apply_object_props` assigns the shape and ignores the derived echo travelling beside it. A tile object's gid carries its flip flags exactly as a cell does, so `tmx._finish` validates it in the same pass and for the same reason: a gid nothing accounts for is the same unreadable file wherever it sits. **Both halves of that pass probe bit 28 by name**, and the object half did not for a while: `gid.decompose` strips the three transform flags and leaves the hexagonal-rotation bit in place, so a tile object carrying one was refused as "uses tile 268435457, which none of this map's tilesets accounts for" — a true sentence about the wrong problem, which is the exact wording the layer half exists to avoid. The rule held for tiles on a grid and not for the same gid on an object layer, which is the shape every miss in this file takes. The `.wmap` door had the same miss in both halves: `wmap._validate` now probes the bit by name — on tile layers and tile objects alike — before the tileset accounting, so a hand-edited map is refused for the feature it carries rather than for an id nothing accounts for. **`.wmap` stores Tiled ids and positions, never process uids** — a uid is minted per process and means nothing in a file, which is why this format needs no `reserve_uid` where `.wblk` does: nothing is ever restored onto a number the counter has already issued. The persistent `id` is the opposite and *is* stored: an ordinary document field with its own monotone counters (`next_layer_id`/`next_object_id`, never decremented on undo, because an object-typed property may go on naming a deleted object's id), and it is what such a property references. A file that carries no id — every v1 and v2 `.wmap` — has one minted on read from those counters, in paint order, by the same rule `tmx._export_ids` applies at the export door; the two have to agree or a map that travelled out through Tiled and back would come home wearing different ids than the same map saved and reopened. The stated cost, accepted rather than engineered around: after an add-then-undo a re-save differs from the saved bytes in those two counters alone, and Tiled behaves identically. **A layer stack is a tree, and one resolver is what flattens it.** There are four layer kinds in `_map_model.py` — tile, object, group and image — and `doc.layers` is the *root* list rather than the layer list: a `GroupLayer` holds children of its own, `layer(uid)`/`index_of`/`parent_uid_of` walk, `children_of(parent_uid)` is the one funnel either list is reached through, and `tile_layers()` returns the tile leaves depth-first in paint order — which is the shape the `.wmap` array enumeration, the flat renderer and the minimap already looped in, so the tree landed behind the name they had rather than beside it. Every kind carries the same six decorations (`class_name`, `tint`, `offset_x`/`offset_y`, `parallax_x`/`parallax_y`, declared field by field on all four because a dataclass base would force `TileLayer.data` to carry a default), and **`plotter/scene.py` owns what they add up to**: `resolve(doc)` yields one frozen `Resolved` per drawable leaf, in paint order, with offset **summed**, parallax and opacity **multiplied**, visibility **AND**ed, tint multiplied per channel and lock **OR**ed. A group is a *state its descendants inherit*, never a surface anything is composited onto — which is why two half-opaque siblings inside a half-opaque group still show through each other, here and in Tiled, and why the leaf's own `visible` never moves when an ancestor is hidden. The lock is **reported and not enforced**, the engine's standing rule: `write_region` has to keep working on a locked layer or an undo could not put back what was there before the lock. `scene.py` deliberately never reads `data` and never asks a layer for its shape — it is the one piece of the drawing path that a change of tile storage must not reach, and infinite maps arrived without it having to move. **An index stopped being an address**, so `LayerAddEdit`/`LayerRemoveEdit` carry a `parent_uid` beside it and `LayerMoveEdit` records `(parent_uid, index)` on each side: reparenting and reordering are one gesture and therefore one step, a removed group travels with its whole subtree as one object, its cost is the subtree's bytes (`_subtree_bytes` recurses — a cost that stopped at the group would report zero for megabytes the edit is the only thing keeping alive), and a group moved into its own subtree is refused by name rather than clamped. **The tree no longer outruns the Tiled formats, and the writer-door refusals it needed are gone.** Groups, image layers, pixel offsets, tints, parallax and layer classes all round-trip through both Tiled spellings now, so `tmx._refuse_unwritable_layers` and `_refuse_unwritable_objects` -- which existed to stop us writing a file our own reader would refuse -- have no work left and were deleted rather than left as no-ops. `TiledUnsupported(..., exporting=True)` is still the right shape for the door and is still raised where the document genuinely outruns the format; what changed is that the layer tree stopped being such a case. **What outruns Tiled now is the other direction: constructs Tiled does not have.** An oblique orientation with `skewx`/`skewy`, a per-layer blend mode, a per-object opacity, a `capsule` shape and a `list` property type are all modelled, written and read back by this editor and by nothing else -- they are **Warlock dialect**, they are listed as their own state in `docs/COMPAT.md`, and the price of having them is that `tsx.TILED_VERSION` stays *behind* the compat target until a human with Tiled installed confirms a real Tiled opens one of our exports. Writing invented syntax under a `tiledversion` that claims otherwise is the one thing this arrangement exists to stop, and it has been done once already: the constant was bumped to `1.12.2` in the same change that deleted the sentence forbidding it, and the bump was reverted rather than the gate satisfied. `.wmap` is the format that carries every dialect construct without qualification. **`.wmap` holds the tree**: its version 3 container is the tree — recursive manifest layer entries (`"type": "group"` carrying a `layers` list of its own), `id`/`class`/`tint`/`offset`/`parallax` on every layer and an object layer's `draworder` beside them, an image layer's pixels as an `images/N.png` member, and objects storing a persistent id, a rotation and one tagged `shape` record instead of the derived `kind`/`w`/`h` echo — while the tile arrays stayed a **flat depth-first** `layers/N.npy` enumeration, so the half of the archive that holds megabytes did not churn for a change to the half that holds kilobytes. v1 and v2 are still read, through tolerant defaults rather than a reader per version (the `locked` precedent). **A newer version is written only when an older reader would lose something.** `wmap.VERSION` is 10 and `BASE_VERSION` is 3, and `_document_version(doc)` returns the *oldest* version that represents the document — one schema with additive defaults, not a writer per version: v4 (`TILED_ERA_VERSION`) is the 1.12-era additions, v5 is a terrain set carrying `phases > 1`, v6 per-tile metadata, v7 tileset presentation, v8 foreign Wang sets, v9 an image collection, v10 an infinite map. Each is mandatory, for one reason in different clothes — an ungated `phases` key leaves an old reader dividing every local id by the wrong row count and misattributing the terrain of every painted cell, an ungated collection leaves it slicing an atlas that is not one, and an ungated `origin` leaves it opening an infinite map with every cell at a different true coordinate than it was painted at — all silently. Nothing generates a phased set since the ground path was deleted on 2026-08-18; the gate stays because the files do, and a document using none of it still writes a file a v3 reader opens whole. The entries themselves are written unconditionally; this function chooses only the number at the top of the manifest, which is why **every field a newer writer adds has to be named in the gate** -- the rule, not a field list, because a list here is exactly what goes stale as writers grow. A field the gate does not ask about is a document declaring an old version while carrying a key that old reader drops on the floor, silently, with nothing in the file to say it happened -- `ObjectLayer.color` was exactly that miss, and a dialect `list` property is the same shape of hazard one level down: properties nest, so the check recurses through every container that can carry one rather than probing the spots someone remembered. **An infinite map is stored as a dense window over the populated extent plus an `origin`, not as sparse chunks** — the reserved `infinite` key activated exactly as reserved, and the reserved `chunks` key stays deliberately unused. The chunked shape is the *Tiled* format's, translated at that codec's door (`tmx.chunks_of`/`chunks_from`), and it never reaches the document: that is what left every tool, both renderers, the terrain and wang engines, the stroke session and `TilePatchEdit` working on the array they already worked on, and made growth an ordinary `resize` — already undoable, already moving objects by the right rule, already with a corpus. `resize` slides the origin by the same offset on an infinite map, in one place rather than at each caller, and the origin rides that step's `ResizeEdit`: a shape undone without its origin is every cell at a different true coordinate. **`offset` follows that rule too, and did not until 2026-08-22** -- it never touched the origin and its `ResizeEdit` push omitted both origin fields, so on an infinite map `wrap=True` rolled over a *window* whose size and position are an artifact of painting history (two documents with identical true content offset differently depending on how they happened to be framed) and `wrap=False` cleared cells shifted past an edge that does not exist, which is data loss rather than a clip. An infinite map now refuses `wrap` by name -- a roll needs a fixed boundary -- and takes the translation instead: whole-map scope is *only* the origin sliding (no array is touched, and objects, being window-relative in the document, travel with it), and layer scope grows the window by exactly what the shifted layer needs and reframes every layer into it, as one `ResizeEdit`. The stated cost is memory on a map painted in two clusters far apart, capped by `MAX_DIMENSION` on the populated extent; the stated gain is that a flood is bounded to the window and the window *is* the content, so “bounded or it does not terminate” is what the storage says rather than something each tool must remember. `WmapUnstorable` outlives the four refusals it was built for — a layer kind the container has no entry for still raises it, and `plotter_io._encoded` and `plotter_mode.export_library` still catch it by name, because a writer-door refusal on the frame thread has to become a toast rather than take the window down, and a handler on bare `ValueError` would dress every genuine encoder defect as a polite refusal. Two renderers exist and neither is redundant, and **both iterate `resolve`** — `panes/plotter_canvas` issues one `add_image_quad` per *visible* cell (a 200×200 map at 32px is a 40-megapixel buffer; recompositing it per frame is not frame-thread work) and `plotter/render.py` composites the whole map for an export. **A blend mode is the one thing the draw list cannot express**, so it is the one case where the canvas asks the flat renderer for a composite, and that exception is bounded rather than open: `plotter_canvas.BLEND_PREVIEW_PIXELS` (4 megapixels) is the frame budget, deliberately far below `render.MAX_RENDER_PIXELS` (a gigabyte of RGBA), because the two answer different questions — the second is arithmetic absurdity for a renderer that runs on a task thread, the first is what fits in a frame. Above the budget, during an open stroke, or with no GL context, the canvas draws the cell loop instead and **says on its face** that the modes are not being shown; a silent fallback would be a canvas disagreeing with its own export with nothing on screen to say why. Two failures made that budget necessary rather than tidy: `render_map` *raises* past its own ceiling and nothing wraps a pane draw, so the first frame that drew a multiply layer on a large map took the window down; and the composite was cached on `history.head`, which a stroke does not move, so painting on a blended map showed nothing until the mouse came up. The cache lives under `plotter_textures.blend_slot` — inside the tab's texture prefix, so `release_doc` sweeps it; parked outside it, a closed tab left a whole-map RGBA array alive for the life of the process. They agree because both take orientation from `gid`, placement from `tile_rect` and inherited state from `scene.resolve`, and both apply the flags **transpose-then-mirror**; what they deliberately disagree about is stated rather than discovered — parallax, which the canvas applies against the view origin and an export treats as 1.0 because a still has no camera to be a fraction of, and the minimap, which resolves like the other two but honours neither a pixel offset nor an image layer, because it is one pixel per *cell*: a fraction of a cell has nowhere to land and an image layer has no cells at all; the quad form is what makes the diagonal flip drawable at all, since a transpose cannot be expressed by swapping a UV pair but can by permuting four corners. **A map's projection is a document property and one function owns it.** `plotter/project.py` maps a cell to a pixel rect and back for all three of `PROJECTIONS` -- orthogonal, isometric and oblique -- across four `RENDER_ORDERS`, and `MapDoc` defers to it, so `panes/plotter_canvas` and `plotter/render.py` take placement from one answer for the same reason they both take orientation from `gid`. Oblique is the dialect one: it is an affine shear of the square lattice by `skew_x`/`skew_y`, exactly invertible like the other two, and Tiled has no such orientation. A ground tile's image is the diamond's *bounding rect*, so the drawn quad stays axis-aligned in pixel space and the transpose-then-mirror permutation is untouched -- isometric is a change of placement, not of rasterisation, which is why both lattices shipped together rather than the second being a rewrite. The inverse is exact rather than a hit test: the diamond lattice is the image of the square lattice under an affine map, so flooring the transformed point **is** the cell, and a click on a shared corner lands in exactly one of them. Draw order is `column + row` ascending for isometric and row-major for orthogonal, because row-major is not monotone in isometric screen depth -- cell `(width - 1, 0)` sits in front of `(0, 1)` -- which is invisible for flat ground tiles and correct on the day something overhangs. Tiled measures an isometric *object* in tile-space units of the tile height rather than in the projected plane, so `project.object_to_pixels` converts in both directions: a spawn point that made the trip untouched would reopen somewhere else and say nothing about it. **A terrain is derived from the gid, never stored beside it.** A parallel per-cell terrain array would be a second source of truth that `.wmap`, the undo stack and every Tiled round trip would each have to keep honest; a 47-case blob set makes the tile id answer alone (`Tileset.terrain_of` is `local // 47`), so a terrain stroke is nothing but a `TilePatchEdit` and a cell hand-stamped with the plain stamp tool joins the field for free. **Precedence is list position and membership is "ranked at or above me"**, which is what makes a three-terrain junction resolve to one tile per cell with the outline always belonging to the higher-precedence terrain; the cost, stated in `terrain.py` so nobody quietly "fixes" it, is that a terrain's edge art cannot vary by what is on the other side. **And a stroke is one step.** `MapDoc.begin_stroke`/`stroke_write`/`end_stroke` is `inker.Document`'s stroke session over gids -- the live array is written with no history and one patch over the dirty box is pushed at release -- because `_apply` runs every frame the button is down, so a stamp dragged across forty cells used to push forty steps and forty entries against the byte budget. It is a second write door into a layer, which is why `end_stroke` is idempotent and the canvas closes any open session on the first frame the mouse is not down: a session left open is a document whose cells are ahead of its head. **`begin_object_edit`/`place_object`/`end_object_edit` is the same mechanism for objects rather than a second one**, and it is closed at the same three chokepoints -- `undo`, `redo`, and that frame-top sweep -- because a drag that moves an object across the canvas would otherwise push one `ObjectPropsEdit` per frame. `place_object` **refuses outside an open session** rather than falling back to the undoable `set_object`: a silent fallback would leave a caller who forgot to open one pushing a step per frame, and the only symptom would be a suspiciously deep undo stack, which is a bug found by counting rather than by failing. A gesture that moved nothing pushes nothing, the no-op rule reaching a drag; a session whose object was deleted mid-drag commits nothing, because the remove is its own step and already on the stack.

**An AI tile sheet is one job, and the grid is imposed rather than requested.** `kind="tile_sheet"` at `stage="tilesheet"` draws **one** SDXL generation laid out as an 8x8 grid of sixty-four *different* tiles, slices it on rectangles decided before the call, reduces each cell to exactly the tile size and quantizes the whole sheet to one palette. One generation is the feature, not an economy: tiles dispatched separately would not share a palette, a light direction or a style, which is the whole difference between a tileset and sixty-four unrelated pictures. Four rules hold it together. **The cells are drawn into a canny guide**, `pipelines/tilesheet.render_guide` -- white boundaries on black, handed to the ControlNet directly (running the detector over line art returns two lines where the guide means one) -- and the slicer then uses *those* rectangles, never rectangles re-detected from the returned pixels: a generation whose art drifted a few pixels still slices identically, where seam-finding would turn one mis-registered sheet into sixty-four differently-sized tiles. The isometric guide inscribes a diamond as well as the rectangle, because the rectangle is where the *slicer* cuts and the diamond is where the tile's top face goes. **There are exactly two guide shapes for three views**, and the third is why: a `three_quarter` sheet gets the plain rectangle a `top_down` one gets, because the tilt is in the art and not in the lattice. Two interior marks were tried and both were *obeyed* and both made the sheet worse — the model drew the mark as a dark stripe rather than a change of plane, and flattened every cell towards one tile doing it (`docs/measurements/2026-08-21-three-quarter-guide.md`). What carries the view is `_VIEW_CLAUSE`, this module's own words under `TILE_SHEET_VERSION`, never `prompt.TILESHEET_TEMPLATE` — that template serves the prompt preview too and a change to it bumps `PROMPT_VERSION`, which re-keys the findings corpus. **The view is one axis, not a lattice crossed with a camera**: `tile_height` derives the lattice from it, `orthogonal` reads as `top_down` (`normalize_view`) so every row and sidecar written before the vocabulary widened still opens, and the stored key stays `projection` while its vocabulary grows. `view` is deliberately **not** in `VECTOR_PARAMS`, as `projection` never was. **The grid is 8x8 and is not a control**: 1024/8 = 128 is the true art resolution of one cell (the pixel-art LoRA draws ~8px art pixels at 1024), so a denser grid asks for detail the model cannot draw and a sparser one wastes the frame. **The reduction is a two-stage partition** -- `reduce_cell` box-means down to the art resolution (a factor capped at 4) and centre-samples the rest -- carried over with its measurement from the ground path this replaced (`docs/measurements/2026-08-17-ground-reduction.md`: a pure box mean flattened contrast to 0.55-0.78 of the art resolution). It runs *per cell* rather than as one resize of the whole image, because the tile size need not divide the cell and a whole-image resample would let one output pixel straddle two cells -- a tile carrying a sliver of its neighbour, which on a tile sheet is the one defect visible in every map painted with it. **A sheet's own view is what the map it lands on is checked against** — `plotter_tilesets._recorded_sheet` reads `sheet.json`'s `projection` as well as its cell size, and `_VIEW_LATTICE` maps it to a lattice. Two views map to one lattice and that is the point: a 3/4 tile is square and sits on the orthogonal grid exactly as a top-down one does, so a 3/4 sheet on a top-down map must never be reported as a mismatch. Only the diamond differs, and a diamond sheet on a painted square map parks a `SheetLattice` — the fourth confirm-popup variant, and the only one about the lattice rather than the cut — because a map's projection is fixed once anything is painted on it. On an *unpainted* map the sheet brings its lattice with it instead, through `land_tileset`'s projection arm, which was kept for exactly this door. **An isometric sheet is generated at 2:1** (`text2image.generate(size=...)`, the only caller that overrides the spec's square frame), because generating it square and squashing it afterwards returns sixty-four ellipses. **`input.png` is the finished sheet**, not a reference: it is staged and renamed (the served-name rule), `sheet.json` is written last as the completion gate, and a cancel publishes nothing -- except `ref.png`, which the *door* wrote and is an input the user supplied rather than something the run produced. A tile sheet **is** rerollable (the whole request is a prompt, a size and a seed) and is **not** remeshable; `rerun_job` refuses the second by name and re-runs the door's weights check on the first, because a reroll days later can meet a models directory the user has since pruned. **And the form's own gate is per-arm, not per-output**, which is the trap the *other* way round from "never offer an action the service refuses": the tile grid is the one output whose door is not `create_job`, so `settings_2d.validate` skips its conditioning, base-model and style-LoRA checks for that arm alone -- a Generate button disabled because `control` is set without a `ref_path` is a refusal about a field `create_tile_sheet` never reads, and it is reachable rather than theoretical, since `control` is persisted while `ref_path` is VOLATILE. The sprite arm is deliberately not exempt: its first step *is* an ordinary reference job and reads all four.
**A worker-minted follow-up is validated at the door that mints its *predecessor*, and it must not inherit that predecessor's settings.** The Create form's Sheet/Sprite output is the rig checkbox's shape a second time: `create_job` stores a `sprite_sheet` block on an ordinary reference job and `_maybe_queue_sprite_sheet` turns it into a `sprite_synthesis` row once `input.png` exists. Two rules fall out of that shape and neither is optional. **The follow-up's options and weights are checked in `create_job`** (`_check_sprite_sheet`, delegating to `service.sprites`' own constants), because the row it mints never passes through `create_sprite_synthesis` — so a bad layout or a missing ControlNet discovered at mint time would be a refusal an hour later, against a row the user never submitted, after the reference they did submit had already been paid for. The check runs *once* per submit and its result is carried down to the params write; it walks the registry and stats the weight files, which is a cost to pay once and a pointless one to pay twice. `rerun_job` holds the same door, keyed on the *block* rather than on the kind — a reroll is the other way in, days later and against a models directory the user may have pruned since, and the tile sheet's reroll guard is the same rule for the arm that does have a kind of its own. **And `base_model` is pinned, not carried across.** A synthesis is a pixel-art restyle whose identity is the SDXL-fitted pixel-art LoRA, which is why the door writes `SPRITE_BASE_MODEL` rather than a choice; inheriting the character's base looks like keeping its style and is the opposite — a character drawn on `flux_klein` would queue a sheet whose base cannot take the LoRA, and `_sprite_synthesis`' tolerance would draw both candidates bare and say so only in the log, from a button that offered two pixel-art sheets. It would also mean the door admitted the follow-up against one base's weights and VRAM while the job ran on another's. The queue may not import `service`, so the constant is restated in `_q_jobs` with that argument beside it and pinned by a test. The seed is the one thing that *is* derived from the predecessor (`spritesynth.candidate_seed`), because this worker has no RNG and should not grow one — and because it makes the whole two-step chain reproducible from the single seed the user can lock.


**A new job kind is a sweep of the stage-keyed tables, and the sweep is the invariant.** Nine of them decide what a row is offered, and every one falls through *silently* for a kind it has never heard of — which is a button that refuses, or no button at all, rather than a failing test. The tile sheet's list, in the order a row meets them: `service.files.ready`/`unready_reason` (whether `input.png` may be served — gated on the sidecar, because the row is marked failed *after* the artifacts are on disk), `files.derived_2d_for` (which 2D exports the stage can produce — `()`, deliberately: every entry in that set is a whole-frame transform of one picture, and a sheet is sixty-four), `progress.PHASES_TILE_SHEET` (CON-02: an unregistered kind falls back to `PHASES_IMAGE`, whose only real phase is `trellis`, so an unknown phase maps onto the *whole* bar — and this kind is the sharpest case, since with a single generation there is nothing to average the fallback's errors out and the bar finishes before the reduction starts), `state.primary_action` (the card's one button) and `state.card_kind` (which filter bucket), `panes/library._remeshable` and `palette.rerollable` (the context menu and the command palette, which must never offer an action the service refuses), `create_stages.IMAGE_STAGES` and `create_stages.available("mesh", …)` (a sheet is a picture, so it opens in Create — and `promote_to_model` still refuses it, so the Mesh segment has to say so in that function's own words), `widgets.STAGE_BADGES`/`ARTIFACTS_TILESHEET` and `thumbs.thumb_glyph` (what the card looks like), and `service.validation.DERIVED_PARAMS` (`sheet_report`, the one thing the worker records about the artifact). Four of the nine were missed when the *previous* grid kind landed and each failed differently; they are written out here so the tenth table is added to a list rather than found. Troupe swept eight of the nine and missed `progress`: `charsheet` fell back to `PHASES_IMAGE`, the Blender render's closing `on_progress(1.0, …)` landed in an undeclared phase, mapped onto the whole bar, and the never-regress creep pinned it at 100% for the entire pixel-art tail — CON-02 for the fourth time, and the reason `tests/test_progress.py`'s sweep is parametrised over every multi-pass kind rather than written out.

**A row with `params["source_job"]` is a product of another asset, and opens where that asset is.** Six kinds are: `rig`, `sheet`, `pixel_sheet`, `sprite_synthesis`, `charsheet` and `retexture`. Each is minted by `Store.create` without a `stage=`, so it keeps the column default `'model'` — `Store.set_stage` exists and is called from nowhere in `src/` — while producing no mesh at all; each writes its artifacts into the *source* job's directory and never creates its own; and nothing any of them writes is in `service.files.LISTED`, so `job["files"]` is empty. Those three facts together made "open this row" a blank screen: `create_stages.stage_for` routed `stage='model'` to Create's Mesh stage, whose `_STAGE_SECTIONS` entry holds neither the sprite panel nor the sheet panel, so a finished sprite sheet toasted "finished" and its **Show** opened a row with no mesh, no files, no thumbnail and eight disabled download buttons — while the sheet itself sat one directory over. **`studio/asset_open.route` is the only place that answers "where does this row open"**, `stage_for` is reached only from it (a scan test pins that, as it pins that every section key `request_open` asks for is some header's registered `persist_key`), and `FOLLOWUP_STAGES` joins the stage-keyed sweep above — a seventh follow-up kind that is not added to it re-acquires this bug silently. The rule is the one `service._jobs_lifecycle.dependent_jobs` already states from the other side: "a rig or a sheet is a separate job row whose artifacts land beside the `model.glb` they were made from". Note that a new *stage* value would not have fixed it: the empty `files` list is a true statement about these rows, and the fix is to stop pointing the UI at them rather than to make them lie in the other direction.

**Every automatic follow-up's queue attempt is guarded on the stage that can produce its input.** `_maybe_queue_sprite_sheet` takes `stage != "reference"`, `_maybe_queue_charsheet` takes `stage != "model"`, and `_maybe_queue_rig` had no such guard — which was survivable only while a skipped attempt was silent. Troupe's door sets `params["rig"]` on the *reference* row (it describes the mesh the gate will promote that reference into, not the reference itself), so the unguarded read fired on every Troupe reference completion, found no `model.glb` — there cannot be one before the user approves the drawing — and, once `followups.persist` made the skip durable, wrote a permanent false "the generated mesh artifact is missing" onto every character anyone started. `followups.APPLICABLE_STAGES` is the reader-side half: a record about a follow-up a row could never have had is the fingerprint of a missing guard, not evidence, and is filtered on read rather than migrated out of the params blob. **A follow-up must also exclude its own kind, and `_maybe_queue_charsheet` excluded only `charsheet`:** `JobStore.create` defaults `stage="model"` and `create_rig` never overrides it, so a rig row carrying a `troupe` block passed the guard, looked for `model.glb` in the *rig job's* own directory and wrote a permanent false "The generated mesh artifact is missing." (fixed 2026-08-23). **There is a fourth now** — `_maybe_queue_sheet_after_rig`, the second half of "send this mesh to Troupe" — and it is guarded on `kind == "rig"` plus its own marker, `troupe_sheet`. Two markers because they are two claims: `troupe` on a reference means "run the whole chain, human gate included", `troupe_sheet` on a rig means "render this sheet once the rig lands". It is nested rather than flat so `VECTOR_PARAMS` — an allowlist of flat settings — cannot pick it up, and the *mesh* row is deliberately not stamped: `rerun_job`/`promote_to_model` copy everything not derived, so a marker there would make the next Remesh silently spend a rig and 256 rendered cells.

**The VRAM accounting and the handoff decision are two halves of one rule and must agree.** The direction of any disagreement is what matters: an estimate that says "coexist" while the worker keeps trellis resident *under*-charges and OOMs, so the accounting must never be the optimistic half. `vram.estimate` prices `tile_sheet` with `TRELLIS_GIB` added under coexist and `_tile_sheet` hands `_acquire_t2i` a real `Conditioning` (the grid guide is always attached), so a running trellis is stopped and the charge is conservative — the same shape `sprite_synthesis` has, and the safe direction. The trap is the other way round: a caller that means "conditioned on nothing but its prompt" must pass `cond=None` *explicitly* rather than take `_acquire_t2i`'s `_ALWAYS_CONDITIONED` default, because that default fires `_needs_handoff`'s conditioning term, stops a warm trellis for ~20 s and buys a full ~8 GB cold reload on the next mesh job, while the admission that let the job in said the two would coexist. The tile sheet's IP-Adapter is the same rule at the level of one term: it is optional on this kind, so the estimate gates its encoder on `params["ip_adapter"]` and the door writes that key only when a reference is actually attached.

**A kind that samples N times spends one `t2i_sample` window across all N, not one per pass.** Feeding each generation's own `i`/`n` straight to `_t2i_step` walks the phase's whole span once per pass — so pass one finishes at the top of the window and the never-regress creep floor holds it there for the rest of the job (CON-02 from the other side). The two answers in the tree: `_sprite_synthesis` gives each pass its own phase in `PHASES_SPRITE`, which is exact but only works for a count fixed in advance; and a kind whose count the *form* chooses has to do the arithmetic instead, reporting pass *k* of *N* at step *i* of *n* as `(k*n + i)` of `(N*n)`. `_tile_sheet` needs neither — it samples exactly once, so the mapping is the identity, which is why its step helper is three lines.

**A selection is view state, and so is the brush that a transform moves.** `state.select` is a normalized inclusive cell rect on the *mode*, beside `brush` and `selected_object` -- Tiled's selections are not undoable either, and an `Edit` would mark a saved map dirty because somebody dragged a marquee across it. It is **clamped at use** (`selection_in`) and never when stored, because a resize is undoable and the marquee is not: a rect trimmed at store time could not grow back when the resize was undone. What it constrains goes through one place -- `_constrained` cuts a tool's region with `tools.clip_region` -- so the tools go on computing what they would do and one decision says what may land. **The flood is the exception and takes its bounds up front**, because a fill clipped afterwards is free to leave the selection, run around the outside, come back in, and have the trip trimmed away invisibly; masking the match makes the wall real. **A blob re-fit is the other exception**: the terrain tool, the terrain fill and erase-of-a-terrain-cell all reach the eight cells around what they touched by design, and cutting that to the marquee leaves the ring drawn as though it still bordered something that is gone -- a broken field is worse than a tool reaching one cell past a selection. One control one owner holds here as everywhere: the canvas owns the marquee gesture, the tools pane owns which shape the Shape tool fills, and the layers pane owns the lock. **A lock blocks content edits and nothing else** -- renaming, hiding, opacity, reordering and deleting the layer all stay available, copying from a locked layer is allowed while cutting is not, and selecting an object on one is allowed while removing it is not -- and it is enforced at the studio layer, never in the engine, because `write_region` must go on working or an undo could not put back what was there before the lock.

**Packwright's packer is deterministic, and that is a contract rather than an accident.** `studio/packwright/` reaches outward exactly seven times — `layout.py` → `pipelines.sheet`, `tsxout.py` → `plotter.tsx` **and** `tilegrid.tileset`, `document.py` → `studio.undo`, `compose.py` **and** `wpack.py` → `plotter.pngio` (the one RGBA-to-PNG encoder, which four byte-identical copies used to spell), `sources.py` → `tilegrid.tileset` again (`frozen_rgba`, one immutability rule rather than two copies of it) — and every one is the same argument: reach for the module that *owns* a definition rather than restating it. `pipelines.sheet` owns the atlas ceiling (and `trim.py` is pinned against `measure_trim` by a parity test, which is the whole licence for a second implementation existing), `plotter.tsx` owns the `.tsx` format with `tilegrid.tileset` owning the `Tileset` that format is written from, and `studio.undo` owns history. **`inker` is deliberately not among them**: `sources.py` takes *a document* and asks it for frames rather than importing the package, because a packer is routinely handed loose PNGs and no document at all. `tests/packwright/test_packwright_imports.py` pins the set of seven exactly, which is the `tests/inker/test_sheetout.py` idiom's third instance. Determinism rests on three things and all three are load-bearing: a canonical input order (`maxrects.order`), positional tie-breaks in the free-rectangle scoring, and **no set or dict iteration anywhere in `maxrects`** — asserted by parsing the module, because iteration order over either is stable until the day it is not. **The layout is derived, never stored**, which is what makes a re-export of an unchanged document byte-identical with nothing to invalidate. `padding >= extrude * 2` is validated in `PackSettings` because two neighbours extrude into one gutter, and the whole pack is offset by one `padding` so a sprite has room on all four sides *including against the atlas edge* — the case an un-offset MaxRects pack gets wrong, which here is an out-of-bounds write rather than a cosmetic artefact. **A repack is a task re-armed by a flag** (`PackTab.pack_dirty`, pumped from the preview pane's draw and cleared *only when the submit is accepted*): the `findings_dirty` lesson verbatim, because `TaskRunner.submit` refuses a key already in flight and nothing else re-arms it. **`read_wpack` refuses at the door**, which is clay/wmap's rule applied a third time: everything that can raise is inside the `with zf:` (a refusal in the gap between `ZipFile(...)` and the block leaves the archive to the collector), the declared unpacked size is checked off the directory before the first read, the source count is refused against `layout.MAX_SPRITES` before a single PNG is decoded, and each member is refused by name if it is not an image or is past `MAX_SOURCE_PIXELS` — a ceiling that *mirrors* `service.validation.MAX_IMAGE_PIXELS` rather than importing it, because this package may not reach for the service layer at all. `PackSettings` is still constructed outside the coercion guard, so its own refusals keep their remedy sentences. **The mode is split the way Plotter's is** — `packwright_mode` decides, `packwright_io` touches the disk, `packwright_state` owns `ctx.state.packwright`, and the first re-exports the other two as plain imports because every pane and every test says `packwright_mode.save(ctx)`. **Every save and export is staged**:
