`packwright_io._write` takes the whole set of files, writes each to a dotfile temporary, replaces them only once all of them exist, and unlinks in a `finally` — plotter's rule with the set-wide staging added, because an export is three files and an encode that raised on the third used to leave the first two sitting on top of the previous one. **`plotter_io._write` was brought up to that shape on 2026-08-22**, having been the weaker version this improved on for the whole time this paragraph claimed otherwise: a Tiled export is `map.tmx` plus a `.tsx` and a `.png` per tileset, and replacing them one at a time left the map pointing at tilesets that were stale or absent. The window between the replaces is stated in the docstring rather than papered over. And because only a `ServiceError`'s text survives `tasks.py`'s classifier, an open that fails and a pack that cannot fit are both wrapped with `invalid_from`: the engine's remedy sentence is the half that says what to do, and unframed it reached the user as "see the log for details". And **the sidecar is TexturePacker's schema, deliberately not `pipelines.sheet.sidecar`** — that format is Warlock's own, versioned, with one writer, and a second one is how `version: 1` comes to mean two documents.

**`studio/tilegrid` is the second shared leaf** — owns the gid word, the sliced-atlas type and the blob collapse; plotter/packwright/inker import it, none owns it, and it imports nothing under `warlock`. The promotion is a pure move (`git mv`, zero content edits) of what were `plotter/gid.py`, `plotter/tileset.py` and `plotter/blob.py`, out from under a single owner and into a leaf next to `studio/undo.py`: two engines had already grown a byte-identical `frozen_rgba` (one in `plotter/tileset.py`, one copied into `packwright/sources.py`) before the second one was collapsed into an import, which is the shape every future duplicate of this vocabulary would have taken without a shared home for it. The packwright→plotter exception shrinks to `tsx` and `pngio` — the only two things packwright still reaches into `plotter/` for, now that the tileset type it builds and the sidecar it slices images against both live in `tilegrid` instead — and the sibling ban `tests/inker/test_inker_imports.py` states is again absolute: `SIBLING_PACKAGES` still names only `clay`, `plotter` and `packwright`, and `tilegrid` is deliberately not among them, because a shared leaf that a fourth pure package (`inker`, from Wave 3 on) needs to reach for is not a sibling engine to ban. The leaf also owns the **separator-band detector** (`tilegrid/slicing.py`): a user's own tilesheet, ruled off with dark lines and cut into slightly uneven cells, is measured rather than sliced blind — and the measurement is **confirm-gated**, offered in a popup at the import door and applied only when the user says so. That does not touch the standing rule that *generated* sheets are sliced on imposed rectangles and never re-detected (`inker/sheetin.py`): this detector runs only at the door where a file nobody in this program made arrives, and it never applies itself. The leaf owns the **blob-role analyzer** (`tilegrid/roles.py`) on the same terms: a 47-case sheet a user drew or a model produced is measured, and the reorder it offers *is* the role assignment (terrain layout is positional and 47 columns are enforced at `Tileset` construction), so it too is confirm-gated at the user-file door with an import-as-plain-tiles button beside it. Neither ratio it uses is corpus-keyed, so neither owes a `docs/measurements/` document — that rule is about constants a change would silently reinterpret already-stored results against.

**The asein refusal ledger moves forward, and it is a ledger, not a rewrite.** Wave 3 chunk 3.5 turned three of `asein.py`'s refusals into readers: the tileset chunk `0x2023`, tilemap layers (kind 2) and tilemap cels (type 3) all open now, where each used to be a bare `ValueError` naming the thing this build could not draw. The invariant that governs the change is the one stated at the top of `asein.py`'s own docstring — **a refusal only ever moves because the reader learned to model the thing, and the refusals that replace it are raised by name** — so the three chunks did not simply stop refusing, they handed their refusal on to the narrower cases underneath them: an *external-file* tileset (its pixels are not in this file at all, so the external-link flag is refused exactly as the whole chunk used to be), a tilemap cel that is not 32 bits per tile (this build's tile-id/flip encoding has nowhere else to live), and a tilemap cel sitting at an offset that is not a multiple of its own tile size (unreachable from Aseprite's own writer, and a tile grid has no partial cell for it to land in). Two more divergences round the set out as warnings rather than refusals: a tileset's `base_index` other than 1 is Aseprite's own display-only numbering and changes no id this reader stores, and per-tile user data — detected by `_Parse.tileset_ud_run`, tracking the spec's own convention that a tileset chunk "could be followed by a user data chunk (empty or not) and then all the user data chunks of the tiles ordered by tile index", so the *first* `USER_DATA` chunk in the run is the tileset's own (the generic warning) and only the *second and later* ones are per-tile — says "the tiles are kept" rather than the generic drawing-level warning, because they are. The remap from Aseprite's *declared* tile-id/x-flip/y-flip/rotate masks onto `tilegrid.gid`'s `GID_MASK`/`FLIP_H`/`FLIP_V`/`FLIP_D` is written as mask arithmetic on the four DWORDs the chunk itself carries, not a cast that assumes the two encodings already agree — numerically the identity on every file Aseprite writes today, but a future Aseprite laying its bits out differently still lands on this build's encoding correctly rather than silently scrambling it. `document_from_aseprite` builds `doc.tilesets` and binds `Track.tileset_uid` the same way `ora._read_tiles` does (Wave 3 chunk 3.4): a tileset survives the import even when nothing draws through it yet, and a `TilemapCel` reached through a linked cel is the same object in both slots, exactly as an ordinary image cel's link already was. **Wave 5 gave the reader a mirror, `aseout.py`, and the ledger is what it writes to as well as reads from.** Every field order in the writer is the one the reader consumes, every constant is imported from `asein` rather than restated, and the automated gate is a round trip through the reader — `tests/inker/test_aseout.py`, plus the committed corpus at `tests/inker/fixtures/aseprite/` (`tests/inker/test_aseprite_corpus.py`, `tests/inker/fixtures/aseprite/FIXTURES.md`), which pins the same fixed-point property across eleven documents rather than one. What a round trip through *our own* reader cannot prove — whether real Aseprite agrees — is the corpus's own stated debt: no fixture there has yet been authored in the app, the same gap the Tiled corpus closed one fixture at a time, and `docs/COMPAT.md` names it as the wave's user-owed manual pass -- carried in `TODO.md` -- rather than a claim this repository can make on its own. `aseout` reaches into `..tilegrid.gid` for the four flip/rotate masks a tilemap cel chunk declares — the one outward import the writer needed, argued in `tests/inker/test_inker_imports.py`'s `OUTWARD_IMPORTS` entry for `("aseout.py", "warlock.studio.tilegrid")` rather than restating four literals a drift could silently disagree with.

**A tilemap cel's pixels are a materialization, and `refs` plus the bound tileset are what is authoritative.** `TilemapCel` subclasses `Layer` rather than growing a case onto it, so `Animation.cels` keeps its `dict[..., Layer]` typing, linking a cel links its `refs` for free (two slots, one object), and every existing reader of a stack — the compositor, the flatten cache, the ORA writer, onion skin, thumbnails, the sheet exporter — needs no new branch: what it sees is ordinary RGBA. Only the code that *writes* one has to know it is one, and there are exactly two doors. `Document.place_tiles` is the refs door — tile-unit origin, a 2-D `uint32` patch clipped to the grid, every id in it refused by name (through `GID_MASK`) if the bound tileset does not have it, and all of that validated **before** `_ensure_cel_for` so a refused write cannot leave an autovivified cel behind with no undo step to take it back out. An out-of-range ref *drawing* as blank is right for one that became stale under a shrink and wrong to accept at the door, because the cell starts drawing a real tile the moment the tileset grows past that number — and `_commit_tilemap_patch` is the funnel's tilemap branch, where a *pixel* write on such a cel is routed back onto the tileset (or thrown away; see the behaviour rule below), and therefore also the only place the grayscale and palette constraints can reach one, since the divert returns before the funnel's own. Every `_apply_*` hook ends the same way: rebuild the frozen tileset or write the refs plane, re-derive the pixels the change covers, invalidate. `_rematerialize_tileset` is **the one walk** and it walks `anim.unique_cel_layers()` rather than the visible stack, because an Auto-mode edit made while frame 3 is on screen has to reach frame 7's cel too, or that frame goes on showing the tile as it was until something else happens to touch it. The tileset itself is edited by **frozen-replace, never in place** (`grow`/`shrink`/`with_tiles` each hand back a new `Tileset` over a new array), which is what lets both the dedup index and the pane's texture cache hold the pixel array beside their answer and compare it with `is` — not `id()`, the recycled-address bug class this repo has already been bitten by. `materialize` treats a local id the tileset does not have as 0 rather than raising out of the middle of a draw, since a stale ref surviving a shrink should draw as an absent tile and not crash the frame; `oriented`/`canonical` are an exact inverse pair, transpose-then-mirror, the same order `plotter/render.py` uses, which is what makes the two engines agree bit-for-bit on all eight square symmetries and what makes a pixel edit on a *flipped* placement land on the atlas the right way up. **Geometry is taught one op and refuses the rest**: a canvas resize translates by whole cells, so `_tile_regrid` pads or crops the refs plane and re-derives the pixels (refusing a non-tile-aligned offset by name rather than rounding it, because rounding would move the drawing somewhere the user did not ask for), while flip, rotate, scale and crop still refuse a document holding a tilemap layer outright — each would have to turn every cell's flag bits by the eight-symmetry algebra, and that algebra is not written. The same reasoning is why merge-down, matte, lift, cut, the range ops and the three whole-document colour conversions refuse *at the door*: each writes pixels before the funnel's refusal could fire, so a guard at the funnel alone would leave a half-written layer behind an exception. The test-only guard the whole tile suite is built on is `materialize(refs, tileset, size) == pixels` after every op.

**`tiles.json` is versioned, written only when there is something to write, and fails alone.** The ORA member that carries the tile structure — the tilesets as PNG strips, each cel's refs plane, and the track bindings — is absent from an archive whose document has no tileset in it, exactly as `animation.json` is absent from a still drawing, so a document that never touched the feature produces byte-identical files to the build before the feature existed (which is what the determinism pins assert). It carries its own version, and a broken, unreadable or unknown-version member costs **tile structure only**: the layer PNGs are the picture and they are read regardless, so such a file opens as ordinary raster layers with a warning rather than not opening at all. The stated cost, in the manual as well as here: an older build of Warlock — or Krita, or GIMP — opens the file, shows the picture correctly, and *drops the member on save*, which is the same trade the animation timeline already makes and is said in the same words.

**Manual/Auto/Stack is view state, and a toolbar toggle cannot dirty a document.** `Document.tile_behavior` decides what a *pixel* edit on a tilemap cel does to the tileset under it — manual never touches it and re-derives the cell from the tile it already names, auto edits that tile in place (so every placement of it, on every frame and every layer, follows through the one re-materialization walk), stack only ever appends — and it is **never serialized**: not in `tiles.json`, not in the ORA, not in any file this app writes. Dirtiness is `history.head != saved_head`, so the rule is mechanical rather than aspirational: the toggle assigns the field and calls nothing, no step is pushed, and the head does not move. Manual mode's revert is **silent at the document** and the sentence is derived at the pane: `_commit_tilemap_patch` has no channel to report through and gains none, because "a gesture happened" is not something a document knows — what it does know is that a reverted commit pushes no step, so `panes/inker_canvas` banks `history.head` at the press and says so once (through `toast_once`, and only for the gesture kinds that actually paint) when the head ends where it started. Auto mode's two exceptions are part of the same contract rather than options: content that already exists elsewhere in the atlas re-points instead of duplicating, and local id 0 is the required-blank slot and is never written.

**An exported map or atlas is an ordinary asset, and `params["authored"]` is what makes it reopenable.** `map.wmap` and `pack.wpack` follow the `paint.ora`/`build.wblk` precedent exactly: absent from `MEDIA` and `LISTED`, never served, gone with the job directory for free, and with no staleness rule (nothing rewrites one behind its document). The marker on the row is the part that is new and it exists for a specific reason: reopening has **no fallback**, unlike *Open in Clay*, which imports `model.glb` when there is no `.wblk` — so the library has to know whether the source is there *before* offering the menu item, and it must answer from the cached row rather than a stat because the menu is built on the frame thread. `authored` is an *input*, like `built`, so it stays out of `DERIVED_PARAMS`.

**Saved workspace layouts reverse "no docking", and only that half.** Wave 5 gave the three-column skeleton names (`layout_skeleton.Slot`/`Column`, the per-workspace tables in `skeletons.py`, the saved arrangements in `layouts.py`) and one renderer (`layout.column`), so a user can reorder and hide the panes inside a column and keep the result under a name. The half of `layout.py`'s header that is **overturned** is the sentence saying there is no docking; the half that **survives and is reaffirmed** is the one about width -- the sidebars are still one of three named widths, a form still has one width that reads well, and nothing here makes a column draggable. Saying which half survived is what makes this a decision rather than an erasure.

General docking is refused with the cost named: `layout.fit`, `layout.centre_width` and `rail.expanded_fits` are arithmetic over exactly two sidebars and one centre, so under a dock tree `test_the_expanded_rail_gives_way_before_the_columns_do` becomes *unstatable* rather than merely failing -- and floating panes need imgui's docking branch, whose whole persistence story is the ini file `main` disables and `tests/conftest.py` polices. What a layout captures is **arrangement, visibility and shares**, and deliberately not the sidebar width, the rail, the UI scale or the theme: a workspace switch that collapsed your navigation is the same class of surprise as the eighteen-failing-tests incident. The settings keys are **top-level** (`workspace_layouts`, `active_layout`, `profiles.py`'s pattern) rather than inside `settings["layout"]`, because `Settings.set` replaces a whole dict and `test_a_save_writes_no_width_key_at_all` asserts that dict's exact key set. The blob is sparse and per workspace -- absent means the built-in, so there is no migration -- and carries **its own `v`** while `settings.VERSION` stays 1: a bump there discards the whole file, and an unknown higher `v` here is kept verbatim and listed greyed rather than coerced, because an older build rewriting a newer layout is the one way this feature can destroy something. `layout_skeleton.reconcile` runs on every read and is **never written back**: a retired pane is dropped, a new one is inserted **after its last already-placed predecessor** rather than appended (or every pane added after a user saved their layout would arrive at the bottom of a column), and a launch that changes nothing does not rewrite the file. Structurally a layout can only reorder and hide -- never delete -- and a hidden pane is always listed in Settings -> Advanced with one click back, so **there is no reachable state in which a pane cannot be got back**; Settings is the canonical recovery path precisely because it is reachable from the rail in every mode and no workspace layout can touch its single-column composition. The editor (`studio/layout_edit.py`, `Shift+W`) is **not** a `panes/` file, so it trips neither the help-button gates nor the no-imgui-controls guard, and it draws nothing inside any pane: `layout.column` records rects into `layout.FRAME_PANES` and one full-viewport overlay renders the handles, which puts `section_blocks`' double-split corruption out of the picture by construction. Splitters are suppressed while it is open, because a resize handle and a drag target on the same two pixels is a gesture nobody can aim. The rail has **no footer item for the switcher**: at the 1100x700 floor and 1.75 scale its column already ends four pixels from the window edge, and `test_the_rail_fits_the_resize_floor_at_every_scale` catches the next item that pushes it out -- so the doors are the command palette and Settings, both through the `rail.request`/`rail.take` one-shot the Issues list already uses.

**A symmetry is a *set* of mirrors, closed under composition.** `brush.SYMMETRY_AXES` is `x`, `y`, `diag`, `anti` and `radial`; `brush.axes_of` is the one reader of the field and `brush.compose` the one writer, so a stored `"xy"` still means `x+y` and `brush.SYMMETRY` survives untouched as the legacy spelling -- which is what makes this an addition rather than a migration, with the engine's parameter still a string and every call site that passes `"xy"` unchanged. `_mirror` iterates to a **fixed point** rather than enumerating cases, and that is the load-bearing part: two mirrors applied in turn generate a third transform neither names (horizontal and vertical give the diagonal one for free -- that *was* the hardcoded `"xy"` third point), and horizontal with a 45-degree mirror generates all eight of D4, so composing by hand would mean writing the multiplication table out. Bounded by `MAX_MIRRORS` (64) because this is the hottest loop in the editor, deduped on positions rounded to 1e-6 because a reflection reached two ways is one dab and float equality would draw it twice, and with the radial rotations generated **directly at their own angles** rather than by composing one rotation *n* times -- a 32-way symmetry must not accumulate 31 roundings. The mirrors are applied in `SYMMETRY_AXES` order over a snapshot, which reproduces `"xy"`'s old point order exactly. Every *reader* of the field goes through `axes_of` for the same reason the guide reads `axis_or_default`: `state.symmetry in ("x", "xy")` draws no horizontal guide for `"x+diag"`, which is a line pointing at the wrong place one field later.

**Inker has one bar above the canvas, and it is the context bar.** The canvas's own view row -- Rotate view, Flip view, Center view, the Tiled combo, Wrap 1/2 and the seam figure -- went on 2026-08-23, and what took its place is Aseprite's: brush type, size, ink, dynamics, pixel-perfect and the four symmetry toggles, which is what a hand reaches for between strokes. None of it had room while a second row above it held six things nobody touches twice an hour. Where each went is decided by *kind* rather than by convenience: the verbs are View-menu ops (`center_view`, four **checked** `tiled_*` rows, `wrap_half` -- Rotate/Flip/Fit were already ops with chords and the buttons were a second door), and the seam figure and the `unsaved`/`saving...`/`playing...` word are **readouts**, so they joined the status bar under the canvas where the cursor position, the layer name and the zoom already are. The symmetry group is a `trailing` block rather than `toolbar.Item`s, and that is layout rather than tidiness: `toolbar` draws items before fields, so as items the five landed between the Dynamics glyph and the size slider -- in the middle of the tool's own settings, which is the one place a session-wide setting must not be. The cost is that they no longer collapse into the overflow menu, which is the right cost for five controls already the width of a glyph. They are labelled `H`, `V`, `\` and `/` in ASCII rather than glyphs, for `inker_timeline._STEPS`' reason: `icons.py` is a transcription of a named lucide release and its docstring forbids guessing a codepoint, and the vendored subset has `flip-horizontal` but no `flip-vertical` and nothing for either diagonal.

**Inker's workspace is Aseprite's default arrangement, and the bottom region is a pane like any other.** Colour over the picker on the left, the toolbox over the preview, tiles and Generation on the right, the timeline along the bottom of the centre column. This *reverses* W2.9, which put the toolbox in a fixed 90 px rail on the left and the palette on the right and called it Aseprite's "Mirrored Default"; the argument that moved it -- "the far side of the canvas from the hand" -- is answered by the fact that Aseprite ships the other way round, and the rail cost the toolbox its heading, its help button and two clipped rows (90 px minus 16 px of pane padding is 74 px of content, which fitted two 34 px buttons and the gap between them exactly and clipped everything under them). The tool grid's column count is therefore a function of the pane's width (`inker_tools.columns_for`, floored at 2 and capped at 6) rather than a constant. **The bottom strip is drawn unconditionally, and that is the decision rather than a default.** It was gated on `doc.anim is not None`, which was the bug: `2a56df6` merged the layers panel into the timeline and said "the strip is always available, Tab toggles it", but the animation gate in `_inker_workspace` predated the merge and was never lifted, so from 2026-08-22 a still document had *no layer list at all*, no eye or lock toggles, and `Tab` toggled a flag nothing read. It was then gated on `state.timeline_open`, and that went too (2026-08-23, by user decision): **this strip holds the layer list**, so every state in which it is off screen is a document whose layers cannot be seen or reached, and both of this pane's shipped defects were instances of exactly that -- the animation gate, and the playback wedge where hiding the strip left `tab.playing` true for ever and `tab.busy` with it, so the canvas refused every gesture silently while the Stop button explaining why was off screen. `timeline_open`, `timeline_shown`, `autoshow`, `toggle` and the `Tab` binding are all gone with it; what a user reaches for instead is the **height**, which is the `inker-timeline` share with `inker_timeline.STRIP_H` (150 design px) as its floor. Every headless test passed throughout, because they all call `inker_timeline`'s functions directly -- the missing coverage class is a **composition walk**, and it is pinned by AST over `_inker_workspace` (no condition there may mention the animation or an open flag) the way `tests/test_layout.py` already pins `main.py`. The playback `_tick` is the only caller of `tick_playback` and so is unconditional at the top of `draw`. The strip's height is the `inker-timeline` share with `STRIP_H` as its floor, and it is the one entry in `layout.SHARE_DEFAULTS`: `settings_share` is a *sidebar* proportion, and handing it to a horizontal split would open every document with half its centre column full of layer rows. `layout_skeleton.Skeleton.strips` stays declared and unread -- the centre column is hand-composed because its flags are load-bearing (the positive-height rule below), and moving it into the data model is a refactor with its own risk that this change does not need.

**The sidebars are one of three named widths — never dragged — and the padding is pushed per pane.** `layout.SIDEBAR_WIDTHS` is the table (`narrow` 260, `default` 300, `wide` 360 design px; M106) and `layout.SIDEBAR_W` is the one in force — module state set by `layout.set_sidebar`, exactly as `tokens.SCALE` is, because eight call sites read it directly and threading a `Layout` through all of them would put the measurement in eight places instead of one. An unknown stored key falls back to `default` rather than raising, since it comes out of a settings file. A *change* to it eases (UX.md Phase 1), which is why `set_sidebar` takes `animate` and why `layout.tick()` is called **once, before any pane reads the width**: the construction path and every headless caller need `SIDEBAR_W` correct with no frame loop at all, so they snap, while the Settings option seeds the motion key and lets the tick slide it — and a half-eased width read by the left column against a settled one read by the right would be two columns disagreeing about the same frame. `layout.SIDEBAR_W` is the width every left and right pane takes and `layout.centre_width()` is what the centre reserves for the right one; a *draggable* splitter is refused by `layout.py`'s own header, so the only proportions the user still drags are the sidebars' internal horizontal splits — and those are **keyed per split**, not one number for the whole app: `Layout.share(key)`/`set_share(key, v)` over `settings_shares`, with the legacy scalar `settings_share` as the value an unkeyed split *starts* at, so an existing profile opens on exactly the layout it had. One number behind all of them meant Inker's toolbox handle silently re-split Create, Clay, Plotter, Packwright, Troupe, Review and Inker's own right column — six screens the user cannot see while dragging, which makes a handle a setting. `Layout.save` writes exactly the surviving keys (`settings_share`, `settings_shares`, `sidebar` — the width's *name* rather than a number, so a stored value can never be a size this build does not offer — and `rail`) — `Settings.set` replaces the whole dict, so the stale `sidebar_w`/`inspector_w` a settings file still carries die on the first save rather than being half-honoured. Every pane goes through `layout.pane` (`pane_child` is the older begin/end shape, kept for callers outside the migration boundary and for the tests that still drive it), which pushes `window_padding` around `begin_child` **and pops it immediately after** — window padding latches at begin, so this scopes it to the pane while a popup or tooltip opened inside keeps the theme's own. Pushing 5 px globally in `theme.py` instead would shrink every modal in the app. **The push is only half of it: both entry points must also pass `ChildFlags_.always_use_window_padding`**, because Dear ImGui zeroes the window padding of a *borderless* child — which every pane here is, since `theme.apply` removed the borders globally — so for as long as the flag was missing the pushed `PANE_PADDING` bought nothing and every pane's content sat flush against the column edge. That it was a missing *flag* rather than a missing push is why it survived: two panes fixed it locally where it showed worst (`panes/app_settings`, `panes/landing`), and both of those draw into `##content` rather than through `layout`, so they keep their own flag and are not double-padded by this one — a local fix in the two loudest places is exactly what stops the global one from being looked for.

**Inker is a mode, not a takeover, and its engine is pure.** `studio/inker/` is a layered raster editor -- blend arithmetic, canvas-sized layers with stable uids, typed undo edits, 8-bit selection masks, a cached brush stamp, gradients, geometry, OpenRaster I/O -- and nothing under it may import imgui, moderngl, pygame or `service`, which is what makes every rule about pixels assertable headlessly. **Undo is dirty-rect patches addressed by layer uid**, never by index, so an undo issued after a reorder still lands on the layer the edit was made to; only whole-canvas geometry copies, and it redoes by *replaying* rather than storing a second copy. Three consequences of addressing by uid are load-bearing and each was once wrong. A snapshot restore (`restore_snapshot`, `_replay`) copies pixels but **keeps the uids** -- an undo restores the document's state, not a set of new layers, and minting fresh ones strands every patch recorded beneath it. A replayed op that *creates* a layer (`flatten_layers`) draws its uid once via `layers.new_uid` and closes over it, because replay must be a pure function of the document and minting is the one part that is not. And a patch can land *below* the active layer, so `Document.invalidate` takes the written layer's uid and repairs the cached `_below` composite over the same rectangle -- otherwise the pixels change and the composite, the flatten and every save derived from it keep showing the old ones. Patches also own their pixels: `PatchEdit` copies any array that is a view, because a slice of a full-canvas buffer reports its own tiny `nbytes` while keeping the whole base alive, and that number is what eviction is driven by. **Dirty is a comparison against `history.head`, not a flag** -- `Document.rev` counts changes and an undo is a change, so a rev-based check would call an undone document unsaved forever. **The content lock refuses tool-level writes at the engine ops themselves** -- `begin_stroke`, `write_colour` (which is where the fill and all six shapes end up, the clicked three included), `gradient`, `begin_filter`, `lift`, `paste`, `float_pixels` (the text stamp's delivery route, and every generator after it), `layer_from_selection`'s cut half, `delete_selection`, `begin_layer_move` and `merge_down` (either participant) -- each refusing *before* it mutates anything, and `LayerOps.write_locked` is the one comment that owns where the line is drawn. Below those doors **undo writes raw pixels** through `PatchEdit`, deliberately: a lock switched on after an edit must not wedge the history that already holds it, and a lock that silently swallowed a Ctrl+Z would be far worse than one that lets a user reverse work they locked afterwards. `commit_floating` is deliberately **not** refused -- a float outlives lock toggles and every save, geometry op and structural op commits it first, so refusing there would not protect the pixels, it would leave a buffer that can neither land nor be saved. Document-scope operations (geometry, `apply_matte`, the palette rewrites, `flatten_layers`) apply regardless, and so do rename, hide, reorder and delete: the lock is about what gets *painted*, not about whether the layer may be managed. **The timeline range ops sit on the applies-regardless side too, deliberately**: nothing in `_doc_ranges.py` consults `track.locked` -- `fill_range`, the flips, turns and shifts, `filter_range` before any of them, and `commit_floating_range`'s per-cel replay all write onto locked tracks (what they do read off the track is `track.alpha_lock`, which is a different lock about a different thing) -- so the same fill that `write_colour` refuses on a locked layer lands when it arrives through the range menu. The argument is the shape of the gesture: a canvas tool needs the door precisely because a stroke lands on whatever the active layer happens to be, while a timeline rect is an explicit bulk selection -- the user named those cells, locked ones included, the way a document-scope geometry op names the whole document. The one refusal in the ranged-transform path is the *lift* that starts it, which is `float_pixels`' existing door on the active layer, not a range check; the replay writes to every target cel unasked. The Aseprite parity programme's Wave 2 deviation #8 was the citable record of this posture; the argument is restated in full above, which is why retiring that file lost nothing. It is the sixth `Track` property, so the four places that copy the list (`Track.of`, `Track.props`, `Animation.placeholder`/`layers_for`, `Document._ensure_cel_for`) have to agree, and it persists as a `warlock-content-lock` attribute written **only when set**, so an unlocked document's `stack.xml` is byte-identical to what this writer produced before the attribute existed. And **the eraser cuts alpha**: the old `erase_color` became `Document.matte`, applied once at flatten, so what a photo exports onto is a property of the document rather than of every stroke. **That matte is a choice, not construction-time state** -- it was decided once by `matte_for` at every import door and nothing revisited it, which made the default ("a photo opened here is still a photo when it is saved") a documented feature with no control and no way out: an opened JPEG exported every erased area as opaque white while the canvas showed a checkerboard there. `set_matte`/`toggle_matte` push a `MatteEdit` like any other op (Sprite > Flatten transparency onto white, ticked from `Op.checked`), refusing on a background document because `flatten` ignores the matte once one exists; `apply_matte` now carries its clear *inside* its `CompoundEdit`, appended last so `reversed()` restores the colour before the pixels, where before an undone cutout lost it for good. It persists as `ora.MATTE_ATTR` on the `<image>` root, and is the **one** `warlock-*` attribute written unconditionally: the setting is tri-state and absence has to keep meaning "infer" for Krita files and older builds, which no write-only-when-set rule can express -- the ORA byte pin was re-measured for it. `.aseprite` and sheet imports carry no such field and go on inferring. **The canvas backdrop draws the matte** (`inker_canvas._backdrop`, a solid quad over the four *rotated* corners rather than the screen AABB the checkerboard is content with), which is arithmetically the same picture as compositing it under the stack for one draw call -- doing it in `inker_textures.composite` instead would cost a second full-canvas buffer per tab, invalidated per dab, on the frame thread. Sheet, GIF and PNG-sequence exports still ignore the matte and that divergence is guarded by a test: an atlas cell is composited over whatever is behind it in the game. **A floating buffer holds the history entry it owns**, not a flag saying it owns one (`FloatingBuffer.lift_edit`, with `.lifted` derived from it): a lift cut a hole and pushed a patch, a paste took clipboard pixels and touched no layer. Cancelling reverses *that* entry, via `UndoStack.revoke`, and never simply "the newest step" -- a buffer floats for as long as the user leaves it floating and every selection op pushes a step meanwhile, so the lift is routinely no longer on top, and reversing the top instead dropped the lifted pixels, kept the alpha-cut and destroyed an unrelated edit. Revoking out of order is sound only because everything that writes pixels calls `commit_floating` first, so nothing above the lift describes the region it restores. It is reversed *unredoably*, because the user asked for the lift not to have happened and leaving it on the redo stack lets Ctrl+Y replay the alpha-cut with no buffer left to restore. `Document.undo` returns as soon as it cancels, since the cancel *is* the step; `paste` clears the redo branch itself, having pushed nothing that would. **A ranged commit replays the gesture, never the buffer.** The preview only ever showed the active cel -- the buffer holds that cel's pixels and no others -- so `commit_floating_range` re-cuts the lift's `source_box` with the lift's mask out of *each* cel, runs the recorded `flips` in order, renders one scale-then-shear-then-rotate and composites at the dragged offset; pasting the buffer into every slot would make N copies of one drawing. Which is why the buffer records the recipe and not just the result (`source_box`, `flips`, `resample`), and why a **paste** falls back to the plain commit: it has no source rectangle to re-cut out of anybody else. The render core is `selection.render_transform`, a module function with two callers -- the live buffer and the per-cel replay -- because two spellings of it would mean a transform that previewed one way and landed another. The active cel replays like every other, its lift revoked by identity first so the replay reads pristine pixels; and `flip()` re-renders with the buffer's own `resample`, or a flip pressed mid-gesture silently turns a nearest-neighbour transform Lanczos-blurred. **A step that changes nothing is not pushed**: `select` returns early when before and after are both `None` (subtracting from an empty selection) *and* when they are equal non-empty masks (a marquee redrawn over itself, Select All twice, feather 0), because dirty is a comparison against the head and a no-op step made a saved document ask to be saved again. `LayerStack.remove` **shifts** `active_index` for a removal below it rather than only clamping it, or the active layer silently becomes a different layer; and `LayerAddEdit` reports its layer's `nbytes` as its cost, because an undone add holds the only reference to a full-canvas buffer and a zero cost hid it from the byte budget entirely. **A stroke recomputes its region from the pre-stroke copy and one coverage number** (`StrokeState._resolve`), which is what stops overlapping dabs piling up, and there are now exactly **three** exceptions, each reading the *live* layer instead: `_filter` (blur and smudge, which are accumulation tools -- the second pass is supposed to blur more), `_shade` (which decides what it writes from the pixel it may have written itself a dab ago), and **an image-brush stamp**, which takes the same live read-modify-write path because a captured tip lays down a different colour per pixel per dab, so recompute-from-`before` would have to keep the whole stroke's colours -- a canvas-sized RGBA plane, four times what the coverage buffer costs, to defend against a compounding the path does not have. The overlap rule survives that: `_place` writes not its dab's alpha but the increment from the greatest coverage the stroke has already laid, so a half-transparent tip dragged over itself is still half-transparent and the picture does not depend on the frame rate.

**The canvas view turns in quarter turns, and that is the decision rather than a first instalment.** `inker_state.ROTATIONS` is `(0, 90, 180, 270)` with `flipped` beside it, both on `PaintView` and neither ever reaching a pixel — there is nothing to undo and nothing to save. The narrowing is what keeps the pane's overlays as they were: a free angle makes the grid stop being two families of axis-aligned lines, the marquee preview stop being a rect and the transform box's handles stop being squares, and a half-done version silently misplaces all of them, which is worse than not having it. A **quarter** turn maps an axis-aligned image rectangle onto an axis-aligned *screen* rectangle, so `_box` (min/max of the four transformed corners) is exact rather than conservative and every `add_rect` in the pane is still drawing the shape it names. Three rules follow. `inker_state.basis` is the orientation alone, **separate from the zoom and orthonormal**, which is why `ants.dash_segments` needed one optional matmul in `_points_at` and no change to any of its arithmetic — every distance in it is an arc length in canvas space and turning does not stretch. `to_image` inverts the basis by **transposing** it, exact for all eight orientations. And every image goes through `_blit`/`add_image_quad` rather than `add_image`, whose two-corner form can only draw an upright rectangle; the checkerboard is the one deliberate exception, drawn upright in screen space because it is the surface the page sits on rather than part of the page. The failure this invites is not a crash but one overlay left computing `origin + x * zoom` for itself — right at rotation 0 and a quarter turn out everywhere else — which is why `_grid` derives its visible index range by inverse-transforming the window's corners instead of dividing a screen offset by the zoom, and why the ants' loop-box cull transforms its four corners rather than scaling the box.

`studio/inker_mode.py` is the only layer that knows about jobs and task threads: no dialog and no encode ever runs on the frame thread, which is why saving is a state (`InkerDoc.saving`) rather than a call that returns, and why a failed save has to clear it (`on_task_failed`) or the tab stays read-only. Two rules follow from `saving` being that state. **Every save commits the floating buffer first, and reads `history.head` after** -- the encoders walk the layer stack and a floating buffer is in no layer, so an uncommitted paste was written to no file while `mark_saved` called the document clean; and the commit pushes a step, so a head captured before it saves the document against a head one behind, leaving the tab dirty however many times it is saved. **And every control that changes the document is gated on `saving`** -- the canvas, the keyboard path (`_MUTATING_CTRL`), the layers panel, `inker_bridge`'s canvas ops and resize popup, and `inker_tools`' selection and transform sections. `write_ora` flattens, writes `stack.xml`, then walks the stack writing one PNG per layer, so a rotate or an undo landing mid-write produces an archive whose parts disagree about the canvas size. A stroke is the deliberate exception: pixels are written in place, so the worst case is a file catching a stroke mid-flight. That gate is now `InkerDoc.busy`, not `saving`: **playback is the second reason the document may not be restructured**, and it is the same list of controls for the same reason, so it is one question asked once rather than two flags nine panes have to remember to `or` together. The layers panel was named in this list and did not have it until 2026-08-22; `tests/test_inker_save.py` now walks `_toggle_all`, `_track_row`, `_row_menu` and `inker_menu._properties_popup` for the call, beside the four surfaces it already walked. **The two drag gestures refuse outright rather than draw disabled** -- `_reorder` and `_drag_toggle` check `tab.busy` themselves, because `begin_disabled` does not reliably stop a drag-and-drop source or a press-and-drag from registering, and a reorder is the exact write the two passes over `doc.stack` cannot survive.

**Hiding the timeline strip stops playback.** `_tick` is the only caller of `tick_playback`, which is the only thing that advances the playhead or ends a clip -- so with `draw`'s `timeline_open` early return above it, Tab (Aseprite's own binding) left `playing` True forever and `busy` with it, and the canvas then refused every paint gesture silently while the Stop button explaining why sat inside the strip that had just been hidden. Both halves are fixed and both are pinned: `_tick` runs above the early return, and `toggle` calls `stop_play` on the way out.

**One gesture is one undo step, in the layers panel too.** `set_layer_props` pushes its own edit per call that changes something, so the header's "hide every layer" cost a ten-layer document ten Ctrl+Z and a drag down the eye column cost one per row crossed. Both go through `Document.set_layers_props` now -- one `CompoundEdit`, `set_range_props`' only-rows-that-change rule, and its `was=` pre-image for the drag, which writes the rows live so the column follows the cursor and asks for its step once, on release. `set_range_props` itself is *not* reusable for this: it refuses outright when `anim` is None, and a still document is where the header row is most often clicked.

**`background` and `reference` are undoable.** They are persisted document state -- `.aseprite` layer-chunk flags `0x08` and `0x40`, `.ora`'s `warlock-background`/`warlock-reference` -- and they are deliberately outside `TRACK_PROPS`, because that allowlist is what `set_range_props` writes through and "make every selected row a background" is not an operation. So they have their own `LayerFlagEdit`, which writes the track and the materialised layer under the one shared uid. `to_background` folds the matte into pixels and clears it, and pushes all three parts as one `CompoundEdit` (`PatchEdit` + `LayerFlagEdit` + `MatteEdit`): with only the pixel patch, undo put the pixels back under a layer still calling itself a background, and the matte colour -- which nothing else in the document holds -- was unrecoverable.

**An animated document is a sparse grid, and `Document.anim is None` is exactly the editor that existed before it.** `studio/inker/animation.py` holds `Track` (a *layer's identity* — name, opacity, visibility, blend, the alpha lock and the content lock, held once) x `Frame` (a duration, and nothing else) with `cels: dict[(track_uid, frame_uid), Layer]`, and `Document._materialize_frame` rebuilds `stack` as the current frame's view — so the panes, the tools, the floating buffer, the selection, the `_below` cache and the compositor all go on seeing an ordinary `LayerStack`, because that is genuinely what they are handed. Properties live on the track because storing them per cel makes hiding a layer an edit to every frame it appears in, and a frame with no cel has nowhere to put the answer at all. Six rules under that are load-bearing. **A link is two slots holding one object** — there is no link table and no "linked to" field, so an edit to a linked cel shows on every frame it occupies with no propagation step that could be forgotten; the price is that every whole-grid walk must go through `Animation.unique_cel_layers()`, or a rotate turns a three-frame background three times and every frame agrees with every other about the wrong answer. **An absent cel is an absent key**, and materialising fills the gaps with placeholders sharing one *read-only* transparent plane: shared because ten tracks over twenty empty frames at 2048 square is 3.2 GiB of nothing, read-only because sharing is only safe while nothing writes and a stray write would otherwise appear in every empty cel at once with nothing to say where it came from. So **every write path calls `_ensure_cel_for` first** (keyed by uid, not "the active layer", because committing a floating buffer writes to whichever layer it was lifted from), the autovivified cel rides into the *same* `CompoundEdit` as the patch, and a write that changes nothing takes the cel back out and pushes nothing — the "a step that changes nothing is not pushed" rule, extended one level. **`Track.continuous` changes what that cel starts as, and is deliberately not one of the six.** A continuous track autovivifies a *copy* of the nearest earlier occupied cel on the same track (walked backwards from this frame, so a gap in the middle does not send the copy to frame zero), pixels and index plane together or the two describe different pictures from the moment the cel exists. A copy and not a link, where Aseprite links: linking would make the first stroke on the new frame edit the old one too, which is the opposite of "carry it forward and change it", and linking afterwards is a verb the timeline already has. It is outside the six-property copy-down list and outside `Track.props()` because those say how a cel is *shown* and this says what autovivification *writes*, so a materialised `Layer` has no use for it and a copy would only be a second answer to drift from; its `animation.json` key is written **only when true** (the `repeat` rule, so a document with no continuous rows stays byte-identical and the determinism pin holds) and the section version stays 1. There is no `stack.xml` attribute for it, because no foreign editor has a concept to read one into. `set_layer_props` refuses it by name on a still document, where the branch would otherwise setattr it onto a `Layer` nothing ever reads. **The playhead is view state**: `set_current_frame` mirrors `set_active_layer` exactly and pushes no step, or a document asks to be saved because the user looked at another frame. **`invalidate_all` stamps no frame** — it is about the composite on screen and most of what reaches it changes no pixels, so stamping there looked conservative and was the opposite of a cache: every step of the playhead threw away every frame's flatten and onion skinning recomposited its neighbours on every keypress. The callers that really do change every frame (`_anim_changed`, `apply_matte`, `_map_planes`, `restore_snapshot`) say so with `_stamp_all`. And **`ReplayEdit` carries the grid as slot *indices* into its snapshot list**, so an undo of a rotate gives back one shared object where the document had one — restoring per slot yields two equal copies and silently breaks the link, which only shows on the next stroke when one frame changes and the other does not. A snapshot whose animated-ness disagrees with the document's is **refused** rather than flattened, in both directions and before anything is mutated: history is LIFO, so a document de-animated after the step was pushed is a state nothing can legitimately reach, and silently dropping the grid to restore the pixels would lose a whole timeline to paper over a bug somewhere else. **`merge_down` and `flatten_layers` run across the whole grid**, and the rule that made that possible is the link doctrine read backwards. A merge is **memoised on the pair of cels it consumes** -- `(id(lower), id(upper))` -- so two frames whose lower and upper cels are both the same objects get *one* merged `Layer` and stay linked, and a frame whose upper differs gets its own; nothing has to be told which slots were linked, because the identity of the inputs decides it. Three rules hang off that. The lower's cels are **never mutated in place** as the still branch mutates its layer, because a lower cel may be linked across frames where the upper is not and the merge of one frame would appear in another. The merged layers are minted **once, at op time**, and the `CelSetEdit`s hold them, so a redo returns the same objects rather than stranding every patch above them (`unlink_cel`'s rule). And a slot with **no upper cel gets no edit at all** -- there is nothing to merge, and minting a copy would break the lower row's own links and cost a plane a frame. `flatten_layers` goes through `_replay` instead, because every cel in the document is replaced: its link partition is computed **structurally at op time** as groups of frame indices sharing the same tuple of cel identities, and that partition, one uid per group and one track uid are all drawn once and closed over, since replay has to be a pure function of the document and the `id()`s will not survive the snapshot restore that precedes a redo. Durations, tags and the layout are untouched -- flatten is a statement about the layers. `CelSetEdit.pinned` therefore takes `charged`'s three readings rather than a bool: several steps legitimately name one merged cel, and charging each of them would evict real history for memory that exists once. Two more callers had to learn `unique_cel_layers`'s rule the hard way and are named here so a third does not. **`duplicate_layer` copies once per distinct cel**, not once per slot — per slot gave the duplicate three independent frames where the original had one shared one, the link silently gone and three times the pixels, and it only shows on the next stroke. And **`apply_matte` walks the grid, not the current frame's stack**: a cutout describes the drawing rather than a moment of it, so mattes applied to one frame while `_stamp_all` announced that every frame had changed was the worst of both. The byte budget learned the same lesson from the other side — `anim_edits.charged` takes the `id()`s of the cels a removal actually *released*, because a frame routinely holds one cel the grid is letting go of beside another still linked into the frame next door, and a bool is wrong whichever way it is set. Tags are the one part of the grid that is not addressed by uid: a tag's identity is its position, so `TagsEdit` snapshots the whole list (a name and three numbers, and no pixels, so it costs the budget nothing) and one type covers create, rename, retime, loop and delete.

**`animation.json` is the record; the frame groups in `stack.xml` are the picture.** An animated `.ora` gains one member carrying the grid — durations, track properties, which slots hold which cel and which slots share one — and `stack.xml` becomes an interop *projection* of it, one nested `<stack name="frame:NNNN">` per frame with frames 2+ written `visibility="hidden"` on the group **and** on every layer inside it, so Krita, GIMP and this reader's own flattening fallback show frame 1 rather than the whole clip stacked on itself. The two cannot be derived from each other (XML has nowhere to put a duration and no way to say "these two entries are the same cel, not two equal ones"), so one is authoritative rather than the truth being split. The JSON stores **indices, not uids** — uids are minted per process and mean nothing in a file — and a linked cel is one `data/cel{n}.png` referenced by several slots, decoded once on read and *shared*, which is the whole of how a link survives a round trip. Any way `animation.json` fails to make sense on its own terms — a version it was not written against, an index outside the grid it declares, a cel naming a PNG the archive does not hold — logs and falls back to the flat read, and it fails the **whole** grid rather than skipping a bad cel: half a timeline is harder to notice than none of one. It deliberately does *not* cross-check the JSON against `stack.xml`: the XML is the projection, so a disagreement is the picture being wrong about a document the record describes correctly, and throwing the grid away over it would lose the animation to fix the picture of it. A frame entry with no `duration_ms` gets `DEFAULT_DURATION_MS`, not `clamp_duration`'s 1 ms floor — a clip a hundred times too fast still plays, which is exactly why the floor was the wrong fallback. The accepted, unrecoverable cost is that an older build opens such a file, sees frame 1, and writes it back flat. **Every member carries a fixed `_EPOCH` stamp**, so `.ora` joins `.wblk`/`.wmap`/`.wpack` as byte-reproducible and two saves of an unchanged document are identical — it was the odd one out, stamping every member but `mimetype` from the wall clock. That one member had always carried this exact stamp (a bare `ZipInfo`'s default) and is the first thing every ORA reader touches, which is the evidence the format tolerates it; `_member` restates `ZIP_DEFLATED` and `external_attr` because `writestr` takes those off a supplied `ZipInfo` rather than from the archive. **Slices ride a third member, `warlock.json`, written only when the document has some** — so an archive from a document with no slices is byte-for-byte what this writer produced before the member existed, which is what the determinism pin asserts and what keeps the addition invisible to every reader that has never heard of it. It is separate from `animation.json` because slices live on still documents too, and because that member fails whole-grid on purpose while a malformed slice must never cost a document its timeline (it fails whole-*member*: the file then opens as a drawing with no slices on it, which is what every other ORA in the world already is). Its per-frame keys are the one place the two addressing schemes meet: a `Slice.keys` dict is keyed by **frame uid** at runtime, because that is what survives a frame being inserted or reordered, and by **frame index** on disk, for `animation.json`'s reason — uids are minted per process and mean nothing in a file. A key whose index falls outside the grid, or any key at all when there is no grid to resolve against (a still document, or an `animation.json` the reader has already rejected), is dropped with a line in the log rather than guessed at.

**There are three colour modes and one funnel, and `_commit_patch` applies whichever one the document is in.** `Document.color_mode` is `"rgb"` | `"indexed"` | `"grayscale"`, and the three constraints are snap / index-resolve / luma. Tools go on painting RGBA in all three, which is what keeps this the only place any of them has to be known: every undoable write arrives here -- strokes, fills, shapes, gradients, filters, pastes, floating commits -- and each constraint is applied *before* `after` is read, so the recorded step is the pixels the document ends up with and undo-then-redo is stable. **A range op cannot use the funnel and uses its sibling instead.** `_commit_patch` pushes a step per call, and a rect of five cels is one Ctrl+Z, so `Document._patch_edit_for` applies the identical constraint in the identical order to a region already written and *returns* the edit for the caller's `_push_range` -- or `None` for a write the mode resolved to nothing, with the materialisation already put back. `filter_range` and `fill_range` both go through it; the one that did not was a genuine bug, writing raw RGBA onto an indexed cel and leaving `layer.indices` describing the picture from before it, which is silent until the document is saved, undone or reordered and then the indices win. The structural ops were that same bug several times over — selection cut and lift, merge down and `_merge_tracks`, `apply_matte`, and every path that mints a layer inside an indexed document (flatten, paste-as-layer, `add_layer`) — and each now goes through `_patch_edit_for` or mints its plane via `_resolved_plane`; `tests/inker/test_indexed_structural_ops.py` holds that door. The **permutations** (`flip_range`, `rotate_range`, `shift_range`) deliberately do *not*: they are exact and shape-preserving, so they take `_map_planes`' index discipline one cel at a time -- permute the index plane and re-derive the pixels, never re-resolve from the mapped colours, or two slots holding one red collapse onto the lower and nothing on screen changes to say so. They also act on whole cels with no mask, because a selection is a *weight* and a weighted permutation would have to invent what goes in the part it did not move. A **single-layer** permutation needs the same discipline and a door of its own: `offset_layer` (Wrap ½) rolls the plane itself, so it cannot use `_commit_indexed_patch`, whose stated contract is that the caller wrote *pixels* and the plane is still the pre-write one. It went through it anyway — which read the already-rolled plane as both sides of the step, found them equal, and pushed **nothing**: the roll was permanent, the history head never moved, so `InkerDoc.dirty` reported the file saved and the next Ctrl+Z undid the *previous* edit. `Document._commit_permuted_indices` takes the `before` crop the caller took beforehand and records it. And both range writes read the alpha lock off the **track**, not off the cel: properties are copied down only when a frame is materialised, so a cel two frames along carries whatever the lock said the last time the playhead was on it.

**Palette-constrained RGB is a mode of its own and is kept, not replaced.** `"rgb"` with a `palette` set is today's document bit for bit: the pixels stay RGBA, `indexed.snap` constrains each write in **straight** (non-premultiplied) RGB for `transform._resample`'s reason, alpha is never snapped and a fully transparent pixel is never touched -- a palette constrains colour and not opacity, so a soft nib is legal and simply bands, and rewriting a transparent pixel's dead RGB would make a no-op write look like an edit and push a step for a gesture that changed nothing. It is kept because documents written before true indexing existed legally contain soft alpha, which one index per pixel cannot represent; entering indexed mode is therefore always an explicit, undoable conversion, and `is_indexed` (the mode) and `is_palette_locked` (the constraint) are two questions, because conflating them is how a pane comes to offer a transparent-index marker on a document with no index to mark. `snap` works over a region's *distinct* colours via a packed `np.unique` rather than over its pixels, which is what keeps a 40-frame 2048-square clip inside a frame.

**In an indexed document the index planes are the record and `pixels` is the materialisation, and re-materialisation has one owner.** `Layer.indices` is an optional `(H, W)` uint8 plane; `pixels` stays `(H, W, 4)` and stays *valid*, so the compositor's nineteen blend modes, the frame-flatten cache, texture upload, GIF and sheet flatten, thumbnails and every native kernel change by zero lines -- the RGBA invariant is demoted from "the record" to "the projection", not weakened. What that buys, and the only thing it buys, is **identity**: two palette slots holding the same colour are one colour in an RGBA plane, so a recolour repaints both, a reorder cannot know which pixels belonged to which, and an `.aseprite` file with a duplicate swatch loses the distinction the moment it is read. `Document._rematerialize`/`_rematerialize_all` are the only writers of pixels-from-indices and they invalidate by default, because writing pixels and not saying so shows the *old* colours in the exported PNG, the thumbnail and the ORA's `mergedimage` while the canvas is correct. `check_materialized` is the test-only assert that the two agree, called after every op in the indexed suites rather than from the model -- an assertion inside the model would be either always on (a whole extra materialisation per stroke) or off in the build that matters.

**Palette identity is per-slot, never per-colour: a remap is an index table and nothing is ever re-inferred.** `index_plane.py` is the whole of the arithmetic, pure numpy, importing nothing under `warlock`. A slot move or a sort becomes `tables_from_order` + `IndexRemapEdit` (two 256-byte tables applied per distinct plane); a merge becomes `merge_table` and undoes by replay, because two slots becoming one is not invertible. Flip, quarter turn, crop and canvas resize hand `_map_planes` an `index_fn` and permute the plane exactly, so duplicate slots survive geometry. **A smooth scale is the one stated exception** -- a resample invents colours that were never in the table, so there is no permutation to apply and the indices are re-resolved from the result. `_map_planes`' default is that honest path rather than the exact one: an op that could have been exact and forgot to say so loses identity and stays correct, where the opposite default hands a 2-D array to `indexed.snap` and raises out of the middle of a rotate. **A reorder carries the transparent index with it**, because that index is a *position*: a sort that left it behind would point it at whatever slid into that slot and turn one swatch into holes across every frame at once, with no visible cause.

**The transparent index materialises as alpha 0 and is never a nearest-match candidate.** It is the only way a pixel becomes a hole, and it is reached only through alpha: `resolve` thresholds at `OPAQUE_THRESHOLD` (128 -- the midpoint, because what arrives at the funnel is routinely a soft edge, and thresholding at 1 fattens every drawn edge while 255 eats one). A palette routinely carries a black in slot 0 as its transparent entry and a *drawn* black elsewhere; if slot 0 could win a distance comparison, every black the user painted would silently become a hole. `lut` zeroes that slot for the materialisation while leaving the **stored** entry alone, because that colour is what a `.aseprite` writer has to put back -- a projection, not an edit. The slot cannot be removed (it has no colour to be near and the document would have no way to be transparent), a merge never lands on it, and a fresh cel autovivifies a plane full of *it* rather than of zero, which is only the same slot by coincidence.

**Indexed palettes cap at 256, refused by name.** It is the cap PNG-P, GIF and `.aseprite` all share and the reason a plane can be uint8 at all -- which is in turn what makes the ORA representation a plain mode-P PNG rather than a format of our own. Truncating would produce a file three formats agree is valid and that has quietly lost colours.

**The P-mode layer PNGs are the record; `palette.gpl` and `stack.xml` are projections.** The `animation.json` doctrine's third instance, with the same stated forward-compat cost. An indexed `.ora` writes each layer as a PNG mode-P image -- `PLTE` is the table, `tRNS` its per-entry alpha, the pixel bytes are the plane verbatim -- so the archive is self-describing and old and foreign readers degrade perfectly: `_decode` ends in `im.convert("RGBA")`, which honours `PLTE`+`tRNS`, so a build that has never heard of index planes opens the file with the right pixels and lands in palette-constrained RGB via `palette.gpl`, and Krita and GIMP decode a P-PNG natively. The cost, unrecoverable and the same one `animation.json` documents: an old build that *re-saves* writes RGBA planes back and index identity is gone. Pillow's PNG optimiser is deliberately off -- it drops unused palette entries and renumbers the plane, which is exactly the identity this member exists to preserve. `warlock.json` gains an **additive** `color` block written only when the mode is not `"rgb"`, so `WARLOCK_VERSION` stays 1 (the bump rule -- "a reader could get it wrong in a way it cannot detect" -- is not met) and every RGB document's archive stays byte-for-byte what this writer produced before. A malformed block costs the mode metadata and never the file, and a document declared indexed whose planes are RGBA has its indices re-inferred rather than being refused. The transparent slot's `tRNS` byte is *forced* to zero on the way out, so the reader canonicalises its stored alpha back to opaque rather than reading the writer's own requirement as user data -- which is what makes save-open-save a fixpoint. **Crash recovery is honest for free**, because the journal payload is `ora_bytes(doc)`.

**A link shares its index plane because it shares its `Layer`.** No new machinery: a link is one object in two `Animation.cels` slots, so there is exactly one plane to share, and every whole-grid walk goes through `unique_cel_layers` as it always did.

**Index-crop undo re-derives RGBA through the *live* lookup table.** `IndexPatchEdit` records index crops (a quarter of the bytes, which is not the reason -- an RGBA patch cannot restore slots, so one undo would collapse duplicate identity) and re-materialises through whatever table the document has by then. That is correct by history *ordering* rather than by luck: a palette change is itself an edit on the same stack, inside the same compound, so the table is always the one that belongs beside those indices. Recording a table on the patch would be a second answer to the same question. `ColorStateEdit` carries `(mode, palette, transparent_index)` as one fact, because a mode restored without its palette is a document declaring itself indexed with nothing to index onto.

**Grayscale is a constraint over RGBA storage, and that is sound because all nineteen blend modes preserve grayness.** Aseprite stores `(value, alpha)`; this keeps `(v, v, v, a)` and enforces `r == g == b` at the funnel with the shared Rec. 709 luma. Three reasons, in order of weight: grayscale has no identity problem, so a second representation buys nothing (`v` is exactly recoverable from either); a two-channel plane would fork the compositor, the caches, the uploader, the native kernels and five export formats -- the same list the indexed design was shaped to avoid forking, and here with nothing on the other side of the trade; and the blend modes preserve it (the channelwise ones trivially, the HSL family because a grey has zero saturation), so even the *composite* stays grey. That last one is a parametrised property test over `BLEND_MODES`, not prose: without it, grayscale would be a constraint the storage enforced and the compositor broke.

**The GIF exporter is told the slots where it can be.** It takes the document's table verbatim when it has one -- slot *n* is the same colour in every frame, which the adaptive per-frame quantise cannot promise -- and falls back to quantising rather than truncating a palette too long for the format. `sheetout.index_plane_one` supplies a frame's exact index plane when the flatten *is* a cel's materialisation, and **the test of that is equality, not structure**: the structural walk picks the candidate, and what decides is whether materialising it reproduces the flatten byte for byte. Without a plane, a duplicate colour falls to the **lowest** slot, so two exports of an unchanged document stay byte-identical. **The session swatch row is a different thing and stays one**: it is persisted in settings, it belongs to no file, and adding to it changes no pixel. `InkerState.set_fg` is the one door that loads the brush, because what has to be right is the *clearing* of `fg_slot` -- a clear that lives at three of the four sites is a brush that goes on claiming a slot the user moved away from.

**Frames are cached by their own stamp, and the cache is LRU where the history is not.** `Document.frame_flat` is what onion skinning asks for two or four of per drawn frame and playback one of per tick, so it is a lookup; it is keyed on `_frame_stamps[frame_uid]` rather than on `rev`, because `rev` moves for any change anywhere. Eviction is least-recently-used and bounded by `FRAME_CACHE_BYTES` — deliberately *not* `UndoStack`'s oldest-first policy, which keeps the newest steps because that is what history means, and which here would evict the frame a playback loop is about to come round to. The entry just stored is never evicted, or a canvas larger than the budget caches nothing and recomposites on every draw. `panes/inker_textures.frame_texture` sits inside the existing `inker_tex:{uid}:` naming so `release_doc`'s prefix sweep collects a fifty-frame tab's textures without knowing they exist.

**Playback ticks from the timeline pane's `draw`, and it never moves the document.** There is no per-mode update hook, so the pane is the only thing that runs every frame — the `motion.py` idiom — and it calls one function in `inker_mode` whose arithmetic is the pure `animation.advance`. `tick_playback` deliberately does not call `set_current_frame`: that re-materialises the stack and recomposites the whole canvas sixty times a second to show a picture the frame cache already has, so `play_index` lives on the tab and the canvas draws that frame's cached flatten, with stopping the one moment the document catches up. `advance` accumulates time rather than deriving an index from a clock, because a duration is per frame and a clip is therefore not a rate; it steps in a **`while`**, or a 200 ms hitch behind 10 ms frames runs the clip at a twentieth speed whenever the machine is busy; and the caller clamps `dt` to `MAX_TICK_MS`, or a two-second stall fast-forwards through twenty frames at once. **The preview pane is a second playhead on the same idiom, and its whole discipline is what it does not touch**: `panes/inker_preview.py` ticks from its own `draw` into `tick_preview`, keeps its state on the tab (`preview_playing` and friends), and never sets `playing`, `saving` or `set_current_frame` — which is what lets a clip run in the corner while the document is being painted on. It draws `Document.frame_flat` through the same `inker_textures.frame_texture` onion skinning uses, so it adds no GPU state, and it re-flattens the frame under the brush at most four times a second (`REFRESH_SECONDS`) rather than per dab. It ignores `PaintView`'s rotation and flip deliberately: those are aids for drawing, and a preview is a check on the result.

**An Inker clip exports through `pipelines/sheet.py`, which is why that module grew non-square cells.** `Plan.frame_w`/`frame_h` default to 0 meaning "square, i.e. `frame_size`", `cell_w`/`cell_h` are what `width`/`height`/`pack`/`sidecar` now read, and `Cell.as_dict(w, h=None)` defaults `h` to `w` — so every 3D sheet takes character-for-character the arithmetic it always did, pinned by a **byte-identical `json.dumps` of a square sidecar** captured before the change (one string equality covers values, key order and the absence of any new key, which a field-by-field check would miss). A non-square sidecar emits `frame_size: 0` and carries `frame_w`/`frame_h`: zero is a loud wrong answer, where putting the width in `frame_size` would let an importer that reads only that key slice the atlas correctly across and wrongly down. `studio/inker/sheetout.py` builds the cells itself — `plan()`'s job is poses by yaws and its `FRAME_SIZES` check refuses a canvas outright — but emits through `sidecar`, because a second writer of a versioned public format is how `version: 1` comes to mean two subtly different documents. Row wrapping is required rather than tidy (32 frames of a 320px canvas is 10240px across), which is why `check_atlas_size` was extracted from `plan` rather than approximated. The export is split at `snapshot`/`compose` and the split is a thread boundary, not tidiness: `_write` gets away with encoding the live document because the encoders only read, and flattening a clip does not — `frame_flat` fills and evicts the frame cache and `layers_for` copies track properties down onto cels, which is what the onion-skin draw is doing to the same dicts. So `snapshot` runs on the frame thread at click time and `compose` takes no document at all. **It is the first module under `studio/inker/` to import outside the package**, and `tests/inker/test_inker_imports.py` pins the whole outward set exactly, so the next one is a decision rather than a discovery; the invariant is about staying assertable headlessly, and `sheet` is stdlib plus a lazy Pillow import, while the inverse placement would make `pipelines` — which runs inside worker and Blender processes — depend on `studio`.

**The twenty-four divergences from Aseprite are decisions, and the numbering is a citable API.** Inker is measured against Aseprite because that is the program its users already know, so the places it deliberately does *not* match are worth writing down as decisions rather than leaving to read as gaps — and because `studio/inker/asein.py`, `textstamp.py`, `inker_mode.py`, `inker_state.py` and six tests in `tests/inker/` cite them **by number**, the numbering below is a public interface: append to it, never renumber it. (The list moved here from the deleted `INKER_UPDATE.md`, which is what those citations used to name.)

1. **Cel opacity** — opacity is a Track property; per-cel skipped (user decision).
2. **Colour modes** — three modes, two storage strategies. *Indexed* is real index-plane storage and matches Aseprite; *grayscale* is behaviour parity with a storage divergence (`(v, v, v, a)` rather than `(v, a)`, sound because every blend mode preserves grayness, and lossless in both directions). Palette-constrained RGB is a fourth state Aseprite has no name for — see #19.
3. **ICC / colour management** — none; sRGB-assumed bytes end to end. Re-examined in Wave 1 and held: honest colour management is a pipeline property, not a feature — the native blend kernels (whose bar is bit parity), the dither metrics and five export formats all assume sRGB bytes, and the only cheap step (carrying an assigned profile blob) buys nothing a pixel-art user can see.
4. **Workspace docking / layouts / split views** — fixed three-width sidebars, one canvas per tab.
5. **Zoom/hand as tools** — wheel-zoom + space/middle pan; toolbox slots not spent on navigation.
6. **Background layer type** — `Document.matte` applied at flatten instead; eraser always cuts alpha. **Retired (2026-08-25), in place and not renumbered.** A real background layer arrived with the editor shell (6.5): `Layer.background` is the flag, `Document.has_background` is the question, the bottom-of-the-stack rule is enforced by the reorder ops, and an eraser on a background reveals the colour under it rather than cutting a hole. `matte` survives as the *stand-in* for a document that has no background layer, which is what `flatten` consults it for. The divergence is a decision that was later taken the other way, so the entry stays as the record of both.
7. **Continuous-layer flag** — links are explicit (+Copy/+Link, two-slots-one-object). **Retired (2026-08-25), in place and not renumbered.** `Track.continuous` exists (`animation.py`) and is a track property like every other, carried through `LayerFlagEdit` and the ORA/`.aseprite` round trips. Explicit links did not go away and are still the mechanism; the flag is Aseprite's own "a new frame on this layer continues the last cel rather than starting blank", which is a different question from whether two slots hold one object.
8. **In-editor sheet packing options** — row-wrapped grids only; real packing is Packwright's.
9. **Convolution matrix & colour curves** — FX staples only (user decision).
10. **Per-tool ink selector** — brush modes + replace toggle + layer locks instead.
11. **Pattern fill via bucket** — flat colour only; patterns would arrive with image brushes if ever.
12. **Cel z-index** — track order IS stack order (compositor + native kernel contract).
13. **Eraser replace-colour mode** — the eraser cuts alpha, period.
14. **Timeline element colours / user-data** — tags are name+numbers+repeat; the `.ase` reader drops these as cosmetic chunks with a warning.
15. **Jumble ink** — skipped; smudge covers the intent.
16. **Finite tag repeat does not fall through** past its span (the manual states it).
17. **No text objects/layers** — text rasterizes on commit.
18. **Playback speed is a preview-pane option**, not a document playback mode — `inker_preview.SPEEDS` is a multiplier over the frame durations the document stores, so "what does this look like at half speed" costs no undoable edit to every frame. **Reworded 2026-08-25**: this entry used to claim *play-once* was a preview option too, and it never has been. The preview's second control is its **scope** (whole clip / active tag); play-once is a property of a *tag* here (`Tag.loop`, and `Tag.repeat` for a finite count — divergence #16), which is where Aseprite puts it as well. There is no document-level "play once" and none is planned.
19. **Palette-constrained RGB** — a mode Aseprite lacks: RGBA pixels with a table applied as a write constraint, so soft alpha stays legal. It is what every document indexed before true indexing existed opens as, and the migration story is one explicit, undoable conversion.
20. **No per-frame palettes** — one table per document. Modern Aseprite authors one palette per sprite; per-frame tables are pre-1.0 legacy the format merely tolerates, so lossless interchange for files Aseprite writes today does not need them. They are also architecture-hostile: a linked cel is one object across frames and its materialised `pixels` live on that object, so per-frame tables would force materialisation to per-slot render time and touch the compositor and every cache. The `.ase` reader warns and uses the final table.
21. **Index resolution happens at commit time, with an alpha threshold** — tools paint RGBA and the funnel resolves, rather than the tools being index-native.
