That is what keeps the whole feature tractable: a soft nib, a feathered selection and an antialiased shape all stay legal at the engine and are thresholded on the way in. `Document.paint_slot` closes the one visible gap (which of two identical swatches a stroke lands in), and the studio offers the pixel nibs only in indexed mode because a control promising a feathered edge that cannot deliver one is worse than an absent control.
22. **A single-frame `.aseprite` opens as a still document, and a tag over it is dropped.** Aseprite's own document is always a timeline — even a "still" sprite there is a one-frame animation — where this package's grid only exists once `Document.anim` is set (see the animated-grid architecture note above), with no room for a one-frame animation of its own. `document_from_aseprite` treats a file of exactly one frame as a still document rather than an animated one with a single cel, so a tag declared over that lone frame is warned about and dropped: there is nothing here for it to play across. Adding a second frame afterward turns the document animated again, and a tag can be created fresh from there.
23. **Every `.aseprite` this build writes carries a palette chunk, and a document with no palette of its own gets one derived from its pixels.** Aseprite writes a colour table into every file it saves; this writer omitted the chunk entirely when `doc.palette` was empty, which is what Wave 5's own "Left open / owed" section named. `aseout._derived_palette` closes it, and the shape of the fix is the decision worth recording: the obvious alternative -- writing Aseprite's *own* default table -- would mean reciting thirty-two colours from memory into a file format, which is the unmeasured claim `docs/measurements/2026-08-15-tablet-pressure-spike.md` is the standing precedent against. So every entry written is a colour actually painted somewhere in the document, ranked by pixel count, capped at `MAX_COLOURS` and emitted in colour order; a document with no visible pixel at all gets the single transparent entry, so the rule has no exception to remember. It is derived **below** the indexed refusal and never for an indexed document, because there the palette is what the stored indices *mean* and deriving one would repaint the sprite rather than save it. The reader installs a palette only at indexed depth (divergence 19), so the table is dropped on the way back in and rebuilt identically on the next save -- which is why the derivation has to be deterministic, and is what keeps the corpus's write-read-write fixed point intact. Eight of the eleven fixtures were regenerated when it landed; the three indexed ones did not move, which is the pin that the derivation stays on its own side of the refusal.
24. **The eight-symmetry flag algebra has one home, `tilegrid.gid`, and both editors reach for it.** Flipping or turning a plane of tile cells has to move the arrangement *and* re-flag every cell, or the result is a mirrored layout of unmirrored tiles. The Plotter had the only implementation, in `plotter/tools.py`, and the Inker needed the identical rules to flip and rotate a tilemap layer (the Aseprite parity programme's Wave 3 last open item) — but the engines may not import each other, so the choice was a second copy or a shared home. It moved to the shared leaf both already depend on: `gid.flip_plane_h`/`flip_plane_v`/`rotate_plane_cw`/`rotate_plane_ccw`, with the derivation written out there (`V^v . H^h . D^d` is the normal form; H and V commute with each other but not with D, and that is the whole content of the rules). `plotter.tools` now delegates and keeps its pixel-for-pixel pins against `render.orient` — which is what makes the delegation safe, because those tests exercise the rules through the Plotter's own door. **The bar on the Inker side is the pixels, never a flag table**: a flipped tilemap must draw exactly the flip of what it drew, asserted against the materialised picture, because a hand-derived table is the thing being derived. The refusals that remain moved rather than vanished, by the ledger rule: a canvas that is not a whole number of tiles on the flipped axis (a materialisation crops the grid to the canvas, so the pixel a flip wants at x=0 is one the crop threw away — no flag reconciles that), a quarter turn of non-square tiles, and an unwrapped or sub-tile range shift. **Scale and descale stay refused permanently and for a different reason**: they resample, and a tileset cannot follow a resample — there is no permutation, only a re-cut.

**The Aseprite-parity programme is finished, and this is what survives it.** `ASEPRITE_PARITY.md` was the six-wave master spec (Waves 0-5, 2026-08-17 to 2026-08-19, with every one of its "Left open / owed" items closed on 2026-08-20); it was deleted on 2026-08-20 under the rule every other finished plan here died under, and `git log --diff-filter=D` finds it. Its wave-by-wave outcomes are described in this file and in `docs/ASEPRITE_INTEROP.md`, so the eighty-odd citations that named it were rewritten to name the **programme** rather than the file -- "the Aseprite parity programme, Wave N" -- exactly as the `REDESIGN.md` retirement did, and `tests/test_ux_todo_fixes.py` ratchets the filename back out of `src` and `scripts`. Two of those citations were *already* wrong and were fixed rather than carried: `FIXTURES.md` and `test_aseprite_corpus.py` both said "`ASEPRITE_PARITY.md` divergence 19", but the numbered divergences have always lived in **this** file, one section up. Three things it carried have no other home and are therefore folded in here:

**Its non-goals, which are citable decisions rather than a backlog.** *Pen/tablet pressure input* -- dropped by user decision 2026-08-17; `docs/measurements/2026-08-15-tablet-pressure-spike.md` is the record, its route-B prescription (`SDL_SetWindowsMessageHook` -> `io.pen_pressure`) stays valid if hardware ever arrives, and the velocity taper stands. *ICC colour management* -- divergence #3, re-examined and held. *Per-frame palettes* -- divergence #20. *Per-cel opacity / z-index* -- divergences #1 and #12. *A live cross-workspace tileset registry* -- frozen-snapshot handoff only. *Arbitrary discontiguous layer multi-select* -- track ranges only. Each is a decision with an argument already written down beside the divergence it names; none is waiting for time.

**Its P1 backlog was waved after all, and the rule that said it would not be is hereby reversed.** This paragraph used to read "unscheduled by design -- items are pulled into sessions individually, never waved". The UX refactor's Wave 6 took the whole list in one programme (2026-08-22), and the reversal is recorded here rather than left to make the file quietly wrong. What that wave shipped, and the one decision inside each worth carrying: **inks** are a five-row table (`inker_state.INKS`) whose rows name real `brush.MODES`, with `copy` added as a sixth mode rather than a flag on `replace` because it ignores both the stroke opacity and the dab's antialiasing -- and Lock Alpha is an ink *and* a layer flag, or-ed at `begin_stroke` so neither can switch the other off. **Brushes** gained the line nib and a brush angle rotated in the stamp's own space (a resampled square has a grey rim, which is the thing a pixel nib exists not to have), plus nine `Alt+1..9` slots that are deliberately not persisted, for `state.stamp`'s own reason. **Selection** gained Copy Merged (which reads `flatten(matte=False)`, so a paste elsewhere does not carry this document's background), New Document From Selection through `open_pixels`, Fill and Stroke through `write_colour`, and Shift as a lift-move-drop rather than an array roll -- a roll wraps the drawing round the canvas edge. `.msk` load/save is **refused**: Aseprite's mask file is a format this build cannot read, and inventing a Warlock `.msk` it would reject is the offer-then-refuse shape this codebase rejects. **Shapes** gained rounded rectangles as a *parameter* of the rectangle (hold `C` and roll) and skew as two numbers on the transform row -- the engine had taken a shear since the transform landed and nothing in the app could express one; a movable pivot is not done, being a change to `render_transform` rather than a handle. **Layer types** retired divergence #6 (see the background-layer entry in `docs/COMPAT.md`), made reference layers a type that `write_locked` reads, and made solo write the ordinary `visible` flags rather than being a mode. **Colour** gained a three-handle curve (a spline is a document object none of this app's formats can store), per-channel *weights*, a nine-scalar convolution, harmonies and shades derived from the colour in hand, Select Used/Unused as one histogram read twice, and `remap_palette`, which keeps indices where `set_palette` re-matches pixels. **Onion** made the two tints state, added a falloff exponent, in-front drawing and tag-wrapping, and its span/index arithmetic **wraps rather than clamps** -- a clamp draws the same ghost twice at a cycle's ends. **Playback** gained a constant frame rate that touches no frame. **Sprite/view** gained Trim, Duplicate Document, the pixel grid, layer edges, tile numbers, grid-from-selection and the two 45-degree mirrors; Duplicate View is **not** done, being a second `PaintView` per tab and a change to how the centre pane is composed. **Export** gained Repeat Last Export (`Ctrl+Shift+X`), the packed arrange (the squarest grid, since these frames are all one size), and three JSON meta switches that join the recorded option set so a repeat writes the sidecar it repeats. **Undo history** is a popover that holds no list of its own. What remains P1 and unscheduled: per-layer/cel user colours and metadata (divergence #14 -- needs a model first), pattern fills, source- vs destination-aligned patterns beyond `STAMP_ALIGN`'s two, and a palette-presets browser. The keyboard-remap dialog left that list on 2026-08-23: `inker_ops` carried every op's default binding as data and the shortcut sheet derived from it, which is exactly why it was later-additive, and `panes/inker_menu._shortcuts_popup` is now the surface over it.

**Its rule of engagement, which outlived it.** A refusal only ever moves because the editor learned to model the thing, and the refusals that replace it are raised by name -- stated at the top of `asein.py` and applied to geometry by the Wave 3 close-out. Beside it: the suite is green after every chunk (a chunk that cannot end green is two chunks), and the standing negative control is that **an RGB document's bytes, history behaviour and ORA output never change** -- which is what the ORA byte-determinism pins are for, and what made the derived-palette change (divergence #23) a `.aseprite`-only change rather than a document one.

**A mesh is an immutable CSR array, and the frozen dataclass is a cache key.** `studio/clay/mesh.py` stores a mesh the way a sparse matrix is stored — one flat `loops` array of face corners and a `starts` array of offsets, so face *i* is `loops[starts[i]:starts[i+1]]` — because an n-gon then needs no triangulation to be *stored* (a cylinder cap is one face the user can select, not thirty fans) and Phase 2's extrude and inset append rather than rebuild. Triangulation is a *rendering* concern, produced by `triangulate` on the way to the GPU and never written back. Every array is copied on construction and made read-only, and every op is `Mesh -> Mesh`. That immutability is load-bearing three times over: an undo step that owns its arrays reports its true `nbytes` to eviction (a view of a shared vertex buffer reports its own tiny size while pinning the whole base alive); a snapshot undo is affordable because the "before" mesh *is* the object the document was holding; and `studio/clay_view.py`'s GPU cache keys on `id(obj.mesh)`, so an in-place mutation anywhere would leave the cache holding a live key over stale geometry and the viewport would render the old shape forever with nothing in the data to say why. The transform is deliberately **not** in that key — it is a uniform, so moving an object must rebuild nothing. **The same immutability is what makes `mesh.RenderLayout` sound, and it is the split every drag preview goes through**: `render_arrays` is the composition of `render_layout` (the corner permutation, the shared/split vertex table, the index buffer — everything a moved vertex cannot change) and `render_from_layout` (the positions and the normals — everything it can), and `document.render_plan` memoises the per-material layouts weakly against the mesh, so a drag builds them on its first frame and reuses them for the rest. 368 ms a frame to 92 at 200k triangles (`docs/measurements/2026-08-16-interactive-defects.md`), and the bar on the split is **byte identity** with the unsplit function, not closeness, because the output goes to the GPU where a changed last bit has nothing to announce it. `preview_primitives` returns the *layout's* index buffer rather than re-triangulating, which is the one deliberate difference and is not a difference at all in effect: `scene.GpuPrimitive.update_vertices` leaves the IBO alone, so the old code ear-clip-screened 200k faces per mouse-move to build an array it then discarded. **And hover is not in the element overlay's cache key** (`_view_overlay._SelOverlay`, keyed on `(id(mesh), id(sel), mode)`): it was, so crossing onto the next face released the whole overlay and re-uploaded both the position VBO and the guide wireframe's 2.4 MB index buffer, at mouse-move rate. The hover draws are held in their own buffer list and are the only thing a hover change touches. `edges`, `_next_corner` and `_face_normals` assume an already-validated mesh and go *quietly* wrong rather than raising on a short face, which is why `serialize.read_wblk` validates rather than trusts.

**One conversion out, three consumers.** `clay/document.to_model` is the only thing that turns a `ClayDoc` into a `viewer.gltf.Model`, and the viewport, `viewer/glbwrite.write_glb` and the flat render handed to trellis all go through it — so "what does this document look like" has exactly one answer and those three can never disagree. A hidden object is simply not in the model: `visible=False` means it does not render, does not export and cannot be picked, and one flag enforcing all three in one place is the only way those three stay true together (`clay_view` releases a hidden object's GPU buffers for the same reason). Materials are `viewer.gltf.Material` objects rather than a parallel Clay type — they are already pure data, and `GpuMaterial`, `to_model` and `write_glb` all de-duplicate on `id(material)`, which is why every edit to a palette entry writes a *replacement* and never mutates one in place. `write_glb` refuses a skin rather than dropping it, and omits the buffer entirely for an empty document, because `byteLength` has a minimum of one in the spec.

**Clay's undo is uid-addressed, and its selection is deliberately not undoable.** `studio/undo.py` is the engine the raster editor and Clay share — it was extracted out of `inker/undo.py` for exactly this, and neither half knows what the other edits. Every edit in `clay/edits.py` names its object by uid, never by index, so an undo issued after an outliner reorder still lands on the object the edit was made to; `ObjectAddEdit`/`ObjectRemoveEdit` hold the `Obj` itself rather than a copy, so re-insertion keeps the uid a mesh edit recorded earlier still names. Selection pushes no step at all: clicking an object is not laborious to redo the way a lasso is, and an undoable selection would move `history.head` and make a document ask to be saved because the user looked at something else — which is what keeps `dirty == history.head != saved_head` honest. `document.reserve_uid` exists because the counter is per *process*: a `.wblk` whose uids run past this session's would otherwise collide with the next new object. A gizmo drag applies in place and commits **one** step per object on release, against the transforms recorded at the press — which is what `set_transform`'s `was` argument is for, and why a gizmo must rebind rather than write through `trs()`'s live arrays.

**Geometry that is no longer what a generator would build freezes it, and `Document.set_mesh` is where that happens.** Not the op layer, which is where it was and where it was only half applied: `clay_ops.run_mesh_op` and Smooth cleared `generator`, while Delete, Bake Transform, Mirror and an element drag went straight to `set_mesh` and kept it — so the object still claimed to be "box, size 1", the properties panel kept offering that size field, and touching it called `build(**edited)` and replaced the mesh with a pristine box. The edit was gone with no warning. The freeze is pushed *with* the mesh edit as one `CompoundEdit`, so it undoes with the edit it belongs to — it used to be a second step, and one Ctrl+Z then restored the generator claim over the still-edited mesh, the exact state the freeze exists to prevent — and `keep_generator=True` is the single exception: `panes/clay_props.py`'s own rebuild, where the new mesh *is* what the generator makes and the claim is still true.

**Clay merges two ways, and the second one is the first one's stated exception.** `clay/ops.join` is a *weld*: it concatenates geometry, fuses coincident points, and keeps everything inside the overlap — its docstring says why, and the reason held for as long as it did because classifying every face against every other solid is exactly the sort of thing that silently deletes a surface the user can see. `clay/ops_boolean.union` does not overturn that argument; it stops being ours to get wrong. `manifold3d` is a published CSG kernel, `trimesh.boolean` dispatches to it, and it is a **core** dependency for `scipy`'s reason — `trimesh.boolean` ships no engine, so `engine="manifold"` would raise at call time rather than at import, and Clay is part of the base app rather than an extra. It is imported *inside* the one function that needs it, joining Pillow in `tests/clay/test_clay_imports.py`'s `LAZY_ONLY`: this package is imported to answer questions about what an extrude does to a UV, and a CSG kernel behind every one of those is the cost the rule exists to refuse. **Both ops survive because they answer different questions**, and the manual says which is which: two shapes that *touch* and should read as one surface is a weld, two that *interpenetrate* and should read as one solid is a union. Three costs are stated rather than discovered, and together they are the whole reason the weld is not simply retired — a boolean recuts every face it touches, so **no output corner corresponds to any input corner** and `uv` is `None` rather than coordinates invented from the nearest survivor; the kernel works in triangles, so **n-gons do not survive**; and **every input must be a closed volume**, because "inside" is meaningless for a surface that encloses nothing — refused by name, with the weld named as the remedy. `material` and `smooth` come from the *target's* first face, which is not an arbitrary pick among the inputs: the target is already what a merge keeps the name, the transform and the frame of. Everything else is `_join`'s shape unchanged — topmost visible as the target, `ClayDoc.join_objects` for the one-`CompoundEdit` bookkeeping, the survivor left selected — and it runs **in-process and synchronously**, because manifold is CPU and fast at the scale Clay authors at, while a `TaskRunner` hand-off would mean a document edit landing from another thread. **Merge Faces is the same kind of decision one level down**: it is `ops_dissolve.dissolve_faces` registered a second time under the name people search for, not a second implementation — "dissolve" stays because it is what the vertex and edge modes do too, and a user who wants two faces to become one searches for "merge", finds nothing, and concludes the editor cannot do it.

**A drag delta is narrowed in one place, and an explicit constraint beats a snap.** `clay/drag.py` is pure in the `vram.py` sense (numpy plus `viewer.math3d` for the one quaternion, nothing from `service`/`queue`/`studio`) and is a *filter*: every function is `(quantity, lock) -> quantity`, so the three gizmos, the element-drag preview and the commit all go on knowing only that they were handed a delta. `ClayView._narrow` is the single place it is applied, above both the object path and the element path, which is also what fixes the order — a user who has typed `X 2` has said exactly where the thing goes, so a nearby vertex must not quietly overrule it, and `_element_world_transform`/`_apply` stand the grid down for either. **A typed value replaces the quantity; an axis lock only projects it**, which is why a number with no lock is still meaningful (it sets the length of the displacement the mouse chose the direction of) and why a locked scale returns the *other* axes to one rather than leaving them dragged. `ClayView.dragging` is checked before every other binding in `clay_mode.handle_key`, because the number row is bound to the element modes and a `1` typed into a drag must be a digit; Esc there is `cancel_drag`, which puts the transforms back and records nothing — it used to clear the pane's bookkeeping and leave the view holding the grab, so the objects stayed where the last motion put them with no step to undo. Proportional editing is one weight per vertex and nothing else (`falloff` is `1 - smoothstep`, because a linear ramp creases at both the selection boundary and the radius, which is the ridge the feature exists to avoid), the radius is world metres divided by the object's largest scale component (`ops.join`'s rule), and the neighbourhood is measured **from the nearest selected vertex** rather than from a centroid the geometry may not pass through. Snapping is screen-space through `pick.nearest_vertex`'s new `allowed` mask, which exists for one reason: a drag that could snap onto its own moving geometry would track the cursor exactly and report a snap, which looks like the feature working.

**The two boundary ops rest on one fact: a `Mesh` stores no wire edges.** `edges` is derived from face corners, so an edge exists exactly while some face uses it — which is why `ops_topo.extrude_edges` and `bridge_edges` refuse an interior edge by name rather than guessing, and why **a vertex extrude is not implementable at all**: an extruded lone vertex is a position no face uses, which `compact_vertices` drops on the way out. `extrude_verts` therefore extrudes the *border* edges a vertex selection implies and refuses when it implies none. `extrude_edges` takes no distance, unlike `extrude_faces`: a closed rim's owning faces point radially outward all the way round, so their mean normal is zero and an `offset` would silently do nothing — and a parameter that silently does nothing is worse than no parameter. **Neither op guesses at winding.** Each new quad traverses its shared edge opposite to the face already on it, which is what "consistently oriented" means, and for a bridge that is true on *both* sides at once because each loop is ordered by the direction its own faces traverse it. The only thing bridge measures is which rotation of a closed loop pairs with the other, because a ring carries no starting vertex — and getting that wrong raises nothing, it just puts a visible twist in the strip.

**One home, `~/.warlock`, and the vendored binaries are the only exception.** `config._home()` resolves `WARLOCK_HOME` (default `Path.home() / ".warlock"`) and `data_dir`, `db_path`, `bench_dir`, `palette_dir` and `t2i_model_root` all default under it -- each reading the helper itself rather than a sibling field, because a `default_factory` lambda cannot see one, which is also what keeps every per-root `WARLOCK_*` variable winning over `WARLOCK_HOME`. Every root used to be computed off `PROJECT_ROOT`, i.e. a user's generated work lived inside the source tree: invisible to anyone who had not cloned it, destroyed by a `git clean`, gone on the next reinstall. `trellis_server_exe`, `gltfpack_exe` and `native.py`'s `_DEFAULT_DLL` stay repo-relative, and that is not an oversight -- they ship *with* the checkout, so moving them would break an install that has them and fix nothing for one that does not.

**The move to that home is copy, verify, delete, in that order, and it is preconditioned.** `migrate.run` is called from `config.get_config()` **before any `mkdir`**, so the "destination already exists and is non-empty, leave it alone" rule sees the destination as the user left it. It is stdlib-only for `vram.py`'s reason -- everything calls `get_config`, including the Blender worker and the offline fetch subprocess. The case it was written for is cross-volume (a checkout on D:, a home on C:), so there is no atomic rename available and the only safety is ordering: `copytree` into a `<dest>.incoming` staging directory, recount files *and* bytes on both sides, `os.replace` into place, and only then `rmtree` the source (`WARLOCK_MIGRATE_KEEP=1` skips even that). Three preconditions run before the first byte: `BEGIN EXCLUSIVE` on the legacy `jobs.sqlite` (a real lock, not `session.marker`, which survives a crash and would wedge the migration forever), free space against the measured size x1.1, and "the user has not already chosen" -- any root with its own variable set, or a `WARLOCK_HOME` aimed back at the checkout, is left alone. `tests/conftest.py` sets `WARLOCK_NO_MIGRATE=1` session-wide and autouse: without it the first test to build a `Config` would start copying the developer's real 95 GB `models/` into their home directory.

**`clean_jobs` is the one bulk delete that ignores `retained_job_ids`, and the confirmation has to say so.** `prune_jobs` and `empty_trash` both spare a job carrying evidence, for the measured reason recorded on `retained_job_ids` (117 verdicts, 100 of them naming directories a bulk delete had already destroyed) -- and the consequence was that nothing could say "remove everything". `service._jobs_lifecycle.clean_jobs` is that, so it keeps no job at all, and the dialog in `studio/panes/library.py` states plainly that the accepted meshes and the labelled references go with the rest and the verdict rows survive with nothing behind them. It refuses outright on a queued or running job rather than skipping it as prune does -- a "delete everything" that left three behind has failed at the only thing it claims -- and it never touches `jobs.sqlite`, `poser/`, `profiles/` or `autosave/`. Its task key starts `delete:` because `main._on_task_done` re-measures storage only for that prefix.

**A built asset is an ordinary asset: `source.glb` is authored, `model.glb` derives from it.** `service.jobs.import_mesh` mints a `done` model row from GLB bytes exactly as `import_reference` mints a reference row from painted pixels — no worker run, because the artifact already exists — and the payoff is that rigging, posing, sprite sheets, the triangle retarget and every mesh export are pure functions of `model.glb` and need to learn nothing about Clay. The `source.glb`/`model.glb` split is the existing invariant rather than a new one, and it is what keeps `optimize_job`'s retarget working on something a user modelled. Grounding applies (a failure is logged and swallowed, as everywhere else that runs on a GLB already on disk); `validation.check_glb` refuses non-GLB bytes and a GLB with no primitive at the door, before a job directory exists; and `params["built"]` is an *input*, so it stays out of `DERIVED_PARAMS` and is what `rerun_job` reads to refuse a reroll or a remesh **above** the `input.png` check — below it, a built asset was refused with the reference message and sent the user to hand-edit an image that never existed. `assets/<job_id>/build.wblk` follows the `paint.ora` precedent — the filename keeps its pre-rename spelling for the same reason, a compatibility contract with every asset directory already on disk (absent from `MEDIA`/`LISTED`, never served) but deliberately has **no** staleness rule: nothing rewrites a built asset's source behind it, and a later retarget makes `model.glb` a derivative of the document rather than making the document wrong.

**A painted reference is a job row with no worker run.** `jobs.import_reference` mints a `done` reference from pixels the user drew -- `JobStore.create` takes an explicit status for exactly this, in one statement, because a create-then-claim pair leaves a window in which the worker's poll picks the job up and tries to run it. `assets/<job_id>/paint.ora` is the layered source behind a hand-edited reference (the filename keeps its pre-rename spelling, a compatibility contract with every asset directory already on disk), following the `input.orig.png` precedent: deliberately absent from `MEDIA`/`LISTED`, never served, and treated as **stale when it is older than `input.png`** -- a revert or a regenerate rewrites the reference without touching the layers, and resurrecting them would show an edit of an image that is gone. The flat half still goes through the untouched `files.save_edited_image`, so the original backup, `hand_edited`, the reference re-measure and the staged replace are all unchanged. That staleness rule decides the **write order**, and it is the opposite of what it looks like: `inker_mode._save_linked` writes `input.png` *first* and `paint.ora` second, because writing the layers first would leave the reference newer than them the moment the save completed — every save marking its own layers stale, and the next open silently flattening a layered document to one layer. Crashing between the two then leaves the layers older than the reference, which is exactly the verdict a half-finished save deserves.

**One GL context, and imgui draws through moderngl.** `studio/imgui_backend.py` reimplements imgui's OpenGL3 backend on moderngl rather than using imgui-bundle's own, which draws through PyOpenGL: moderngl caches GL state, so raw `glBindTexture`/`glUseProgram` calls behind its back leave the viewport rendering with whatever the panels last bound. The same bridge is why `widgets.texture_ref` *registers* a texture as well as wrapping it — an id the renderer does not know maps to no moderngl object, and every image in the UI comes out as the font atlas. Registration has a matching half: a texture must be `forget_texture`d before it is released, or the backend keeps a dead object under a GL name the driver will reuse. `ThumbnailCache` also defers the release itself by one frame and never evicts an entry handed out during the current frame — a card asks for its texture while the UI is built and the pixels are not read until the backend draws, so freeing inside that window frees something the draw list still points at.

**A merged font's base wins every collision, and a rendered frame is the only thing that can see it.** `fonts.load` builds each face as Inter with `lucide.ttf` merged in, and imgui resolves a codepoint against a merged font's sources **in order**: the base font wins and a merged source only fills gaps. Inter ships 745 Private-Use-Area cmap entries — stylistic-set alternates like `divide.squared`, `question.squared`, `less.circled` — and Lucide 0.525.0 assigns its icons across U+E038–U+E682, so the two overlapped on 478 codepoints and **41 of the 83 constants in `icons.py` drew an Inter glyph instead of an icon**: Settings a division sign, Clay's ruler a question mark, Quit a digit six, and Save, Search, Plus, Undo, Redo, Play, Check, Pencil, Eraser, Brush and Palette all punctuation. It shipped because nothing below a rendered frame can see it — the GL smoke suite runs on imgui's default atlas where every icon is a missing-glyph *box* by design, and its own docstring says it asserts nothing about what anything looks like. The fix is at the font (`scripts/strip_font_pua.py`, idempotent, re-run after any font bump) rather than at the loader, because imgui 1.92's Python binding exposes no glyph exclusion on `ImFontConfig` and the two alternatives are worse: making lucide the base source would take the line metrics from a font with ascent 1000 / descent 0, and remapping lucide's codepoints would break the one rule `icons.py` states — that its numbers come from a named release's `info.json` and are never guessed. `tests/test_fonts.py` asserts the wider invariant (no PUA in any Inter face at all, not merely none that collides today) because the icon set grows and a future collision would be attributed to the new icon rather than to the font.

**A persisted file is untrusted input, and a startup failure is a dialog.**

`settings.get` returns whatever JSON held, and `settings.get(k) or {}` reads as a type guard while being nothing of the kind: a stored `"panes": "left"` is truthy and raises `AttributeError` on the `.get` after it. Seven sites read that way — one of them inside `widgets.section`, which most panes draw every frame, so one wrong byte was a crash on the first frame of whichever mode read it, on **every** launch, because nothing on that path rewrites the file. `settings.as_dict`/`as_list` are the answer, and they are free functions rather than `Settings` methods because the same question is asked *inside* a stored blob as often as at the top of one (`layouts.Arrangement` asks it four times about values that never came from a `Settings` at all). A scan test refuses the `or {}` form. `restore_form` and `_safe_form_value` had done exactly this for *form* dicts since they were written, on the stated grounds that settings are read before any control can clamp them; the argument was never form-specific. `filters_from_stored` now checks types as well as key names, so `{"text": 5}` no longer reaches `parse_query` and imgui's `input_text`.

`Settings.load` has three failure modes and had one answer. `_migrate` ran *outside* its `try` and reads stored values as the shapes it wrote — `value in ASSET_TYPES` on a stored `asset_type` of `{}` is `TypeError: unhashable type`, raised before there is a window, a GL context or an excepthook. A **version mismatch** discarded every preference in silence, with no notice and no rename, and the first successful save overwrote the file; it is the same loss the unreadable case is careful about, and renaming aside matters *more* there, because the likeliest way to reach it is an older build opening a newer file. All three now keep the old file under a timestamped name and say so once.

The journal's own sidecar is the same class of input: `at=float(meta.get("at"))` was unvalidated and its caller is `snapshot`, which runs on the **first frame after a crash**. `status_line` wraps its whole call for this reason; the startup offer had no wrapper and needed one less.

**And nothing dies quietly before the window exists.** Under `pythonw` stderr is the null device, so a failure there is a process that starts and vanishes — the user double-clicks the icon and nothing happens, twice. `instance.alert` needs no window, no GL and no imgui, and `instance.ask` is its MB_YESNO twin (No is the answer to anything that is not an explicit Yes, because what it authorises moves the user's library index). Guarded now: `get_config()` (which runs `migrate.run` and `mkdir`s four directories), `_run_locked`'s `except` (which logged and returned 1 with no dialog), and two failures that are recoverable in principle and so carry their own words rather than a type name — `pygame.display.set_mode`/`moderngl.create_context` say the driver does not support OpenGL 3.3, and `fonts.load`, which had no existence check at all, says the installation is incomplete. That font path is *also* reachable mid-session through `fonts.reload` from the UI-scale slider, which is why the file check runs before `clear_fonts` and why the caller treats a failure as a toast rather than the end of the session.

**A number inside a document file decides an allocation, so it is checked before anything allocates from it, and by the object rather than by the call site.** `zipguard.BoundedZip` established the shape: a member is read through its own declared size as a hard bound, because a rule eighteen call sites have to remember is a rule that holds at seventeen. What that closed is the *archive* layer, and every format here has a second one below it — a number in the payload that no container ever sees. Four leaves now hold that layer, and each of them is one bound made a property of the thing being read. **`npyguard`**: `np.lib.format.read_array` sizes the buffer from a 128-byte header and `wmap._read_layer_array` compared the shape on the line *after*, so a member `BoundedZip` waved through as 128 honest bytes was a 4 TB request; `.npz` is worse, because `np.load` on one opens a nested zip with numpy's own plain `zipfile` and the outer ceiling is not in the path at all past the outer member. **`pixelguard`**: the ceiling is asked before `convert`, which is the call that allocates — `packwright/wpack.py` stated that rule at the one door that had it. `plotter_io._within_ceiling`'s docstring argued that Pillow's own `MAX_IMAGE_PIXELS` was the bomb guard and that a second ceiling would be a second answer; **nothing in this repo ever set Pillow's limit**, and its ~89.5 M default only *warns* between one and two times itself, so a 200 KB PNG decoding to 715 MB passed under every ceiling in the app. The number is 8192 squared because that is `pipelines.sheet.MAX_ATLAS_PX` and `inker_mode.NEW_MAX` both — the largest image this build can legitimately be handed — and a hard refusal there rather than promoting `DecompressionBombWarning`, because that promotion is a `warnings` filter and `warnings` filters are process-global state in an app that decodes on task threads. **`xmlguard`**: the DTD refusal is on the parser's own DOCTYPE event. The `b"<!DOCTYPE" in data[:4096]` probe both XML doors carried was **bypassed two ways by direct execution** — a UTF-16 document spells it `< ! D …` so the byte substring never matches, and a comment is legal prolog content so five thousand bytes of `<!-- -->` put the declaration past byte 4096, which is the exact sentence the docstrings rested on. `ET.XMLParser` does not expose its expat parser under the C accelerator, so there is no `StartDoctypeDeclHandler` to hang this on; what it *does* do is call `doctype()` on its **target**, which is where the refusal and the depth cap both live. The depth cap is the half neither door had, and the shallow case is the worse one: a document nested a few hundred deep *loads*, and then the frame-thread walkers blow up once a frame, which is a session that cannot be closed rather than a file that will not open. **`sizeguard`**: one `within_ceiling`, because `MAX_CLAY_SOURCE_BYTES` existed from the day the format did and was applied at the upload and at neither door a user reaches.

**A task result is claimed by prefix, and what nothing claims is reported.** `main` has stated that rule about task keys for as long as it has had them, and enforced it on the *failure* path only: `_on_task_done` is forty-odd `if key.startswith(...)` branches and a key matching none of them fell off the end, so a mode closing while its own task was in flight looked exactly like a result that had been handled. The tail now reports one, once per key rather than once per arrival (several are resubmitted by panes that run every frame), at `log.info` rather than as a warning — because `SILENT_TASK_KEYS` is the set of prefixes whose *success* genuinely has nothing to do here, and a deliberately silent key nobody added to it would otherwise cry wolf for the session. Each entry on that list carries its own reason: `journal:` moves its own mark and it is the failure the user is owed; `open-log` hands off to the OS; `thumb:` is picked up by a cache keyed on mtime; `derive:` writes inside the job directory and is deliberately not `save:`, because "Saved to <internal path>" names a file the user cannot find; `wrap:` is re-read by the pane that asked. `screenshot` was on neither list and is the case that shows why the rule matters — a capture the user had just chosen a destination for finished in silence, which is indistinguishable from a save that did not happen.

**A broad `except Exception` in `studio/` must leave a trace — surface, log, or say why it is silent.** The pass expected to find swallowed user actions among the ~163 of them and found none: every one either toasts, sets an error field a pane draws, logs, or degrades to a documented default, and the frame loop genuinely has to degrade past a lost display, a stand-in backend and a headless run. Six were silent *and* unexplained, which is the only shape that cannot be reviewed afterwards — a bug and a deliberate fallback look identical — and each now says which it is. The rule is held by a scan in `test_winjob`'s idiom rather than by review, with the two guards that class of test needs: one asserting it is still finding handlers at all, and one asserting its detector fires on a handler that *is* silent. Scoped to `studio/` deliberately: this is a rule about what the user is told, and the worker and service layers have no user in front of them — `queue.py`'s catches are reported through the job row.

**A ceiling gates the door it was written beside, and the user comes through the other one.** Five of this pass's resource findings are one finding: the number existed, was chosen carefully and was argued in a comment, and there was a second call site nobody wired it to. `glbimport.MAX_TRIANGLES` gated *import* while Clay's Smooth multiplied the face count by four per press with no budget at all; `layout.MAX_SPRITES` was asked at *pack* time, of a document that had already accepted the sprites, so a full pack could only be fixed by deleting some; `inker_mode.NEW_MAX` was wired to the new-canvas form and not to the resize popup, which stored `(max(1, w), max(1, h))` — a floor and no ceiling — and fed it to `doc.scale`, so `100000` was 40 GB a layer on the frame thread over an unsaved document; `MAX_LIST_LIMIT` clamped the library's *query* while `load_more` incremented a counter without bound, so past the ceiling "Load older" went on offering to do something it could no longer do. The resize case is the one where the second door needed a *different* answer rather than the same one: shrinking is the usual reason to open that popup, and the comment on `NEW_MAX` said so, so `clamp_resize` caps growth and leaves shrinking free, at `NEW_MAX` **or the document's own size**, whichever is larger — otherwise a 12,000-pixel canvas somebody imported snaps to 8192 the moment its owner opens the popup to crop it.

**A cache bounded by count is bounded by nothing when the item size is an argument.** `ThumbnailCache` evicts at `MAX_TEXTURES = 120` and `max_side` is *per call*: the manual passes 1600 where the library passes 256, so one "limit" spans 31 MiB of VRAM and 1.2 GiB. It carries a byte budget beside the count now, maintained beside every insert and removal rather than summed in `_evict` (which runs on every insert), and read off the texture object rather than remembered beside it — the two insert paths mint their textures differently, and a size recorded at one and not the other is a budget that quietly stops counting half the cache. The same argument, one layer along: Inker's *cel* thumbnails are correctly capped by count because a cel thumbnail is 36 square whatever the document is, and its **frame** textures needed bytes because a frame texture is the whole canvas — fifty frames is 2.6 MB of thumbnails and 800 MB of frames. Both bounds are kept there, because a 64-square document is 16 KB a frame and the byte budget alone would let eight thousand entries into the list the sweep walks.

**Where an app-global dictionary is keyed on something interpolated, the sweep is the feature.** `motion._STATE`/`_TARGET`/`_FRAME`/`_VELOCITY` are keyed on strings with job ids in them and `forget` had one caller, so browsing a thousand assets left a thousand entries. What makes the sweep exact rather than a guess is `_FRAME`, which is already recorded for an unrelated reason — the idle clamp must not be held at 60 fps by a card that stopped being drawn mid-move — and is therefore a per-key record of when something last asked. It runs on one frame in six hundred, from the frame loop beside the other four censuses, because the walk is O(keys) and the keys are what it bounds. `state.preview`'s per-tab entries are the same class of problem with a different answer: `release_doc`'s `_PER_TAB_KEYS` is a list somebody has to remember to add to, and its own comment records four keys that were quietly outside it — so an AST scan now finds every f-string in Inker's modules whose literal head is a `name:` prefix and whose interpolations mention a uid, and fails on one the sweep does not cover. `imgui_backend._textures` gets neither: the renderer cannot ask whether an owner still wants an entry, so guessing would free a texture a pane is about to draw. It gets the thing it was actually missing, which is a log line at a threshold that doubles each time it fires.

**A per-item bound is not a bound until something counts the items.** `tmx._decompress` and `_chunk_side` are each correct — per layer, per chunk — and nothing capped how many of either a file could declare: a minimal zlib-encoded 4096-square layer element is ~250 bytes of XML decoding to 67 MB, against a 100 MB file ceiling. `_Budget` rides beside `placed` through both layer readers for the reason `placed` does: the readers recurse, so no single frame ever sees the total, and a check per call site would be a check per group. The TMJ half is where this was found — it computed its chunk sides with `int(...)` while the TMX half beside it called `_chunk_side`, so the cap the XML reader's own comment argues for was written and not called on the other spelling.

**The job store is the app's single point of total failure and had the least recovery in it.** `sqlite3.connect`, `executescript` and `_migrate` were unguarded in `JobStore.__init__`, so a malformed image was the generic "ran into a problem while starting" box on every launch with no way in and nothing naming the file. It raises `db.StoreUnreadable` carrying the path; `main._offer_store_reset` asks, once, and `db.set_aside` renames all three WAL parts under a timestamp — a rename because a database sqlite will not open is still a file `.recover` might, and all three parts because a fresh database beside a stale `-wal` is a database whose first read has to decide whether that journal is its own. A `quick_check` row in `doctor.py` is the other half: a database that has *started* to go should be found while the app is still up and a backup is still possible.

**`scripts/screenshot_modes.py` is what "somebody looked at it" means here.** It drives the **real** `App` — `setup_window`/`setup_runtime`/`setup_context` and `App.frame` — and reads frames back off the default framebuffer, one PNG per mode per palette, so the dispatch exercised is `_build_ui`'s own and the fonts are the loaded atlas. Reimplementing the mode dispatch in a harness would be a second dispatch, which is the thing `modes.py` is data rather than a callback table to avoid. `--seed` opens an Inker canvas and a Clay model through the same entry points the buttons call, because both modes' empty state is exactly the frame that shows none of their controls. **The seeds are the picture's subject, and a mode with none is a picture of nothing**: `--troupe` builds a finished character sheet through the app's own `clips.expand_clips`/`charsheet.plan`/`sheet.sidecar` -- the shipped clip library and no weights, so it costs a millisecond rather than a GPU -- because until it existed all three Troupe images showed four empty-state panes, no sprite and both handoffs greyed. **The doctor banner is dismissed before any capture.** It describes the machine that ran the pass rather than any mode, it is a full-width strip that shifts every pane down in some pictures and not others, and it prints the path it searched: 44 of the 84 images in this repo shipped with a developer's real home directory drawn across the top of them. It found four defects in one pass, and three of them share a cause worth stating on its own: **the smoke suite runs at UI scale 1.0 and nothing else does.** `theme.apply` sets `item_spacing` through `sp()`, so a grid that subtracted a literal `8` for its gaps was right at 1.0 and short by 4.8 px per gap at 1.5 — the Inker toolbox lost its fifth column and Clay its fourth button, on the only scale anybody runs. `widgets.grid_width` asks the style instead; `widgets.labeled_slider_int`/`_float` are `labeled_combo`'s rule applied to the control that was missing it (imgui draws a slider's label *outside* the widget, so nine full-width sliders had no visible name at all); and `widgets.card` takes `no_scrollbar`, since a scrollbar in a fixed-size tile is a symptom that worsens itself by taking its width out of the content region.

**Startup is three phases, and `run` drives them itself so a splash can cover the slow one.** `setup_window` (DPI, pygame, GL attributes, moderngl, fonts, theme) is fast and must come first; `setup_runtime` is the slow half — doctor, the worker, and the out-of-process bpy probe — and runs on a plain worker thread while `studio/splash.py` pumps frames; `setup_context` builds `AppState` and `Ctx` back **on the frame thread**, because it makes textures and textures need GL. The splash holds for `max(3 s, load time)`, and a quit during it *waits the load out* rather than abandoning a half-started runtime with a `python.exe` stranded mid-`import bpy`. `in_setup` stays true across all three, so the log still tells "never appeared" apart from "vanished after twenty minutes" — a failure to build the Ctx is a startup failure even though the window is already up. **There is deliberately no `setup()` composing the three.** It existed briefly with no caller, while two tests stubbed `app.setup` to isolate `run`; once `run` stopped calling it those stubs became silent no-ops, `run` reached the real `setup_window`, initialised pygame for real, and the live display it left behind broke every GL test that ran after it — 74 errors, none in the code that changed, and green in isolation. A convenience method with no caller is not free.

**Rendering parity is against three r170's stock pipeline, not against an idea of PBR.** The browser build never wrote a custom shader, so `viewer/programs.py` reimplements what three does: the Stephen Hill ACES fit *with* its `/0.6` pre-scale (dropping it is the classic "everything is dark" bug), the exact piecewise sRGB OETF, and the Lazarov/Karis `DFGApprox` polynomial three itself uses. The viewport background is deliberately **not** tone-mapped -- three sets the clear colour straight back to sRGB, so it is the literal hex. `viewer/env.py`'s gradient probe exists for one reason: ambient from *below*, without which a quarter of every mesh renders darker than the background and reads as holes.

**A verdict is a row keyed by job id, and it carries a copy of the settings it judged.** `service/verdicts.py` records a judgement against any finished job — a sweep unit in Review, or an ordinary asset in Create's inspector, feeding one pool. The `vector` column is denormalized *on purpose*: `prune_jobs` deletes job rows and their directories, and the learning corpus has to outlive the assets it was learned from. So is the sweep context (`sweep_id`/`sweep_unit`/`seed`/`prompt_hash`, migration 5): matched-pair comparisons must survive `delete_sweep` taking the job rows, and rows recorded before the columns existed simply never pair. Verdicts are append-only and latest-wins per `(job_id, source)` by max id (an AUTOINCREMENT rowid, not `created_at`, which ties at the rate a reviewer presses a key); `source` stays a free string so a future `ai:<model>` judge sits beside a human rather than overwriting them. **A mesh verdict is a grade, not a bit, and `verdict` survives as a derived column with exactly one writer.** Migration 10 adds `verdicts.grade` — an integer −5..+5, model stage only — because the binary corpus failed at its own purpose: 3 accepts against 81 rejects on 2026-08-07, in which a solid slab, a smeared texture and a mesh a modeller would fix in five minutes are the same row, and a bit can say a mesh failed but never how close it came. The `verdict` TEXT column is *not* dropped: `vectors.verdict_for_grade` derives it at record time, which is what leaves prune retention, the judge's label reads, the `latest_verdicts`/`unverdicted_models` SQL and every findings-v3 reader unchanged — and one writer is what stops that from being the two-spellings hazard, which is also why `record_verdict` **refuses** a caller-supplied `verdict` on the mesh stage rather than checking it. Existing rows are backfilled ±3 and the cut is `grade >= 3`, and those are one decision with two names (`BINARY_GRADES["accept"] == USABLE_GRADE`, asserted rather than commented): a cut above +3 would silently demote every row the migration just wrote, one below it would claim a grade nobody recorded was already usable, and ±3 is the mildest grade preserving what a binary reviewer actually asserted — leaving ±4/±5 for judgements they had no key to make. `docs/measurements/2026-08-09-grade-scale.md` is where every one of those numbers is argued, per the rule that a constant the stored corpus is keyed on gets its document first. **Tags replace reasons and are legal at every grade**: the five bad spellings are the old `REASONS` tuple frozen (the corpus carries those exact strings in the same `reasons` column and there is no alias table, so a rename would split the evidence rather than migrate it), five good ones join them, and the two vocabularies are **disjoint strings** — which is the whole licence for one storage namespace, since polarity is then recoverable from the tag itself and the findings writer splits by membership, keeping `bench/findings.py` pure-stdlib. Five per polarity is load-bearing rather than tidy: it is what makes Ctrl+1-5 and Shift+1-5 map positionally with no second table naming which digit is which. **Image-stage labels stay binary and their `grade` stays NULL permanently** — they feed binary logistic probes, so a grade would be thresholded straight back to a bit, and the two-key loop is what makes a hundred-image pass viable at all; `record_verdict` refuses a grade there and a verdict on the mesh side, so each stage takes exactly its own argument. `service/findings.py` aggregates them into the same `bench_dir/findings.json` the generate panes already read through `bench.findings.load`. **The marginals are confounded, and it is deliberate:** a verdict credits *every* `param: value` in its vector — the price of letting daily use feed the hints at all. The unconfounded answers live elsewhere in the document: `vectors` ranks whole configurations **by Wilson lower bound of the usable rate** (`wilson_low`, z=1.96 — a raw-rate sort let a lucky 5/5 outrank a 19/20; the raw rate is still displayed beside the floor), with `mean_grade` as the **first tie-break and deliberately not the primary key**: a mean−SE bound degenerates at n=1 (sd is zero, so the bound is the mean), which re-creates the lucky-5/5 pathology in a new coordinate system, and over the freshly backfilled corpus every grade is ±3 so the mean is an affine function of the usable rate anyway. A bucket with no grades sorts last among its ties rather than as 0.0 — `Filters.order`'s unanswerable rule, and here it matters twice over because 0 is a real grade meaning "no opinion either way" and so cannot also stand for "nobody has said", and `comparisons` holds matched pairs recovered from sweep structure — two rows pair iff same `(sweep_id, source)`, same `seed`, vectors differing in exactly **one** key (absence is a side, rendered `"unset"`; never parsed from a `sweep_unit` label). `VECTOR_PARAMS` is an allowlist rather than "params minus DERIVED_PARAMS", so adding a derived value to queue.py can never silently widen what a verdict is filed against; floats are rounded to 6dp because an imgui float32 slider hands back `0.6000000238418579`. **It lives in `warlock/vectors.py`, not `service/`** (re-exported by `service.findings`), because queue.py may not import `service` and the worker needs the same vocabulary for observations — a rule that since 2026-08-11 covers the `_q_*.py` siblings holding the rest of `Worker`'s methods as well, and is pinned rather than stated by `tests/test_queue.py::test_the_worker_never_imports_service_studio_or_imgui`, which AST-walks `queue.py` and every `_q_*.py` including function-body imports — and the grade scale and tag vocabulary live there for a *second*, unrelated reason that lands in the same place: `service/verdicts.py` imports `service/findings.py` at module top, so a constant both need cannot live in the importer. `findings.json` is at version 4 and strictly additive — every v3 key keeps its value and its meaning, because `accepts` is now counted off the derived cut and computed identically, so a v3 reader is correct on a v4 file and `bench.findings` renders the v3 strings character for character on a file that carries no grades. A comparison's win is the **higher grade** (over the backfill that is exactly the old accept-beats-reject predicate, and on graded rows it says strictly more: a +4 beating a +1 was filed as a tie), with `_grade_of` falling back to the same `BINARY_GRADES` table the migration used so a pre-migration row is the same evidence as a migrated one.

**An observation is the verdict table's pattern applied to machine measurement, and nothing deletes one.** `queue._observe_finished` appends one `observations` row per model-stage job that reached a terminal status — config vector, sweep context, and `vectors.observation_metrics` (worst/mean hole fraction from `mesh_audit`, watertight/triangles/ready from `mesh_report`; a malformed piece costs its own key, never a raise) — via `Worker._record_observation`, which logs-and-swallows like `_audit_mesh` because a diagnostic must never fail a job whose mesh is on disk. That is what lets evidence accumulate on every *generation* rather than every review: findings marginals carry a `metrics` sub-object (same confounded semantics), a bucket can exist with zero verdicts, the panes hint `"holes 3% · watertight 71% (21 meshes)"` below the verdict threshold, and comparison entries carry paired metric deltas the moment a sweep finishes. `import_mesh` and the retarget re-audit deliberately write no observation — the corpus is about what generation settings produce.

**Terminal status, not `done`, and the composition gate is why.** `_process` calls `_record_observation` for `error` as well, because a job refused at `reference.prepare` *is* a measurement of the settings that drew the reference — the 17 refusals of the 2026-08-07 rogue sweep wrote nothing at all, so a reader saw each checkpoint's accept rate among the references that survived, which flatters exactly the checkpoints that fail most often. A **cancel** still writes nothing: that is the user changing their mind, and it is the one status that discards the artifacts a row would describe. The metrics decide, not the status — `observation_metrics` returns `{}` for a job with nothing measured, and that is still what keeps an empty row out. The refusal keys are `refused` plus one `refused_<code>` per `reference.REFUSAL_CODES` (`empty`/`occupancy`/`edge`/`multi_object`, index-aligned with `Report.reasons` because a rule that grows a sentence and no code makes a refused job look like a passing one), and **a job that passed the gate records `refused: 0.0`** — the mean is only a *rate* if the passers are in the denominator, and over refusals alone it is 1.0 by construction. `service.findings._metric_summary` averages every `refused*` key it finds into `<key>_rate`; the hint tier renders it first and says **references**, never meshes, because a refused job never reached one.

**The dispatch loop survives a hiccup and gives up on a wall, and the terminal write is retried.** `Worker._run` catches everything, sleeps `POLL_INTERVAL` and loops, which is right for a DB hiccup and was wrong for a corrupt page or a full disk: `self.fatal` was set only from the task-died callback, so a store that answered every question with an exception left `Runtime.alive` True, every job in `queued` with nothing on screen saying why, and one traceback a second written to the disk that had caused it. `LOOP_FAILURE_LIMIT` consecutive failures now sets `fatal` and returns, which is exactly what `main._check_worker` already knew how to report once and well. The counter resets on a successful `next_queued`, because a job that fails inside `_process` is accounted for on its own row rather than being the queue's problem. And `store.finish` gets `FINISH_ATTEMPTS` tries before it gives up — a terminal write that raises leaves the row `running`, and the next launch's `reconcile_startup` calls that "interrupted by shutdown", so a mesh that generated perfectly is reported to the user as a crash. The retry is the cheap half; the value is that the failure now escalates somewhere visible instead of stranding a row in silence.

**`backup_to` opens its own reader and holds no store lock.** It was the one lock-then-blocking-I/O path in `JobStore` — a page walk over the whole database, out to disk, with every other method queued behind it, including the ones the *frame thread* calls. A second connection to the same file is what makes that safe rather than merely faster: WAL readers neither block nor are blocked, and `Connection.backup` snapshots under sqlite's own locking. What it copies is the **committed** database, which is what a backup should contain and the one visible difference (rows inside an open `deferred_commits` block are not in it yet — the same way they are not visible to any other reader).

**Findings are also scoped per subject, and the label is half the feature.** `findings.json` v3 carries a `prompts` section — the same marginals again, one set per `prompt_hash`, built by the same `_marginals` the global ones are so a scoped number means exactly what a pooled one means over a narrower set. Confounding across *settings* is the accepted price of letting daily use feed the hints; confounding across *subjects* is not the same bargain, and it was demonstrated rather than predicted (the regenerated file reported `bg_removal: auto` at n=84 when only 80 were rogue-sweep units, the other four old wood-prompt rejects). `bench.findings.hint(..., prompt_hash=...)` prefers the subject's own evidence, falls back to the pooled corpus rather than to silence, and appends `· this subject` / `· all subjects` either way — an unlabelled fallback is the silent mixing the scoping exists to stop. Passing nothing keeps the old behaviour to the character, label included: a caller that does not know its subject makes no claim about one. A row with `prompt_hash == ""` (an upload, a painted reference) joins no scope at all, or every unrelated one of them pools into a single subject that does not exist. Scoped buckets keep only the four fields `hint` reads, because there is one per distinct prompt.

**But an appended row is not a finding until something recomputes the file, and the worker cannot ask.** `service.findings.refresh` is a task, and queue.py runs on the asyncio thread knowing nothing about tasks or panes — so the *only* place a finished generation is noticed is `main.App._refresh`'s `announce` callback, which mirrors `_observe_finished`'s own condition and marks `AppState.findings_dirty`. A **flag pumped every frame** (`review_mode.refresh_findings` / `pump_findings`), never a direct submit: `TaskRunner.submit` refuses a key already in flight and nothing re-arms it, so a burst of verdicts recomputed over the set as it stood at the first press, and the request following the *last unit of a sweep* — with nothing after it to pick anything up — was dropped for good. The flag is cleared only when the submit is accepted. Both verdict paths go through the same request rather than keeping two spellings of Review's key, and the end-to-end property (`findings.json` carries an observation with **no verdict filed**) is pinned in `tests/test_findings_service.py`, not just the aggregation — testing `aggregate` alone is exactly how a recompute nothing triggered survived review.

**A verdict now says which question it answers, and the third value is the whole reason the column exists.** Migration 7 adds `verdicts.stage` — `model` | `reference` | `blank`, defaulting to `model`, which is what every row written before it is about. Migration 10 gave that column a second job: it is what decides which argument `record_verdict` takes, since **the mesh stage is graded and the image labels stay binary with `grade` NULL**. The instinct is a two-value column mirroring `jobs.stage` and recovering the intent by joining back to the job, and it breaks for the reason `verdicts.vector` is denormalized at all: `prune_jobs` deletes job rows, so a label whose meaning depends on one is uninterpretable exactly when it matters. Two of the values are *intents over the same artifact*, because at the Reference stage the image is the deliverable — a dramatic plate with pillars and a cast shadow is a better **asset** and a worse **blank**, and those are opposed objectives rather than one refined. So a reference image carries two independent labels, which is also why intent may not live in `source`: `latest_verdicts` keys on `(job_id, source, stage)` now, and without stage in that grouping one answer would silently supersede the other. Three consequences. `db.unlabelled_references` is a genuinely different query from `unverdicted_models` and cannot be it with a parameter — that one filters `status = 'done' AND sweep_id IS NULL`, excluding the two things a labelling pass most needs (an errored job, i.e. a reference *refused at the composition gate*, which is the most informative negative available; and sweep units, which are the entire corpus) — and it derives the population it lists from the label stage through `LABEL_POPULATION`, because the product question is about a reference-stage job's image and the blank question about a model-stage job's. `record_verdict`'s `status == 'done'` rule is about **the artifact a verdict judges**, not the status word, so an image label needs the *image* (a refusal has one; the gate runs after the picture is drawn) while the mesh side is untouched — an accept filed against a mesh that never existed poisons the corpus permanently. And `findings.aggregate` filters to `stage == 'model'` **at the top**, which is more than the quality-judge programme asked for, and deliberately: `_marginals` alone leaves `vectors` advertising an image label as a ranked mesh configuration, and `_comparisons` pairing a blank label against a mesh verdict *on the same unit* as a matched pair.

**The judge is a linear probe and it is advisory, which is the only claim it may make.** `judge.py` is pure in the `vram.py`/`memlog.py` sense — stdlib plus numpy plus `bench.metrics` for the embedding (one DINOv2 loader in the repo, with its `local_files_only=True` and its per-device cache, rather than a second answer to "which weights"), nothing from `service`/`queue`/`studio`, and `None` rather than an exception for every way of having nothing to say: no weights, no probe, an unreadable PNG, a width mismatch after a model swap. **`score` returns a probability and there is no threshold anywhere in the module**, because a threshold is a constant the stored corpus is keyed on and owes a `docs/measurements/` document first. Four rules around it are load-bearing. **The probe is trained on pixels, never on the audit scalars** — AUC(`hole_worst` → reject) is 0.115 on the existing corpus, not weakly informative but *backwards*, because a slab has no holes and `meshaudit` scores the dominant failure mode as perfect; a probe fitted to those numbers would need a sign flip to beat a coin and would then be fitting the slab artefact, and the same inversion disqualifies `hole_worst` as a sanity check *on* a probe. **Labels must be human**: the gate's own refusals are tempting free labels and are the rule's own output, so a probe fitted to them reproduces `reference.py` including its blind spots — and `baseline s23` passed those rules and is still a poor blank, which is the case a learned judge exists to catch. **`fit` returns `None` rather than a probe below `MIN_PER_CLASS` of each class**, because the corpus as it stands is 3 accepts against 81 rejects and a probe fitted to that learns "reject" and scores 96% doing it, which is worse than no probe because it is believed. And **`judge.STAGES` is spelled exactly as `verdicts.STAGES` is** (`model`, not `mesh`), pinned by a test rather than by a comment: a translation table between two names for one thing is how the two drift, and the drift would show up as a probe trained on the wrong population. The mesh probe is declared and unbuilt: it is blocked on a corpus with positives in it, and its pooling over eight views is a decision to make with data.

**Labelling is a second loop in Review, and the flag is the same flag.** `review_mode.LabelPass` owns the keyboard whenever it is open (`_label_key`, checked before every verdict binding, so a keypress about a picture can never be filed as a verdict about a mesh), offers two keys and no second step — grades and tags are mesh-stage concepts, and eleven values plus ten tags is far more than a two-second-per-image pass can carry — and keeps an answered row **in place** rather than removing it, since a shrinking grid renumbers itself under the cursor mid-pass. Three of its rules are lessons from elsewhere applied verbatim. Thumbnails upload **one per frame** (`next_thumbnail`), which is `viewer/sheet.StripRender`'s rule at a hundred cells rather than sixteen, and an un-uploaded cell draws a same-size placeholder so the grid never reflows under a click. Retraining is a **flag pumped every frame** (`AppState.judge_dirty` / `pump_judge`), never a direct submit, for exactly the reason `findings_dirty` is one — and it carries the *stage* rather than a bool, since a pass is about one question. And `judge.status` is read **once per listing task and then kept current by arithmetic**, because it is a whole-table scan plus a stat and the panel draws every frame. `by_score` **sorts and never filters**: a judge that hid what it disliked would make its own mistakes invisible, which is the filter bubble, and unscored rows sort last rather than as 0.0 because "no opinion" is not "bad".

**The guided judging pass is the third holder of one licence and the only place retention is overridden.** `review_mode.JudgingPass` is `LabelPass`' shape reused -- a pass rather than a mode flag, so "which loop is `A` pressing" has one answer rather than two flags that can disagree -- and it walks *every* bucket with outstanding units in one run, `RECENT_ID` first, then sweeps in list order. Its `order` is fixed at the start rather than recomputed, because a bucket that gains a unit mid-pass (a job finishing) would extend a pass whose length the user was told, and `filed` counts what this pass filed rather than what is left, or a re-grade would make the header count down. **`A` and `R` are bound again, and that does not reopen the argument that unbound them.** The objection was to a *silent* remap: filing `+3` for somebody reaching for a key the eleven-point scale had retired is a wrong number rather than a missing one. Here nothing is silent -- the pass is entered from a button, its panel draws "Accept (A)" and "Reject (R)", and `vectors.BINARY_GRADES` (+-3) is exactly what migration 10 backfilled a binary row to and exactly what a reviewer with no stronger key was asserting. Outside a pass `a` stays unbound and `test_a_records_nothing_at_all` still pins it. Two keys **change meaning** inside a pass and both are labelled on screen: `R` is *reject* rather than "arm the negative sign" (a binary answer has no magnitude to arm; the negative grades stay on the digits and on the pane's buttons, which keep filing and keep advancing the pass), and `Esc` **ends the pass** rather than disarming. The digits, the tag modifiers and the arrows deliberately fall through unchanged, so the scale is a power path inside the pass rather than something the pass replaces. The report is built **from `state` and never from the store**: `_collect` fetched every unit and `record` keeps the dicts current, and this runs on the frame thread behind the one serialized connection at the moment a reviewer expects the pass to end. It uses **blind labels under blinding**, because naming the arms at the end of a blinded pass un-blinds every unit in it after the fact. **And a sweep every unit of which has a verdict has its assets removed, with a toast and no dialog.** `sweeps.cleanup_sweep` is `delete_sweep` with `jobs.retained_job_ids` replaced by the caller's own keep set, both over one extracted `_remove_units` so the cancel-then-`worker_is_inside`/`dependent_jobs`/`delete_if_not_running` guards cannot drift into two copies. Everything `retained_job_ids` documents about the 2026-08-09 incident (117 verdicts, 100 naming directories that no longer existed) is still true; this is the one **user-chosen** exception, and it is legitimate only because the user has just judged every unit on screen, at which point the assets have served the purpose retention was protecting. The warning is given **up front on the entry card** rather than as a modal afterwards -- the user's explicit choice -- which puts `ask_clean`'s burden on that one sentence: the app has said twice that a bulk delete keeps your evidence, so the place that is false has to name which promise it is breaking. It fires from `record` on `todo` reaching zero **whether or not a pass is running**, because the requirement is about the sweep and not about how it was judged; it refuses `RECENT_ID`, whose units are ordinary library rows and whose removal is prune's job; and it goes out under `CLEANUP_KEY`, sharing `DELETE_KEY`'s toast block so the "delete again in a moment" sentence cannot come to be missing from one of the two. The stated cost: filing on a sweep's *last* unit takes its meshes at once, so the "Left arrow to re-grade it" route that toast names is gone for that one.

**Blinding a review hides the order, not just the label.** `ReviewState.blind` renames every unit to an id prefix *and* presents them in `blind_order` — sorted by a SHA-1 digest of the job id, so the presentation is independent of enqueue order and identical in every process (`hash()` is salted per run, which would re-present the same units under different names tomorrow). Hiding the label alone blinds nothing: `sweeps.expand` enqueues the baseline first and then one unit per axis value, so in a two-arm sweep position names the arm. It is a *session* flag and is not persisted, like everything else in `ReviewState`, because a review resumed blinded without saying so is worse than one that starts unblinded; and `label(state, unit)` takes the state rather than a keyword flag so a call site that forgot it is a TypeError on the first frame rather than an unblinded label inside a blind review.

**A gltfpack tier is qualified by data, and the harness may not consume its own corpus.** `tiercheck.py` is pure (stdlib plus `glbio`) and reads the **JSON chunk** of both GLBs rather than going through trimesh, which merges a scene into one mesh with one material and so destroys the exact property under test — whether a multi-material mesh still assigns each primitive its own material after a simplify pass. Every rule is "not worse than the source", never "identical to an ideal", since a rock with one untextured material is a legitimate input; the accepted cost is that a qualification could pass by preserving nothing, so a source that was already missing something says so in `notes` beside the verdict. It also fails an output that **requires an extension the source did not** — the one loss no UV or texture count would catch, because the viewer's loader refuses a file needing `KHR_mesh_quantization` and `-noq` exists to stop gltfpack writing it. `scripts/qualify_tiers.py` drives `optimize.run` (the same binary and flags a job uses, injected so the table's own behaviour is testable without gltfpack) and writes **only** under its output directory: `service.jobs.optimize_job` rewrites `model.glb` in place and deletes the derived artifacts describing the old mesh, so driving *it* would consume the accepted meshes the qualification needs — and a harness that destroys its inputs can be run once. Its corpus defaults to the meshes a human accepted and it refuses to run when there are none, because whether a tier *preserves* something cannot be judged on output that is already broken.

Every mean carries `counts[metric]`, its *own* sample size: `n` is the bucket's, and a `meshreport` that failed or came back `invalid` leaves an observation with holes and no watertight flag, so one reading in twenty-one was being advertised as twenty-one. The reader thresholds and labels per measurement, and says **meshes** rather than runs — a model-stage observation credits every 2D setting its job was promoted with, so these lines appear under prompt controls where "21 runs" would read as a claim about the picture. UI strings for all of this stay inside imgui's default Basic-Latin+Latin-1 atlas range: `·` (U+00B7) is safe, a real `≥` or `Δ` renders as the missing-glyph box, hence `"(41%+)"` and `"worst-hole"`.

**A derivation is not a save.** `app_ctx.save_key` / `derive_key` are two namespaces because `main._on_task_done` toasts `"Saved to <result>"` for every finished `save:` key — and `derive.get_file` returns the path *inside* the job directory and is never `None`, so the pixel preview submitted under the save key told the user a file had been saved somewhere they never chose. `Ctx.artifact_busy` checks both, which is what keeps a preview and an export of one artifact from ever describing different files.

**A sweep is a family of settings vectors on the live queue, and it goes through the front door.** `service/sweeps.py` expands a `SweepPlan` into `UnitPlan`s (the OFAT fan-out is the convenient builder; hand-picked `vectors` are equally expressible) and submits each through `service.jobs.create_job` and nothing else — same validation, same VRAM admission, same directory-before-row ordering. Membership is two *columns* (`sweep_id`, `sweep_unit`), not params keys: the list filters on them, and because `rerun_job`/`promote_to_model` copy params only, membership can never leak onto a reroll — which sidesteps `DERIVED_PARAMS` instead of bending it. Admission is **all-or-nothing**: every unit is validated before a single row is written, and one bad unit refuses the whole sweep naming itself. Units are ordered into server-config groups (base config first) to minimise trellis restarts, and that ordering is best-effort only — correctness lives in `Worker._generate`, which calls `trellis.ensure_config` with fully *resolved* values on every model-stage job, so an ordinary job after a sweep unit restores the config itself and no bookkeeping can go stale. `ensure_config` adds no spawn site: it writes the fields `_argv` reads and stops a running server whose config differs, letting the existing lazy `ensure_started` do the restart. `Filters.matches` hides sweep units from the library, because one launched sweep otherwise buries a workshop.
