**Mesh candidates are columns, and "Keep" dissolves the group rather than flagging a winner.** Migration 6 adds `candidate_group`/`candidate_index` to `jobs` for exactly the reason migration 4 added `sweep_id`/`sweep_unit`: the library filters on it, params is a JSON string sqlite cannot index into, and — the half that matters — `rerun_job`/`promote_to_model` copy *params* and nothing else, so membership can never leak onto a reroll, which sidesteps `DERIVED_PARAMS` instead of bending it. `service.jobs.promote_candidates` is the entry point (the generic `count > 1 requires output=reference` refusal in `create_job` stands unchanged; candidates are the deliberate exception and are reachable only here), and every candidate is an ordinary mesh job through `promote_to_model` — same validation, same per-job VRAM admission, same directory-before-row ordering. **Candidate 0 keeps the requested `mesh_seed`, the rest draw fresh ones**, the idiom `create_job` already applies to reference candidates. Admission is all-or-nothing like a sweep's, and it needs *no* validation pass of its own: every candidate is the same job but for a pre-checked seed and `promote_to_model` refuses before it writes anything, so a refusal can only land on candidate 0 with nothing yet written — duplicating the check list here is the drift `sweeps._check_unit` has to work to avoid. `candidate_group` is NULL for an ordinary job *and* for a decided one, so `Filters.matches` asks one question and `keep_candidate` clears the column for the **whole group** in one statement: a winner flag would leave the losers hidden with nothing to reach them by, since the picker (`studio/candidates.py`, pure; `panes/candidates_panel.py` draws it, above the inspector's header so it survives an empty selection) derives itself from the rows rather than from remembered state precisely so a restart cannot strand a group. `candidate_index` survives the dissolve and is read by nothing, the forensic half `sweep_unit` is. Keeping never deletes: the losers are *named* and offered to `library.delete_asset` behind the ordinary confirm, and picking a candidate only calls `state.select` — `_sync_viewer` owns the pose guard, `viewer.pending` and the off-thread parse, and a shortcut into `load_model` from the picker would reproduce two of those three and get the last one wrong.

**A sprite draft is write-once, and the sidecar is what says it exists.** A `sprite_synthesis` job invents a sheet from one 2D reference: two candidate 1024px atlases from two seeds, sliced on a *fixed* grid (`pipelines/spritesynth.GEOMETRY` — turnaround 2x2 at 512, walk 4x4 at 256), matted, put on a shared baseline, reduced in one NEAREST pass and quantized to one palette across the whole atlas. Four rules hold it together and each is the same shape as a rule already here. **Nothing is published until both candidates are assembled**, and then in one order — both PNGs, then `<id>.json` last, exactly as `_sheet`/`_pixel_sheet` publish — so `rigging.list_sprite_drafts` treats the sidecar as the completion marker and a cancelled run leaves nothing at all. **A draft is never rewritten in place**: every generate run mints a fresh `draft_id` at the door and the only mutation is delete, which is precisely what makes `sprite_panel.draft_records` sound behind a *directory* mtime stamp — the racily-clean hazard `panes/stamps.py` documents can cost that listing a draft for 50 ms and can never make a cached record wrong. **The grid is never re-detected from pixels**: adoption slices on the sidecar's own `cells` rectangles, so a mis-registered generation is an editable document rather than an unopenable one. And **a warning is never a refusal** — `structural_warnings` reports `empty` / `clipped` / `occupancy` / `unmatted` as machine codes beside sentences (the `reference.REFUSAL_CODES` split, for its reason), because the deliverable is a *pair* the user compares and discarding one leaves them judging a draft against nothing. The pose guide is fed to the canny ControlNet **directly and is not run through `cv2.Canny`** — it is already white line art on black, and detecting edges on it returns the outline of every stroke, i.e. two lines where the guide means one. The heavy matting model runs **once, on the source**, never per cell: it is ~11.5 s an image, and the "plain background" clause in the prompt is what earns the cheap per-cell corner fill instead.

**The cell order is held twice and a test is its only owner.** `pipelines/spritesynth` decides where a cell sits in a generated atlas; `studio/inker/animation.DirectionalLayout` decides what timeline frame *i* means once that atlas is opened. They cannot share code — the inker package imports nothing outward (`tests/inker/test_inker_imports.py` pins the exact set) and `pipelines` modules run inside worker and Blender processes where `studio` is not importable — so `DIRECTION_ORDER`, `DIRECTION_YAWS` and the per-kind grid exist twice, and `tests/test_sprite_geometry_agreement.py` is the **single place** the copies are checked against each other. If it fails, a draft generated with the back view bottom-right opens in Inker as frame 2, silently, until somebody plays the animation.

**A layout is additive metadata about a grid whose pixels are already correct.** `Animation.layout` is construction-time state: no V1 op edits it, and a whole-canvas snapshot restores by mutating the existing `Animation` in place, so it survives undo with no snapshot change. It rides `animation.json` as a top-level `"layout": {"kind": ...}` written **only when set** — the version stays 1 and a layout-less document's member is byte-identical, the same additive rule `tags[].direction` set — and it is read in its **own guard** outside the block that rebuilds the timeline, so a malformed or unknown layout degrades to `None` and never falls the file back to a flat read. On the way out, `sheetout.plan_frames(..., layout=)` replaces the wrap with the sheet's own fixed grid and **refuses a frame count that no longer fills it**, naming both numbers: padding would emit an atlas with a hole where "back, frame 3" belongs, which is the one outcome a game does not notice until it plays the animation. `inker_mode.export_sheet` raises that refusal on the frame thread before the file dialog, with the engine's `ValueError` as the backstop. `snapshot` returns a 4-tuple and `compose` takes the layout **positionally**, so `compose(*snapshot(doc))` still holds — a keyword-only fourth element would have made that spelling drop the grid in silence. Wave 4's export options extend the identical doctrine one file down, into the *other* sidecar `sheetout` writes — the per-export JSON `sheet.py::sidecar()` embeds under `"animation"`, not `animation.json`'s own top-level `"layout"` above: `arrange`/`wrap` (how an ordinary clip packed), `merged`/`skipped` (which frames were deduplicated or dropped), and `trimmed`/`padding`/`extrude` (how the cells were sized and spaced) are each written only when the export actually used them, `SHEET_VERSION` stays 1 throughout every one of them, and a pre-feature export's bytes are unchanged end to end — pinned by `test_a_default_inker_sidecar_is_byte_for_byte_what_it_always_was` the same way `layout`'s own addition is pinned by the no-layout equivalent.

**A new job kind is four edits, not one.** `sprite_synthesis` is the current example and the checklist is: a branch in `Worker._generate` *and* one in `_discard_artifacts` (which must name only this job's own files — the source directory holds every earlier draft, each from a different and successful job); a branch in `vram.estimate`, because an unlisted kind falls through to `return 0.0` and the admission gate becomes a no-op that refuses nothing; and a phase table in `progress._PHASES_BY_KIND`, because `ProgressBus.update` falls back to `(0.0, 1.0)` for an unknown phase and would drag the bar back to zero. The last one is why `_sprite_synthesis` maps `on_step` into its own per-candidate phase rather than reusing `Worker._t2i_state`: that helper emits `t2i_load`/`t2i_sample`, which `PHASES_SPRITE` does not carry.

**The GLB loader is hand-rolled on purpose.** trimesh discards a scene root's transform -- which is exactly where `normalize_glb` puts the grounding transform -- and has no notion of a skin. `viewer/gltf.py` keeps the node graph live after loading, because posing is setting a joint node's local rotation and recomputing world matrices. Being hand-rolled means the spec's edges are this file's problem, and three of them are load-bearing. A file whose `extensionsRequired` names anything outside `SUPPORTED_EXTENSIONS` is **refused**, because `KHR_mesh_quantization` — which the vendored gltfpack writes with `-c` — decodes as silently wrong geometry otherwise. An accessor's `normalized` flag is honoured (`Reader.decoded`), or a `ushort` UV reads back as 65535. And a texture that cannot be *decoded* is skipped exactly as one that cannot be *found* is: the stated policy is that an image is a cosmetic loss, and raising turned one corrupt map into "this file will not open" for an intact mesh. On the draw side, a zero-scaled node's normal matrix falls back to the identity rather than raising `LinAlgError` out of the frame.

**Accessibility is measured, not asserted, and the measurements are in the suite.** `tokens` owns the WCAG maths (`luminance`, `contrast`, `composite`) and the two bars — `CONTRAST_TEXT = 4.5` for copy (SC 1.4.3 AA), `CONTRAST_UI = 3.0` for a control's boundary (SC 1.4.11) — and `tests/test_accessibility.py` re-derives every ratio from the palette that ships, on all four steps of the elevation ramp, in every theme that ships (the parametrization is `sorted(tokens.PALETTES)`, so a new palette enrols itself rather than waiting to be added). **The composite step is what makes the file honest**: contrast is a property of what reaches the eye, so a translucent role has to be measured on the blend and never on the token it came from — that is the whole of UX-18, where MUTED is *stored* at 6.67:1 on PANEL and, at the 0.6 alpha `text_disabled` carries, *drawn* at 3.20:1. So `imgui.text_disabled` is **banned outright** (a scan test enforces it): it is the right rendering for a control nobody may operate and the wrong one for a sentence somebody has to read, and every call site in the tree was the second kind. `widgets.secondary` is the one way to draw second-rank copy, and it is MUTED at full opacity — a fix to the *alpha*, not a new colour. **Contrast is not the only axis, and `pixel` is why the second one is in here.** A ratio qualifies a colour against its *ground*; it says nothing about whether two roles can be told from each other. Dark and light never had to answer for that — indigo against amber separates itself — but a palette with a warm accent puts ACCENT and WARN in one family, and `pixel`'s first pass landed them **2 degrees of hue apart**, each clearing its own bar independently while a focus ring and a warning badge were the same colour under two names. `test_the_accent_is_told_apart_from_the_warning_colour` asks the pair for 12 degrees of hue and, inside 60, for 0.15 of saturation as well, because hue alone is the weakest of the three channels for a red-green deficiency. It does not carry the legibility on its own: `theme.STATUS_GLYPHS` pairs every status with a Lucide glyph, so colour is never the only channel — this stops the *chrome* from collapsing into one hue, which no glyph covers.

**Studio presentation has one control boundary and one pane boundary.** `studio/controls.py` is the only pane-facing route to buttons, inputs, menus, switches, choices and selectable rows; its roles and regular/compact sizes own hover, press, focus, disabled reasons, tooltips, errors and selection. `studio/forms.py` owns field labels, help and validation placement, using stacked fields below 480 design pixels and a 120 design-pixel label column at and above it. `studio/layout.pane` owns major surfaces: sidebars and inspectors use `PANEL`, primary content uses `BG`, and only named major edges receive the one-pixel `DIVIDER`; cards and sections do not grow borders of their own. Pane modules may use invisible canvas and drag targets, but an AST guard rejects direct presentational ImGui controls. Selection is one language — an accent wash and leading boundary for rows, a wash and accent ring for cards, and a selected pill for segmented choices — while a two-pixel accent ring remains focus, not selection. A group has at most one accent-filled primary action, and document commands keep the order New, Open, Save, Save As, Export. The developer-only component gallery (`WARLOCK_DEV_COMPONENTS=1`) is the executable catalogue for both palettes and every supported scale.

**No two items in one window may claim one imgui id, and a scan test is what says so.** imgui hashes an item's id from its label and the id stack and then routes hover, focus and *activation* by that id alone — so two items sharing one is not a cosmetic clash, it is the second item never reliably acting because the first already answers for it. Plotter shipped exactly that: `header("Resize")` and `primary_button("Resize")` in one pane, which made the button inert and the map look unresizable, and the only symptom outside a debug build was Dear ImGui's red "conflicting ID" panel drawn over the fields. The guard in `tests/test_studio_smoke.py` asks imgui's own question rather than comparing label strings — it wraps every id-taking call for one frame and resolves each label through `get_id`, so the id stack is accounted for and a `push_id` loop is correctly *not* a conflict. Each pane is probed in **its own window**, because an id stack is seeded per window: two panes that are separate windows in the app cannot collide, and a probe that drew them into one shared host would report conflicts no user can meet. The fix is a `##suffix`, which is invisible to the reader and is the whole of what imgui hashes differently. **A "New map" dialog and a Resize section are the shape this recurs in** — a form whose submit button is named after the section around it — so anything with a header and a verb belongs in the probe's list.

**A new map is asked for, not invented, and two of its four numbers are one-way.** `plotter_mode.ask_new_document` is the single door — the canvas, the map panel, Home, the command palette and `Ctrl+N` all raise `PlotterState.setup_pending` and none of them builds a document — because a popup belongs to the window that begins it (the rule `inker_canvas.new_canvas_popup` is written around) and three of those five callers are not Plotter panes at all. `studio/plotter_setup.py` owns the presets, the caps and what the numbers mean and is *pure*, so the dialog is a body that draws fields and the tests need no window. What makes the dialog worth existing is that two answers cannot be taken back: the **projection** is fixed once anything is painted (a set drawn for one lattice paints the wrong shape into every cell drawn for the other) and the **tile size** is what `plotter_tilesets` slices a plain image at, so a tileset added to a 32px map is a 32px tileset for good. The **caps are on a slip of the keyboard, not on the engine** (`MAX_TILES`/`MAX_TILE_PX`, 512 each) and clamp on the way *into* the fields as well as out, because a map multiplies — 512 square at 32px is the 268-megapixel composite `export_library` already warns about.

**Changing a map's tile size is allowed on a painted map, and changing its projection is not.** They look alike and are not. A projection decides the *lattice*; a cell's size decides only how large that cell is drawn, and `render` already treats a tileset whose tiles do not match the grid as ordinary — "a 32px map with 48px trees", anchored bottom left. So `MapDoc.set_tile_size` re-slices nothing, renumbers no gid and loses no painting: a gid names a tile whatever size the cell under it is. `TileSizeEdit` is two ints and the object rectangles rather than `ResizeEdit`'s snapshot of every array, because no layer's *shape* changes — snapshotting the document to record two integers would be the whole map's cost for nothing. Objects scale with the cells for `ResizeEdit`'s reason arrived at from the other direction: they are absolute pixels, so a grid whose cells change size leaves them describing a different part of the map unless they travel with it. The scale goes through `scaled_shape`, which handles **both** spellings of an extent — four of the seven shapes state `w`/`h` and two state vertices — because a helper that knew only about `w`/`h` would silently leave every polygon at its old size.

**A file-picker filter is (label, patterns) pairs, and the first pair is the one the picker opens on.** `studio/dialogs.open_file`/`save_file` are the only pickers in the app — portable-file-dialogs, the OS's own, never an in-app browser — and pfd consumes `filters` **two at a time**: a label, that label's space-separated patterns, the next label. Its loop is `i + 1 < size`, so a trailing odd element is discarded silently, and the Windows backend rewrites the spaces to `;` itself. `filetypes.pattern()` renders the patterns half; `_globs()` is private precisely because splatting one entry per glob beside a label does not mean "these patterns under this label" — the second glob becomes the first row's *only* pattern and the third becomes the *next row's label*. Three filters were built that way and all three shipped broken: Plotter's "Add a tileset" advertised six formats and filtered on `*.tsx` alone, so a folder of PNGs came up **empty**; Plotter's "Open a map" could not see a `.tmx` or `.tmj` at all; Packwright's image picker was PNG-only, which is why nobody noticed. The parametrized tests over those same four filters passed throughout, because they read `" ".join(entry[1:])` — joining the tail erases the pairing that decides which patterns are in force, which is the whole of what was wrong. What guards it now is a **scan**, not a list: `tests/test_studio_wiring.py` collects every module-level `*_FILTER` plus `ARTIFACT_FILTERS` and holds each to an even length, patterns where patterns go, and a label that names *exactly* the globs beside it or names none at all. `pfd.all_files_filter()` returning `["All files", "*"]` is the shape the library states for itself.

**And nothing downstream of `save_file` truncates the file it was pointed at.** `studio/atomic.py` (`staged`/`write_bytes`/`write_text`/`save_image`) stages beside the destination and renames, `os.replace` being atomic only within one filesystem — which is why the staging name is a dotfile *sibling* rather than a temp in `%TEMP%`. There are twenty-two `save_file` sites in `studio/`, every one of them writing to a path the user named, and a path the user named is one they may well have named before: `export it again over the last one` is the ordinary case. `Path.write_bytes` and `Image.save` both truncate before writing a byte. The helper existed for a year as `inker_mode._write_atomic` with four callers while nine other exports wrote straight onto the user's file, which is the whole argument for the scan (`tests/test_atomic_writes.py`): it follows the destination through the names derived from it, so the family an exporter writes *beside* the chosen file is covered too, and "remember to stage this one" stops being the mechanism. `save_image` resolves the Pillow format from the real destination's suffix, because the file it hands over is called `.name.tmp`.

**Tab traverses, and the arrows belong to the surface that binds them.** imgui's keyboard navigation is on app-wide, which it could not simply be: Home and the library move a selection with Up/Down, Review steps units with Left/Right, and Inker and Plotter hold Space to pan, so one press would both move the app's own selection and step a focus ring. The arbitration is at the **one door those keys come through** — `imgui_backend._NAV_KEYS` and `reserve_nav_keys`, driven from `modes.NAV_KEY_MODES` — rather than by flagging `no_nav_inputs` per window, which would not have worked anyway since not all five surfaces are child windows. Tab is deliberately **not** in the reserved set, or those five modes would have no traversal at all; modifiers are never reserved, or imgui reads Shift+Tab as a plain Tab and backwards traversal silently becomes forwards; and a reservation always yields to `want_text_input`, where the arrows are caret movement. A glyph button's tooltip is its accessible name, so it is a **required** argument and it shows on `nav_visible` focus as well as hover — on focus *alone* it pops a tooltip on the first frame, before anybody has pressed anything.

**Characters come from `TEXTINPUT`, keys come from `KEYDOWN`, and neither does the other's job.** Synthesising characters from `KEYDOWN.unicode` is what made an IME impossible — the composed result never arrived, only the raw keys that composed it, into the field being composed for — and the `< 0x10000` filter beside it dropped every astral code point in silence. `TEXTEDITING` is swallowed rather than inserted, because imgui has no pre-edit rendering and the half-finished composition would be left in the field. SDL text input is started only while `want_text_input`, which is both what makes typing work at all and what keeps a candidate window off the viewport; `sync_text_input` runs *after* `imgui.render()`, because the flag is only true once the field that wants it has been drawn. The caret position is unreachable from Python (imgui_bundle exposes neither `Platform_SetImeDataFn` nor `PlatformImeData`), so the IME rect is the *field's* rect, set from the two `widgets` wrappers rather than at twenty call sites.

**A column that cannot have the width it claims pushes the next one off the window.** Three-column workspaces reserved two `SIDEBAR_W` sidebars and floored the centre at 300 design px unconditionally, so at 2x UI scale they wanted ~1800 physical px against a resize floor near 1100 — and because the right-hand column is sized from what is left, the pane that fell off the edge was always the inspector, for exactly the users who had enlarged the UI in order to read it. The floor deliberately does **not** multiply the user's zoom in (`main._min_window_size`): that was tried, and it demanded a window bigger than a 1080p display and refused to shrink. So the columns give way in a stated order instead — both sidebars narrow to `SIDEBAR_MIN` first, then the centre drops below `CENTRE_MIN` to `CENTRE_FLOOR`. The fit is settled **once per frame** in `layout.measure()`, beside the width easing, or the left column and the right disagree within a frame; `measure` is separate from `tick` because `tick` is pure arithmetic that headless tests call, where reaching for a viewport is an access violation rather than an error.

**DPI is re-sampled when the window changes display, and the user's zoom is re-read rather than recovered.** Sampling once at startup left a window dragged from a 100% monitor to a 150% one drawn at the old scale until the next launch. The rebuild was never the obstacle — the UI-scale slider has done it since K99 — so `_resample_display_scale` takes that route, including raising `fonts_dirty` so the atlas is re-baked *between* frames rather than under the `ImFont` handles `_build_ui` is holding. Both `WINDOWDISPLAYCHANGED` and `WINDOWMOVED` trigger it, because a display whose own scale is changed in Windows' settings raises the first for nobody. **The zoom is re-read from settings, never divided back out of the scale in force**: `set_scale` clamps the *product*, so on a 250% display a stored 2.0x is drawn at 1.6x, and recovering it by division would bake that clamp in and shrink the UI again on every crossing.

**Layer groups are a parallel tree over the flat stack, and the stack stays authoritative.** `studio/inker/groups.py` holds `GroupNode` and two dictionaries on the document -- `groups` (uid -> node) and `group_of` (member uid -> the group it is in, absent meaning root) -- and nothing about paint order changes: composite slicing, the `_below` cache's index arithmetic, "a track index and a stack index are the same number", `_materialize_frame` and the `stack_region` kernel's contract are all untouched, because making the stack a real tree would have rewritten every one of them for a feature that is about organising layers rather than painting them. One invariant makes it sound: **a group's leaves are contiguous in stack order and spans nest** -- that is what makes a group a slice of the painter's order rather than a scattering of it. The ops maintain it (create refuses a non-contiguous run rather than silently reordering somebody's stack; delete, reorder, merge and move-into all carry membership with them) and pure `groups.check()` asserts it in the tests; the compositor never asks. **Every op that inserts a row above the active one must inherit that row's group** -- `add_layer`, `duplicate_layer`, `paste_as_layer` and `layer_from_selection` -- and they share `inherit_group_edits` for it, which takes the member uid from `member_uids()[active_index]` rather than from a parameter precisely so a new call site cannot forget to pass one. A row inserted mid-span that does not join the group splits it, and the invariant is then false with nothing to say so; that is exactly what pasting inside a folder did before the two `_add_layer_edit` callers were fixed. **Empty groups are disallowed** -- one has no span and composites nothing -- so a group is created *around* existing rows and the last member leaving dissolves it, upwards. Compositing is **pass-through**: `groups.resolve` folds visibility (AND), opacity (multiply) and the content lock (OR) down the ancestry into one triple, and `LayerStack._entries` applies it per row, so by the time `composite.stack_region` sees a layer it is an ordinary triple that knows nothing about a tree. `group_fold` is `None` on a document with no groups, which is every document until somebody makes one. **Isolated** group compositing -- rendering a group to its own buffer so a group-level blend mode could mean something -- is deliberately not in v1 and is named in the docstring and the manual rather than left to be discovered. **Membership is keyed on the *track* uid on an animated document** (`Document.member_uids`), never on the materialised layer's: an empty slot materialises as a placeholder carrying a per-slot uid, so keying on `layer.uid` would un-group every empty cel -- invisibly, right up until somebody drew on one and a drawing appeared inside a hidden group. Undo is four uid-addressed cost-0 edits (`GroupAddEdit`, `GroupDissolveEdit`, `GroupPropsEdit`, `MembershipEdit`), with a membership change travelling in a `CompoundEdit` beside the stack move that keeps the span contiguous; moving a group into its own subtree is refused **by name** (`groups.descends_from`) rather than by letting `ancestry` quietly stop at the repeat. `ReplayEdit` carries a copy of the tree for the one op that rewrites it, `flatten_layers`. Persistence is the format's own: nested `<stack>` elements in `stack.xml` (the reader builds the tree while still flattening into the authoritative list, so a Krita group survives instead of being dropped, and unmodelled attributes are logged rather than guessed; group construction is suppressed outright when the root carries `frame:NNNN` stacks, because reaching the flat read with those present means the animated read fell back and those elements are *frames*, so building folders from them turned a degraded path into a noisy one), nested inside each frame group on an animated document, with the *record* an additive `"groups"` key in `animation.json` guarded exactly like `layout` -- malformed costs the folders, never the grid. Written only when there is a group, so an ungrouped document's file is byte-identical to what it was. Collapse state is view: `InkerDoc.collapsed_groups`, on the tab, never persisted and never undoable.

**The Troupe programme's plan file is retired and these entries are the record.** `LPC_ALT.md` was deleted on 2026-08-21 with phases 0a-0d and 1-5 shipped; what those phases established is the six entries that follow, its measured ULPC facts are passing oracles in `studio/troupe/ulpc.py`, and its open phases (0e, 6, 7, 8) moved to `TODO.md`. Its citations were rewritten to name the programme rather than the file, the same way the UI redesign's were.

**Troupe's legacy frame table is held twice; every configurable sheet carries its own resolved v2 snapshot.** `studio/troupe/spec.py` (over `data/layout.json`, versioned) and `pipelines/charsheet.py` retain the immutable five-animation/eight-direction table so jobs and sidecars written before v2 keep their original meaning, while `charsheet.resolve_layout` is the authority for new 1/4/8/16-direction, per-movement frame tables. The request is resolved at the service door, copied through the approval chain, revalidated by the worker, and written additively as the sidecar's `troupe` block. Troupe preview reads that selected-sheet snapshot and falls back to the legacy Studio table only when the block is absent; Inker continues to read the ordinary `animation` block. **Cell order remains grouped by `(animation, direction)` and dense** -- grouped because an Inker `Tag` is a contiguous span and timeline frame *i* is cell *i*, dense so a partial final row is the only padding. A sheet warns above 256 cells and is refused above 512 independently of the atlas-size ceiling.

**A clip is an ordered keyframe list, and root translation is interpolated rather than refused.** `sheet.interpolate_clip` takes keys plus per-segment frame counts and an easing; `interpolate(a, b, frames)` is the two-key door onto the same `_expand`. **Cyclic** clips (Idle, Walk, Run) have one segment per key, the last wrapping, and no segment lands on its far key because that key is the next segment's frame 0 -- the seam rule the two-key version always had. **One-shots** (Attack, Jump) have one segment per gap plus one final frame landing exactly on the last key, because a one-shot's last frame is a pose it holds rather than a seam it hides. The old refusal of an endpoint carrying a `root_translation` was a correctness guard against a disagreement that no longer exists: `rigging.root_offset_world`, `queue._sheet_root_offsets` (keyed `(pose id, frame)`, already per frame) and `op_sheet`'s per-cell `root_offset` were all clip-shaped already, and the guard was costing a walk cycle the one thing it needs most -- a vertical bob. The offset is emitted on **every frame of a clip whose keys carry one and no frame of one whose keys do not**: per clip, not per frame, because a bob passes through zero at its midpoint and a frame that dropped the key there would read as "no opinion" rather than "at rest height" -- and because an offsetless clip then produces the byte-identical records it always did, which is what keeps `_sheet_root_offsets` short-circuiting. The shipped library is `templates/clips/humanoid.json`, loaded by `rigging.clip_library`/`clip_keys` on `_load_pose_library`'s rules (a clip naming a pose the file lacks costs that library, not the app).

**The sidecar's `animation` block has two writers and one format.** It was defined in `sheet.sidecar` from the start and populated only by the Inker exporter, so a *rendered* sheet reached an engine as frame indices with no fps and no loop tags -- the one thing the renderer knew and the importer could not guess. `charsheet.animation_block` is the second writer and deliberately emits the same keys with the same spellings, `repeat: 1` for a play-once tag and omitted otherwise, so `version: 1` cannot come to mean two documents. On the way back in, `sheetin.span_tags` is the general form of `walk_tags` (which is now three lines of delegation to it) and `sheetin.document_from_sheet` is the door for a sheet whose sidecar carries the block -- `layout` is **None** there, `document_from_grid`'s reason: a `DirectionalLayout` is the claim that these cells are four named directions of a fixed grid, and eight directions across five animations is not that. The tags carry the directions instead, which is what playback reads.

**The pixeliser is arithmetic, and its order is the design.** `pipelines/pixelize.py` is the AI-free path: reduce whole-atlas alpha-weighted, snap alpha, map to a **designed** palette, then clean orphans and outline **per cell**. Whole-atlas reduction so a cell boundary is in the same place computed from either side (`spritesynth.reduce_atlas`'s argument); alpha-weighted because a plain RGBA mean averages the transparent background's black into every silhouette edge, which is a dark fringe on every sprite and the exact "muddy" failure the program exists to escape; a designed ramp rather than `quantize_shared`'s MEDIANCUT because median-cut picks the colours the render happens to contain, and the palette map is what puts back the contrast the box mean flattened. The last two passes are per cell because a dense atlas has **no gutter**: a sprite touching its cell edge would otherwise be outlined against its neighbour. An outline is one pixel, one colour from the ramp (`darkest`), four-neighbour -- eight puts a pixel diagonally off a corner, which reads as a stray -- and `inner` never changes a silhouette while `outer` grows it by a pixel and can clip at a cell edge, so `outer` is opt-in. The chain has no RNG, so **byte-identical run to run** is the bar, not a tolerance. Beside it, `_setup_render(taa_samples=1)` on the flat sheet path: an emission render has no noise for TAA to average away (`op_views`' own argument), and antialiasing a native low-res render is a shrunk render rather than pixel art. A *lit* sheet keeps Blender's default, because it does have noise.

**A stored joint rotation is in one of two frames, and they are not interchangeable.** `node` is what the pose editor saves and what every shipped *pose* carries: the joint's orientation in the glTF node frame, **absolute relative to its parent joint**. Identity there does not mean "at rest", it means "aligned with the parent", which is why `blender_worker._rest_local_rotation` has to be divided back out. `delta` is a rotation **from the bone's own rest orientation** -- exactly what Blender's pose basis already is, so it applies with no correction. **Clips are authored in `delta`**, for two reasons: an author thinks in it ("swing the thigh forward 24 degrees"), and it is the only one of the two that survives a *re-fit*. A node-local value bakes in the rest orientation of the skeleton it was authored against, so the same numbers on a rig whose joints were measured off the mesh rather than scaled to its bbox produce a different -- in practice, a spectacularly broken -- pose. This was found the only way it could be: by rendering the shipped walk clip and looking at it, whereupon the character was lying down. `sheet.POSE_SPACES` and `blender_worker.POSE_SPACES` hold the same pair and `tests/troupe/test_charsheet.py` checks they agree; the space rides the record (`space`) and the cell (`pose_space`), **emitted only when it is not `node`**, so every pose row is the byte-identical cell it always was. In `delta`, **local X is the swing axis for both `thigh` and `upper_arm`** -- measured, not assumed -- and the shipped clips carry a constant -58 degrees about it on the arms, because a base mesh is authored with the arms out and every animation wants them down.

**Joints are measured off the mesh when the mesh is not in the template's pose.** `rigging.fit_template` is bbox-proportional and the shipped humanoid template is a fairly steep **A-pose**. A user's base mesh is as likely to be a **T-pose**, and on one the fitted arm chain runs diagonally down through the ribcage while the arm geometry lies out at full reach -- so automatic weights bind the arms to the chest and every clip renders a figure whose arms barely move. Nothing in the fit can detect that, which is what makes it worth a second source: `pipelines/jointfit.py`, pure numpy over a vertex cloud, reached by `rig_spec(joints="measured")` and applied by `op_rig` into `spec["bones"]` so that from `_rig_bones` onward it is indistinguishable from a user's hand correction -- which is what it is, taken automatically. It sits *below* a real correction and *above* the template in the same order of preference. The arm is found by **fitting a line to the outer half of the reach and extrapolating inward**, never by slicing all the way in: around the armpit one slab holds arm and ribcage at once and their mean is neither, which puts the elbow inside the chest. **Y is pinned to zero along the arm**, because a shoulder measured a few centimetres forward tilts the bone and a swing about its local X then carries the arm forward as well as down -- a character walking with its arms folded across its chest. A mesh no wider than its own hips is **refused by name** rather than measured, since the fit would otherwise trace a leg and call it an arm, and a rig with the shoulders bound to the hips looks perfectly well-formed in `rig.json`.

**A character sheet's atlas exists only at the logical size, so the supersample reduction is per frame.** The program renders at 512 and reduces, and a 256-cell sheet packed at 512 would be 4096x16384 against `MAX_ATLAS_PX` 8192 -- `check_atlas_size` refuses it, correctly, because no engine would load it. `pixelize.reduce_frames` is therefore not an optimisation but the only route: it reduces the rendered cell PNGs before `sheet.pack` sees them. Per frame costs nothing that whole-atlas reduction was buying, because the boundary argument (`spritesynth.reduce_atlas`) is about targets that do not divide the source and every frame reduces to exactly the cell size. It also keeps `sheet.pack` off the pixel-art path: that function resizes a mismatched frame with **LANCZOS**, which is right for a preview and wrong for a sprite -- a filtered downscale is the soft, fringed result the alpha snap then has to guess about.

**The Troupe chain is four ordinary jobs and one human gate, not an orchestrator.** Phase 4 is `reference -> gate -> mesh -> rig -> charsheet`, and every link but the last already existed. The request rides on the reference row as a nested `troupe` block -- the shape `rig` and `sprite_sheet` already use, and nested so `VECTOR_PARAMS` (an allowlist of *flat* settings) cannot pick any of it up by accident. It is validated at the *reference's* door (`service.troupe.check_troupe`, called from `_jobs_create`), because the follow-up rows are minted by the **worker**, which can refuse nothing: a bad palette or an unrigged template discovered there would be a refusal an hour later on a row the user never submitted. It survives `promote_to_model` (which copies everything not derived and not conditioning) because the promoted row is exactly the one that has to honour it, and `_maybe_queue_charsheet` fires on `stage == "model"` -- **after** `_maybe_queue_rig` in the same `finally`, which is the whole ordering: the queue is serial and FIFO, so the rig row finishes before the sheet row is claimed. A missing rig is still refused in `_charsheet` rather than assumed, because the user can cancel the rig on its own; that two-row shape is what makes the cancel meaningful. `warlock/clips.py` exists for `vectors.py`'s reason exactly -- the worker may not import `service` and the door may not reimplement the worker, so the one function that joins the shipped clip library to the frame table lives in neither.

**The T-pose guide is drawn, never detected, and it is drawn by the worker.** The reference stage conditions on `spritesynth.render_tpose_guide`, fed to the ControlNet **directly**: it is already line art in canny space, and running the detector over it returns the outline of each stroke -- two lines where the guide means one, which is `render_guide`'s documented "why does my character have four legs" failure. `_conditioning` recognises it by `control_hint_source == "guide"` and *renders* it rather than reading a file the door wrote, because a rerolled reference is a new job id with an empty directory and would otherwise fail on a file that belongs to the row it rerolled; a guide is a pure function of its variant, so redrawing is free and keeps `params` the whole description of the run. `guide_variant`/`control_hint_source` are in `CONDITIONING_PARAMS`, so a promotion or a remesh -- image jobs where SDXL never runs -- do not carry a guide describing a run that cannot happen. `TPOSE_GEOMETRY` is deliberately **not** in `spritesynth.GEOMETRY`: `tests/test_sprite_geometry_agreement.py` owns the claim that `GEOMETRY`, `SHEET_TYPES` and `inker.animation.SHEET_KINDS` name one set of *sprite sheet kinds*, and a T-pose reference is not one. Why a constrained T-pose at all: both the reconstruction and the bbox-proportional `fit_template` are far more reliable against one, and limb separation is the failure a single-view reconstruction cannot recover from.

**A character sheet is an ordinary sheet, and that is what buys every way out of it.** `_q_troupe` publishes `sheets/<id>.png` plus its sidecar into the *source* job's directory -- the same pair `_sheet` writes, with `charsheet.animation_block()` on it -- so Inker's grid slicer, Packwright's sheet adder, the exporters and the library all read it with no new code. Nothing about it is a format of its own; what makes it a Troupe sheet is the frame table it was laid out on. The pixel-art pass runs over the **packed atlas** rather than per cell: one nearest search instead of 256, and one palette across every cell, which is what stops the same shirt coming out two shades in two directions. A designed palette and a median-cut one both arrive at `pixelize_atlas` as an ordinary palette, so a derived sheet is not a second code path with its own bugs. The un-quantised render is staged as `.<name>.render` beside the served atlas and unlinked after the publish -- the served file is never briefly the un-quantised one, because the library would thumbnail it in the state nobody asked for -- and `_discard_artifacts` names it off the sheet id the door minted, so a cancel takes this run's atlas and no earlier one of the same character.

**Troupe is the one workspace mode that holds no document, and its omissions are deliberate.** No journal provider, no `palette._DOC_MODES` entry, no undo stack, and entering it from Home creates nothing: it is a *selection* over sheets a worker published, so a Save would have nothing to write and a recovery nothing to recover. `tests/troupe/test_troupe_mode.py` asserts both absences, so adding one is a deliberate act rather than a drift. The preview is a **clock, not a frame counter** -- durations are milliseconds per movement and the window runs at whatever rate it runs at, so stepping one cell per draw would play a 60 ms run cycle at monitor speed on one machine and half that on another; `advance` loops rather than branches, so a slow frame skips cells instead of falling behind forever. Which cell is on screen comes from the selected sidecar's explicit movement/direction runs (or the immutable legacy table), never from recomputing offsets in the pane. The sprite is drawn at an **integer** scale through a **NEAREST** filter: a sprite drawn at 6.3x through a linear filter is the blurred result the whole pipeline exists not to produce, and drawing it that way in the one place a user judges it would be the pipeline lying about its own output.

**The guided tour points and waits; it never acts for the reader.** `studio/tour/` is the steps and the tours, pure the way `inker`, `clay`, `plotter` and `packwright` are and stricter -- *no* outward imports, so every rule about a tour is assertable with no window (`tests/tour/`). `studio/panes/tour.py` draws, from `App._overlays`, above the Manual (a step's "Read more" raises the reference over the card that offered it) and below the palette (Ctrl+K is how you leave anywhere). It is deliberately **not** in `App._modal_open`: point-and-wait means the app underneath stays live and clickable, and a tour that took the keyboard would be a modal wearing another name. A step's waiting condition is a **named** predicate plus an argument rather than a callable -- a closure would drag imgui and `service` into the pure half -- and `CONDITIONS` and the evaluator's `HANDLED` are asserted against each other in both directions, because an unanswered name reads as "never satisfied", which on a point-and-wait step is a tour that hangs on a screen where the reader has already done the thing. **The scrim is not a mask and cannot be**: `ImDrawData` is a flat list of command lists with no window provenance (`vibrancy`'s own reason), so it fills the viewport *around* holes -- and it needs two, because the foreground draw list paints above every window and one hole leaves the tour dimming its own card. The card's rect is one frame stale for `window_shadow`'s reason: an auto-height window has no rectangle until it has drawn. The scrim takes no input because it is draw-list geometry rather than a window, so there is nothing there to hit. **`anchors` is `layout.FRAME_PANES` one level down** -- where a named *control* ended up, marked by the pane that drew it and read by the overlay afterwards, cleared beside `FRAME_PANES`; positions are read rather than computed because the rail recomputes every item's box each frame across the compression ladder below, so arithmetic would drift the moment a window got short. `TOUR_VEIL`/`TOUR_RING` are in all three palettes and their ratchet measures the **blend**, since the scrim is translucent and contrast is a property of the glass -- the light ring failed that check as first written and was deepened until it cleared the control boundary on its own scrim.

**Adding a mode can run the rail out of the window, and the ladder has a third rung now.** `rail.draw` gives up the row-to-row spacing first, then compresses the rows down to `MIN_ITEM_H` -- 24 design px, a floor with a citation and not a number picked to make the arithmetic come out. Troupe made the rail fifteen rows, which at the resize floor on a 175% display wants 23.85 px a row: four physical pixels of overflow, and four physical pixels of overflow is a mode nobody can click. So the **section gaps** compress too, last of all, because a section marker is air and air is what this ladder spends first. `tests/test_studio_smoke.py::test_the_rail_fits_the_resize_floor_at_every_scale` is what catches the next one.

**A drag-drop source over a label needs `SourceAllowNullID`, and a pane that raises hides why.** `imgui.text` adds its item with id **0**, and `BeginDragDropSource` asserts outright on a null id unless told to derive one from the item's rectangle. The Inker timeline's `_reorder` drags a layer by its *name*, which is exactly that -- so it asserted, and reaching the assert needed only the mouse held plus either the row hovered or anything else in the same window holding the active id. Holding a timeline cell or frame header is that second condition, which made it a live crash on an ordinary click rather than a dormant edge case, and meant layer drag-reorder had never once worked: the gesture it exists for takes the path that asserted. The **symptom named neither the row nor the reason**, and that is the part worth remembering: the exception unwound through `layout.pane`'s `finally`, whose `end_child` asserted in turn on the now-unbalanced frame, so what surfaced was `Missing PopID()` in `inker-timeline-grid`. A pane that raises therefore reports the *rescue's* failure rather than its own -- finding the real error meant wrapping the pane function to catch it first. The assert is native and aborts the frame rather than raising something a refactor would notice, so the flag is pinned by `tests/inker/test_timeline_reorder.py` directly, alongside the assumption it rests on (all three label branches draw text, not an id-bearing control). It was found by `scripts/exercise_mode.py`, which is the only thing in the repo that presses a button, and it is the class of defect that pass exists for: the smoke suite asserts the pane *builds*, and it does.

**The control probe is env-gated, read-only, and derived from one chokepoint.** `studio/probe.py` is `anchors` one step further out: a per-frame census of *every* control a frame submitted -- label, kind, rect, enabled, reason, selected, visibility, and the `layout.FRAME_PANES` slot it landed in -- so a driver can address one and click it. It exists because the smoke suite asserts only that a pane *builds* and `screenshot_modes.py` photographs a mode *at rest*, which between them let a control that draws correctly and does nothing pass both. The census is **derived**, not hand-kept: `controls._finish_item` is the one door every studio button, field, choice, row and menu item passes through, so recording there cannot fall behind the UI -- the same rule `screenshot_modes.py` follows deriving its mode list from `modes.KEYS`, and `tests/test_probe.py` asserts every call site names a `kind`, since an untyped row is a row nothing can press. `ENABLED` is sampled **once, at import** (`WARLOCK_UI_PROBE`), so an ordinary run pays one attribute lookup per control and nothing else -- re-reading the environment per call would be most of the cost the gate exists to avoid. Positions are **read** from imgui rather than computed, for the reason `anchors` gives one level down. Cleared beside `FRAME_PANES` and `FRAME_ANCHORS`, because a stale census points a click at whatever took the control's place. It lives in `studio/` and imports imgui, so it stays out of `inker/`, `clay/`, `plotter/` and `packwright/`. The controls that call imgui directly -- ten of them, all in `widgets.py` -- are invisible to it; the number is **pinned by a test and reported by the driver** rather than chased, because a coverage line that omits a known blind spot is the same lie as a silent cap. What counts is what the driver cannot *reach*, not what avoided an import: a raw call inside a function that hands the census its own row is excluded, and `widgets._button_with_note` is exactly that case -- it draws with `imgui.button` because `primary_button` and `ghost_button` push their own fill around it, and a role-styled button would paint over them. It went unrecorded for its whole life, and took `disabled_button` with it: the **only** helper in the app that carries a `reason`, so the `disabled-no-reason` verdict had nothing anywhere it could fire on, and Troupe's two primary actions were absent from its census entirely. It records itself now, *before* `set_tooltip` -- a tooltip is an item of its own and overwrites the rect being read, which is the same hazard that function already existed to work around.

**The exercise driver clicks for real and runs nothing real.** `scripts/exercise_mode.py` presses each censused control by **posting pygame events** into `App.frame` -> `_events`, never by calling a handler: calling the handler would prove the handler works while saying nothing about whether anything on screen reaches it, which is the entire question. Exactly two things are neutralised -- `TaskRunner.submit` on the *instance* (one patch covers `ctx.submit` and every direct caller, which is what the single-door invariant buys), and a short explicit refusal list for Quit and the controls that open an OS file dialog, since that is a message pump owned by the OS and it hangs the frame loop rather than failing it. Every press is classified by a pure `verdict` in severity order, so a reader looks at the pictures that need judging and not the rest. After each press the driver undoes back to the baseline digest and **re-seeds if it cannot** -- an action that cannot be undone is itself a finding, recorded as `hard-reset` rather than allowed to leak into the next control. The frontier walk repeats until a round finds nothing new (a control behind a tab, a header or a menu is not in the first census) and **reports what its bound dropped**. `scripts/_appharness.py` holds the boot, warmup, settle, seeding and popup teardown for this and for `screenshot_modes.py` both: two scripts booting the app two ways is the drift the derived mode list exists to prevent. Needs a real window, and must not run while `pytest` does.

## Stack

- Python 3.12+, uv-managed (`uv sync --extra studio` — dev tooling is a default dependency group, not an extra; `uv run pytest`, `uv run ruff check .`). No web framework: `httpx` remains only because `trellis-server.exe` speaks HTTP on a local port.
- **The suite pins the machine it runs on, and the gpu lane is the one exception.** `tests/conftest.py` pins `WARLOCK_HOME` at a session tmp dir and `memlog.system_memory` at a roomy constant — the home pin session-autouse, the memory pin autouse at *function* scope, and that scope is load-bearing: applied fresh per test, a test's own `monkeypatch` lands after it and wins, which is how `test_queue.py`'s refusal cases patch a scarce reading over it. This is a correctness rule before it is a speed one: every config root resolves under `_home()` unless its own variable is set, so thirteen test files that built a bare `Config` were reading the developer's real `~/.warlock/models` — and with birefnet downloaded there, `matting.mask` ran a real CPU inference and produced **a different matte than the same test on a machine without the weights**. The memory pin is the same shape: `queue._require_commit_headroom` refuses a dispatch on real host commit, so without it the queue tests grade the developer's other windows (the 2026-08-16 pre-parallel baseline failed nine that way, none about memory — and the suite was its own worst offender, since each BiRefNet load leaves ~1 GiB resident). It is pinned at `memlog.system_memory` rather than at `queue.commit_fraction` because that is the single reader *both* branches of the check share. **The gpu lane opts out of both** (`_selects_gpu`): those tests resolve checkpoints through `get_config().t2i_model_root` and `pytest.skip` when the files are absent, so a pinned home would turn the whole lane into eighteen green skips that loaded nothing — the silent pass the pin exists to prevent, reintroduced one level up. A negated mention (`not gpu`, the default expression) must not count as selecting the lane.
- **Parallel by default, and the worker count is measured, not `auto`.** `-n 8 --dist loadfile`. `loadfile` is load-bearing rather than tuning: it keeps a file's tests on one worker, which preserves "at most one imgui context alive at a time" (upheld by within-file ordering — two contexts over the one GL context crash the process) and the module-level `_CACHE.clear()` couplings. Eight because the curve flattens there and then reverses — 4→71.9 s, 8→54.2 s, 16→59.6 s, 24 (`auto`)→64.1 s on a 24-core box: past the knee the run pays for redundant interpreter startup, so `auto` is both slower than eight and less safe (at 24 workers it intermittently failed on memory — an 8192² f32 canvas is 1 GiB by design and this host idles at 53 of 69.5 GiB committed). Tests asserting a wall-clock budget carry `@pytest.mark.perf` and are excluded, because a budget measured under contention measures the scheduler.
- Optional native kernels: `pwsh native\build.ps1` → `vendor/warlockc/warlockc.dll` (MSVC, clang or zig; the script picks). Not needed to run anything — see the native-kernel invariant above.
- `trellis-server.exe` is a vendored compiled binary (`vendor/`), not a Python package — confirm behavior against its actual CLI help / exposed routes, not against the upstream TRELLIS project's docs
- **An image model is a registry spec, not a directory.** `models.py` owns `BASE_MODELS` / `STYLE_LORAS`: a `BaseModel` carries its dir name *and* the settings it must run at (size, steps, guidance, variant, scheduler, always-on step-distillation LoRA), because those are properties of the checkpoint — a 4-step distilled model run at 25 steps with CFG produces mush, and Hyper-SD silently degrades without trailing timestep spacing. Add a model there, never by hardcoding constants in `pipelines/text2image.py`. `guidance.py` validates and stores the selection but deliberately keeps model keys and LoRA trigger words out of the composed prompt — triggers are prepended next to `PROMPT_TEMPLATE` instead. **`family` and `residency` are declared, never sniffed.** `family` (`sdxl` | `flux2klein`) decides which encode/sample path runs — `_sample_sdxl` chunk-encodes through two CLIPs and passes pooled embeddings, `_sample_flux2` passes one 512-token string and no pooled anything — and it is stated rather than derived so a future checkpoint cannot become "SDXL-shaped" because it happened to ship a `unet/`. Everything SDXL-specific is refused on another family rather than degraded, at the door and again at the pipe: conditioning (`_conditioned`) and seamless tiles (`tile_bases()`, `create_job`) — a tile is the sharpest case, because circular padding is a `Conv2d` property and a DiT has none, so patching only the VAE would yield an image that looks seamless in a thumbnail and seams in a material. `residency` picks `.to("cuda")` or `enable_model_cpu_offload()` and the two are mutually exclusive; `vram_gib` is the peak *under that residency*, which is what `vram.estimate` charges a text job (falling back to `SDXL_GIB` for a base the registry no longer carries, the tolerance `Worker._resolve_base_key` owns for all four t2i stages, and which `_retexture` gained on 2026-08-11 — it fell straight to the registry default with no config retry and said nothing). `probe` overrides doctor's `unet/`-shaped presence formula for a layout that has no unet. **A style LoRA is the one of those three that is a *pair* rather than an architecture rule**, and `StyleLora.family` is what makes it one: `models.lora_fits` is the single owner, `loras_by_base()` is what the picker lists, and `lora_bases()` keeps its name and meaning while changing its derivation — no longer "is this SDXL" but "does the registry hold an adapter fitted to this architecture". Sharing that derivation with `loras_by_base` is right precisely where `tile_bases()`' separate spelling is: tiles are a second question with a coincidentally equal answer (and the two lists now genuinely differ), whereas `lora_bases` is the same question asking whether the map's entry is non-empty. Every consumer is that one function — `guidance.normalize`'s refusal (whose `field` names `style_lora` when some adapter fits the base and `base_model` when none does, because a highlight must name the control the sentence mentions), `_load_loras`' per-adapter filter, `settings_2d.lora_options`/`clear_unusable`, `queue`'s log-and-drop — so they cannot come to disagree about a pair. **Style adapters attach on selection, not at load.** `_load_loras` takes the required step-distillation LoRA only (fatal — a 4-step base without it is noise); a style is attached by `_ensure_adapter` at the moment a job picks it, once per pipe. Three failures were one design: a corrupt optional file raised *inside* the load transaction and made the whole checkpoint unusable over a style nobody selected; the adapter set was frozen for the pipe's life, so a LoRA installed in Settings between two jobs was dropped from the second with a wrong "not downloaded" warning while the row recorded it anyway (and `style_lora` is in `VECTOR_PARAMS`, so that row became corpus evidence about a style that never ran); and every fitting adapter's weights sat on the device uncounted. A selected style that will not load is now a **refusal**, never a warning — the one exception is an adapter fitted to another architecture, which is dropped with a warning because it is reachable only from a stored job the user can no longer edit. Belt and braces behind it: `fetch.bump_store_generation()` on every successful download/uninstall, and `Worker._evict_stale_t2i` rebuilds a pipe whose generation has moved. **And they detach on deselection**: `_apply_adapters` calls `delete_adapters` on every style the job's selection no longer includes, before enabling the active set — so residency is bounded at the base adapter plus at most one style, and *attached equals what `vram._adapter_cost` priced* (the unbounded-accumulation half of MDL-17; before this, every style picked during a pipe's life stayed resident in host and VRAM until full unload, which is also where the peft "Already found a peft_config" warning came from). `BASE_LORA_ADAPTER` is structurally undeletable — it lives in `_base_adapter`, never in `_adapters`. A failed delete is a logged warning with truthful bookkeeping: `_adapters` shrinks only on success, so the reload guard keeps skipping still-attached adapters and the delete retries on the next apply. `vram._adapter_cost` prices what is actually attached — the base LoRA always, the selected style if it fits — because `BaseModel.vram_gib` is the checkpoint alone and under-pricing is the direction its own comment forbids. **`_apply_adapters` is gated on "is there anything to disable", never on family**: the family early return it replaced was wrong for SDXL too (`turbo` has no base LoRA, so a host with no `loras/` called `disable_lora()` on a pipe with no PEFT state). And it must call **`enable_lora()` before `set_adapters()`** — `disable_lora()` sets `_disable_adapters` on every PEFT layer and `set_adapters()` writes only the scaling, so one job generated without a style silently switched every later job in the process to no style, for the life of the resident pipe. It reads as working because the trigger words are still prepended, which is exactly how it survived: the output *does* change when a style is picked, it just is not the adapter doing it (`docs/measurements/2026-08-10-pixel-art-klein.md`). A `default_weight` is a multiplier on the scale the adapter's own metadata declares, so an entry trained with `use_rslora` (α/√r = 16 rather than α/r ≈ 2) needs a default an order of magnitude smaller and it is *measured*, never taken from a model card.
- **A t2i stage gives its checkpoint back when it ends, and the card was never the binding constraint.** Coexist used to keep the image pipe resident between jobs -- the card had room for it beside trellis, so why pay a reload -- and only the 600 s idle sweep dropped it. The *host* is what refuses the next job: `_require_commit_headroom` tests a percentage of **system-wide** commit, and a resident ~7 GiB pipe charges that commit through its WDDM backing and its own arenas for the whole timeout after a job that has finished. Measured 2026-08-21 on the developer's machine: 63.5 GiB RAM, a 14.2 GiB pagefile, so a 77.7 GiB commit limit -- sitting at 96% and refusing everything **with 24 GiB of RAM free**. Both teardown families unload now (`Worker._release_t2i` unconditionally; `_generate`'s coexist branch, which used to `trim()` only) and neither clears `_text2image`: `.loaded` is what every reader asks and `load()` rebuilds from `_pipe is None`, so the object is a reusable cache entry rather than stale. The cost is one reload per job, accepted deliberately. `_evict_stale_t2i` therefore skips a pipe that is already unloaded -- otherwise "the switch unloads the previous pipe exactly once" becomes twice, for a thread hop and a lease that free nothing.
- **One base model resident at a time.** The 32 GB card holds trellis (~16 GB) plus a single SDXL-class pipe (~7 GB) and not two, so a stale pipe (wrong base, or wrong store generation) is unloaded (via `asyncio.to_thread`) before the next builds — the unload now happens in `Worker._acquire_t2i` via `_evict_stale_t2i` *before* the commit-headroom check, so the outgoing weights are not charged against the incoming ones; `_get_text2image` keeps the same call as a backstop so the staleness predicate has one owner. The MDL-05 generation rebuild now passes through the bytes check too (it was previously unchecked, because the check keyed on the base key alone). Style LoRAs are the opposite: adapters on the resident pipe, switched per job with `set_adapters()` and no reload. A `residency="offload"` spec is the one that is *not* fully resident: FLUX.2 klein is ~16 GB whole, which would put coexist out of reach, so it streams one submodule at a time and peaks near its recorded 10 GiB — slower per job, but it displaces nothing.
- **SDXL 1.0 at full CFG (`sdxl_cfg`, `models/sdxl-base-1.0`) is the default, and it is measured rather than preferred.** In the 2026-08-09 re-baseline `base_model=turbo` took 1 of 5 — the weakest checkpoint that produced data — while `sdxl_cfg` took 3 of 3; the two rank oppositely on refusals and on quality, and the default picks quality (`docs/measurements/2026-08-11-default-base-model.md`). SDXL-Turbo (`models/sdxl-turbo`) is now the *fast* option and a separate optional download. Do not restate the old causal story: "because FLUX is gated" was never why the default is what it is — FLUX being gated (**both `dev` and `schnell`**, and 12B params won't coexist with trellis anyway) is only why no FLUX entry is the blessed download. The distribution half matters too: one 7 GiB `stable-diffusion-xl-base-1.0` download is shared by four recipes (`sdxl`, `sdxl_cfg`, `pixel`, `lightning`), so blessing this entry unlocks the speed recipes for a small LoRA each. All weights are one-time manual `hf download`s under `WARLOCK_T2I_ROOT` (default `~/.warlock/models`); `WARLOCK_T2I_DIR` still relocates the `turbo` entry only. See `pipelines/text2image.py`
- **One Warlock per home, and the OS says so.** `instance.InstanceLock` takes an exclusive OS-level lock on `<home>/instance.lock` in `studio.main.run()` *before* `migrate` is even imported (importing it performs the one-time move), before the store opens and before the engine port is claimed; losing it is a native dialog and exit 1, not a log line. Three properties the `session.marker` it replaces could not have: a crash releases it, it cannot be won twice, and it stops startup rather than annotating it. The marker stays, for crash attribution only. The lock file is deliberately **empty** — Windows locks the byte range, so anything written into it cannot be read while held. Doctor's `single instance` row asks `instance.held_by_us()` first, because re-locking one file from one process proves nothing. **Executable identity is not proof a listener is ours.** Trellis port reclamation additionally requires a *claim* — `<data_dir>/trellis-<port>.owner`, written at spawn and removed at a confirmed stop — whose recorded owner pid is gone. The exe path is a property of the install, so two homes sharing one binary and the default port 17971 would otherwise let the second instance terminate the first's live server and log the kill as an orphan cleanup. **The migration holds its exclusive `BEGIN EXCLUSIVE` across the copy**, not for the instant before it: the check used to `ROLLBACK` and close, then start a multi-minute cross-volume `copytree` with no guarantee at all. The `rmtree` runs *after* the hold is released, because the open handle is on a `jobs.sqlite` inside the tree being deleted and Windows will not unlink it. **A `done` job may be degraded, and says so.** Normalization and the mesh report stay non-fatal — the reconstruction is on disk and usable — but a swallowed failure is recorded under `validation.ARTIFACT_HEALTH` (`"degraded"`), which is in `DERIVED_PARAMS` so a reroll starts clean, and is surfaced by `export.degraded_ids`. The key is spelled twice (`_q_mesh` and `service.validation`) because queue may not import service; a test pins the pair. **The queue fakes emit a real PNG and a real GLB.** Marker bytes meant every post-processing step raised and was swallowed, so a happy-path `done` assertion and a degraded-path one tested the same thing; the fake image carries an actual subject because a flat one is correctly refused as "no subject found". **Malformed `WARLOCK_*` values never raise and never vanish**: `config._env_int`/`_env_float` fall back to the documented default and record into `config.INVALID_ENV`, which Doctor reports as one `environment` row. `get_config()` runs before any log handler exists, so raising there produced a traceback naming `int()` and not the variable.

**A panel section is a surface, and the fill is drawn through a split draw list.** `widgets.section` opens a tinted block that runs to the next heading (or to `widgets.end_section`, or to the end of the scope); `widgets.section_blocks` is the scope, and `layout.pane` opens one around every pane so the ~100 bare `section()` call sites did not change. This reverses half of UX.md Phase 2 — which removed the rule under a heading on the grounds that "a rule is a line drawn where the rhythm failed" — and the grounds still hold: **nothing draws a line**, the block's edge is its fill. What Phase 2 did not account for is that a 300 px sidebar with six headings is not a page with six headings, so the gap between two sections and the gap between a heading and its own first control were the same gap, and which one you were looking at could only be recovered by reading the words.

The mechanism is the part that breaks silently. A block's height is unknown until its content has been drawn and an imgui draw list is append-only, so the scope **splits the current window's draw list into two channels** — content on 1, fills on 0 — and merges on the way out, in a `finally`, because an unbalanced splitter corrupts the list and the frame that *looks* broken is the one after. Two rules follow and both are enforced in code rather than by convention. **One splitter per draw list**: a scope opened over a list that already has one hands back the existing scope and splits nothing, since imgui gives each list a single splitter and a nested split would corrupt it — with the damage surfacing in whichever pane is drawn next, not in the one that caused it. And **the granularity is the child window**: every `begin_child` gets its own draw list, which is exactly why hanging the scope off `layout.pane` is safe and why a child opened inside a section draws above the fill rather than under it. A section drawn with no scope open keeps the old flat heading, which is deliberate and load-bearing — the manual's prose headings and the library's date groups opt out on purpose, each saying so at the call.

**Crash recovery is one mechanism for every authored thing.** `studio/journal.py` (UX-05). Inker had a crash-safe autosave since it shipped and nothing else did — a Clay model, a Plotter map, a Packwright atlas, a pose being authored and a profile draft were each one power cut away from gone. The mechanism Inker proved was right and the whole of what was wrong was that it lived in one mode, so it moved out whole: frame-pumped in *every* mode (a crash while the user is looking at the library still loses the painting), debounced at 120 s **from the pump that first sees the slot** — `Mark.at`'s 0.0 default is a never-seen sentinel and the first observation arms the clock; comparing boot-relative `time.monotonic()` against zero would take the copy on the very first pump after an edit, and a `drop` re-arms the same way — gated on the document's head rather than on a flag (an undo back to the copied position is not a new edit), **encoded on the frame thread** and written on a task (the encoders read the live document, so encoding after a hand-off would capture whatever was drawn in between), through `ctx.submit` whose key-dedupe *is* the backpressure — a slow encode skips a beat rather than queuing.

It is **beside `docmodes`, not in an engine package and not in `service/`**, and both halves of that matter: the engine packages import no imgui, no moderngl and no `service` and this knows about `ctx`; `service/` is about *jobs* — rows in a store, work in a queue, artifacts under a job id — and a document open in a tab is none of those. It follows `docmodes`' import discipline (stdlib-only at module scope, everything else lazy), pinned by a test, because it is called from the frame loop in modes that have loaded none of the editors.

**The mark moves in two halves, at two different moments, and that split is the whole of the journal's honesty.** `journal.write` records `name` and `at` when `ctx.submit` is *accepted* — they are the debounce, and a refused key (the backpressure above) has to be retried on the next pump rather than recorded as done. It records `head` **from the task, after `_write_pair` returns**, because `head` is the field that means "a copy of *this* state is on disk" and until the write lands that is not true. Advancing it on submit recorded a crash copy that a full disk, a yanked directory or a permission change had prevented: the slot read as journalled at the current head, `_write_if_due`'s first gate saw nothing changed, and no retry ever came for the life of the session. **The app believed a crash copy existed when none did** — the one thing it cannot be wrong about, because every other promise the journal makes is conditional on that one. The price is that a failed write retries `JOURNAL_SECONDS` later rather than on the next frame, which is deliberate: a disk that has just refused a write is not a disk to re-encode a document against sixty times a second. Two orderings hold it up. The task re-reads the drop generation under `_drop_lock` before it marks, and `drop` resets the mark **inside** the hold rather than above it — above it, a write already in `_write_pair` lands its mark afterwards and a just-saved document is left believing it holds a copy the unlinks have removed. And the failure is *said*: `main._collect_tasks` claims the `journal:` prefix and names the autosave, because since this change that toast is the only signal the user gets.

**Two files per slot, and the sidecar is the completion gate.** The payload is the mode's own format (`.ora`, `.wblk`, `.wmap`, `.wpack`, `.pose.json`, `.profile.json`), so a crash copy is openable by hand and by the mode's ordinary reader; `<name>.meta.json` beside it names the kind, the title and when it was taken. Both go through tmp+`os.replace`, which makes each file whole or absent — and renaming the sidecar **second** is what makes the *pair* atomic: recovery is driven by sidecars, so a payload without one was interrupted mid-write and is never offered. **Both stagings happen before either rename**, which is not the obvious order and is the one that closes the gap the gate opens: staged-renamed-staged-renamed, a disk that fills between the two puts a *whole* first-ever copy on disk with no sidecar to offer it, so the work is present, permanently invisible and never swept. Staging both first moves the two byte writes to before anything is published and leaves between the renames only two metadata operations on one directory. A sidecar rename that fails anyway leaves the payload **in place**: it is the user's work in the mode's own format, an unoffered file in `autosave/` can still be opened by hand, and deleting somebody's work is this module's stated worst outcome. No sqlite: the store is one connection behind an RLock on the asyncio thread, and a crash copy that needed a lock the crashing thread was holding is a crash copy that is not written. The directory keeps Inker's name (`data_dir/autosave/`) because renaming it would strand every copy sitting in one right now.

A **provider** is six answers and three bookkeeping fields on the slot (`journal_name`/`journal_head`/`journal_at`, Inker's three generalised — on the slot because that is what has a lifetime; a dict here would leak an entry per closed tab and hand a reopened one a stranger's). **All three have to be *declared* on the slot class, and that is a rule with no natural enforcement**: `set_mark` assigns them and `mark_of` reads them back through `getattr` defaults, so a slot that has silently lost one keeps working — with its debounce or its head reset to the default on every launch, and nothing anywhere red. `PlotterDoc.journal_at` went missing exactly that way on 2026-08-18, deleted with the block of ground-set fields it happened to sit above; `tests/test_journal.py` now pins the three against `dataclasses.fields` of each of the four state classes, because a round-trip through `set_mark` is the test that passes either way. `head_of` is anything comparable: an undo serial where there is one, and the encoded payload where there is not (a pose, a profile draft) — the pose provider uses payload equality *despite* now having a history, because the editor's stack is dropped on every rebind and its head would restart. Registration is a side effect of importing a mode, which is right for the pump and wrong for the startup offer — so `ensure_providers()` imports all six and **re-registers from each module's `JOURNAL`**, since the second `import_module` is a cache hit that runs no module body.

**Declining keeps the files** — "not now" is not "delete my work" — and there is deliberately **no age-out**. A kind no provider claims is skipped and logged rather than deleted: the mode may simply not be built into this run, and deleting somebody's work because this build does not understand it is the one outcome worse than not offering it. A pose is the one adopter that can *decline for a reason the user hears*: it is not a document and cannot be opened on its own, so if its rig is not loaded — or the loaded one is a different asset — it says so and keeps the file, because rotations by bone name land on whatever shares a name, silently and wrongly.

**The offer is a section at the top of Home, one row per document, not a modal at startup.** The per-row chooser that 4a deferred is what shipped: `journal.take` reopens one and `journal.discard` deletes one, and the all-or-nothing question is gone. The modal was wrong in three ways and only one of them was the modality — it was all-or-nothing, so a crash that left one document worth keeping and two worth abandoning had no right answer; it was once, so "not now" was indistinguishable from "never" until the next launch; and it arrived in front of a screen the user had not read yet, which is a dialog people dismiss. **What did not change is when the directory is read.** `journal.snapshot` scans on the first frame that has a `Ctx` and never again, holding the result on `AppState.recovery` — where `None` ("not looked") and `[]` ("looked, found nothing") are different answers, because a falsy test would rescan every frame. That one-shot *is* the correctness: the autosave directory is also where this session writes, so a panel that re-read it would begin offering the user their own open documents back. `take` leaves the files on disk for `drop` to clear when the recovered document is saved or closed — deleting them at adoption would remove the copy for exactly the window a second crash would land in. **`MAX_RECOVERY` is a property of the offer, not of the scan.** `recoverable` returns everything; the pane draws eight rows and counts the rest, and a row leaves the snapshot the moment it is recovered or discarded, so the next one takes its place. Capping the *scan* buried the remainder — never listed, never offered, never swept — and `status_line`, the sentence the crash dialog shows, counted the truncated list and told the user there were eight.

**And a crash says so on the way out.** `instance.alert_fatal` (UX-06) is `MB_YESNO` — "Open the log folder?" rather than a wall of text with an OK button, because the second is a dialog people dismiss and the first is a question with an action behind it.
